# 騙經 (Pian Jing) Translation QA — Issues & Feedback Report

- **Evaluator Model**: `Qwen/Qwen3.5-35B-A3B`
- **Total Scored**: 228 segments
- **Average Overall Score**: 8.57 / 10
- **Average Accuracy**: 9.13 / 10
- **Average Fluency**: 8.94 / 10
- **Flagged Segments (Score <= 6)**: 16
- **Total Segments with Notes/Issues**: 190

## Top Recurring Issues

- **(1x)** the translation 'an affair with a maid' implies a romantic relationship which is not explicitly stated in the chinese title. the term '奸人' here likely refers to a 'wicked person' or 'deceitful individual' (the maid) rather than an 'affair'.
- **(1x)** the structure 'then the silver is stolen' is slightly passive and less punchy than the original's direct causality.
- **(1x)** the phrase 'doing business in your own prefecture' for '共府作事' is slightly ambiguous; it likely refers to local customs or familiar dealings within one's home region rather than a literal comparison of business types.
- **(1x)** the translation 'handed over behind her back' for '背交' is a bit clunky and potentially misleading (implying secrecy/deception) rather than the intended meaning of 'without her immediate presence/presence being required'.
- **(1x)** 'slipped away' for '脱身去矣' is good, but could be more idiomatic like 'made his escape' or 'slipped off' to better capture the swindler's intent.
- **(1x)** the phrase 'cheating a woman into bed' is slightly too modern and explicit for the classical register; 'tricks to seduce her' or 'schemes to get her into his bed' would better capture the nuance of '骗姦' (swindle/defile) in a ming vernacular context.
- **(1x)** the translation 'boys' for '人' is a slight over-specification; the original text likely refers to victims in general (men/people), though boys are implied by the context of 'marrow'.
- **(1x)** the phrase 'eats their marrow' captures the literal meaning but misses the idiomatic weight of '精髓' which can imply 'essence' or 'vitality' in a more metaphorical sense common in ming horror tales, though 'marrow' is acceptable for visceral impact.
- **(1x)** the phrase 'take his trousers right off' is slightly too literal and modern for the ming context; 'strip off his trousers' or 'remove his trousers' better captures the urgency and action of 脱之 without sounding like a modern instruction manual.
- **(1x)** the translation of 光棍 as 'smooth operator' is acceptable but slightly softens the connotation of 'crook' or 'swindler' which is more consistent with the text's tone. 'sharp' or 'rogue' might be more fitting.
- **(1x)** the phrase 'we will pay extra wages' slightly misrepresents the original '加还其工钱', which implies paying back the swindler's wages (or compensating for the loss) rather than just adding a bonus to new hires. the context suggests chao is offering to cover the cost of the swindled money or pay the new porters well to ensure they don't cheat him too.
- **(1x)** the translation 'murderous hands' for '毒手' is acceptable but slightly modernized; 'deadly trap' or 'harmful scheme' might fit the swindling context better, though 'murderous' captures the severity.
- **(1x)** the phrase 'unable to help it' for 不得已 is too weak; it implies lack of agency rather than being forced by circumstances or the swindler's manipulation.
- **(1x)** 'bitterly urged' for 苦劝 is slightly off in tone; 'earnestly pressed' or 'insisted hard' fits the context of a con artist's persuasion better than 'bitterly'.
- **(1x)** 'only then could stop the hunger' is awkward phrasing; 'only barely staved off hunger' is more natural.
- **(1x)** the translation misses the nuance of the swindler's internal state shifting from feigned asceticism to genuine physical need, making the transition feel abrupt.
- **(1x)** the phrase 'begrudge a few dozen strokes' is slightly awkward; 'spare' or 'hesitate to inflict' would be more natural in this context.
- **(1x)** misinterpretation of '好种': in the context of ming swindler stories, '好种' refers to a 'fine breed/lineage' (often implying a valuable child or family line for adoption/scam purposes), not specifically 'bloodline' which carries modern genetic connotations. the phrase likely implies stealing a child or heir.
- **(1x)** ambiguity in '地理': while 'geomancer' is a reasonable interpretation of someone practicing feng shui/geomancy, the title structure suggests the geomancer is using his wife as part of the scam setup, but the translation 'leaves his wife' might be too literal if the original implies he abandons her to pursue the scam elsewhere or uses her as bait.
- **(1x)** tone mismatch: the translation sounds like a modern thriller title rather than capturing the wry, cautionary tone typical of zhang yingyu's work.

## Flagged Translation Issues & Recommendations

### 1. [4/10] 第五類 偽交騙 Category 5 · False-Friendship Swindles — 激友讼奸以败家 (cat-05-s03) `[body]`
- **Score**: **4/10** (Accuracy: **3/10**, Fluency: **8/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 田卖尽，自鸣绝不与往来。朝夕相借，惟干谒亲兄，方知亲者终是亲；彼酒肉朋友，真伪情也。
- **Current English**:
  > Once the land was all sold, Ziming cut off all dealings with him. Ying had to beg loans morning and night, and in the end only his own elder brother would take him in. Only then did he learn that blood is blood after all, and that his wine-and-meat friend had been false from the start.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - Major hallucination: The translation introduces a character named 'Ying' who does not exist in the source text.
  - Misinterpretation of subject: The source '自鸣' (Ziming) is the name of the person selling the land and cutting off contact. The translation incorrectly splits this into two characters/actions or invents a new subject for the second sentence.
  - Incorrect meaning of '方知': The source says 'only then did he know/realize', but the translation changes the structure to 'in the end only his own elder brother would take him in', which conflates the action of borrowing with the realization.
  - Omission of key phrase: '朝夕相借' means borrowing morning and night, but the translation attributes this to 'Ying' instead of Ziming.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Once all the land was sold, Ziming cut off all dealings with him. Borrowing from others morning and night, he found that only his elder brother would help. Only then did he realize that blood is indeed thicker than water; those wine-and-meat friends were false from the start.

The phrase likely implies stealing a child or heir.
  - Ambiguity in '地理': While 'Geomancer' is a reasonable interpretation of someone practicing feng shui/geomancy, the title structure suggests the geomancer is using his wife as part of the scam setup, but the translation 'Leaves His Wife' might be too literal if the original implies he abandons her to pursue the scam elsewhere or uses her as bait.

### 3. [6/10] 第十七類 姦情騙 Category 17 · Illicit-Affair Swindles — 地理寄妇脱好种 (cat-17-s02) `[body]`
- **Score**: **6/10** (Accuracy: **5/10**, Fluency: **8/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 有一富家子，往佃户家取租，见其妇美，累挑之。妇不敢从，密报于婆。婆曰：“他富家子，若与他有子，后日亦讨得吃。”富子后又挑之，妇即允，与入房中解衣。富子曰：“往时累说不从，今何故便肯？”妇曰：“已对婆婆说过了。”富子曰：“你婆要拿奸么？”妇曰：“非也！婆曰傍你富家种，若有儿，亦讨得吃。”富子一闻漏种话，猛然自省曰：“不可！不可！”连说四句“不可”，因转言曰：“我非真欲奸，只爱你生得好，故与耍耳。今送银三钱，与你买粉，我不污你也。”淫情已动，驰归家，夜与妻交。其夜受胎，后生一男，长中进士，官受知县。
- **Current English**:
  > A rich family's son went to a tenant's house to collect rent, saw that the wife was pretty, and propositioned her again and again. She dared not consent, and secretly told her mother-in-law. The mother-in-law said: "He's a rich man's son; if you have a child by him, later we'll have something to eat." The rich son propositioned her again; the woman agreed, and they went into the room to undress. The rich son said: "Before you always refused; why agree now?" The woman said: "I've already told Mother-in-law." He said: "Does she want to catch us in the act?" The woman said: "No. She said to lean on your rich-family seed; if there's a son, we'll have something to eat." At the words about leaking the seed, he suddenly came to himself: "Must not! Must not!" He said "Must not" four times, then changed his words: "I didn't really mean to; I only liked your looks, so I was playing. Here's three qian of silver for powder; I won't stain you." Lust already aroused, he hurried home and slept with his wife that night. She conceived that night; later a boy was born, grew up, passed the jinshi examination, and became a magistrate.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - Mistranslation of '漏种话' (leaking seed talk): The phrase refers to the mother-in-law's scheme of planting a rich man's child ('sowing seed'), not literally 'leaking'. The translation 'leaking the seed' creates confusion and loses the specific connotation of the swindle.
  - Inaccurate rendering of '猛然自省': Translated as 'came to himself', which is too mild. The context implies a sudden realization of the danger/trap, better rendered as 'suddenly realized his folly' or 'woke up to the trap'.
  - Literalism in 'Must not! Must not!' repeated four times: While capturing the repetition, it feels stiff in English. A more natural phrasing like 'No! No!' or 'Impossible! Impossible!' might flow better while retaining the urgency.
  - Omission of nuance in '淫情已动': Translated as 'Lust already aroused', but the context suggests he was still acting on impulse before changing his mind, so the timing of the arousal relative to the decision needs clarity.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > A rich young man went to a tenant's house to collect rent, saw the wife was beautiful, and propositioned her repeatedly. She dared not consent and secretly told her mother-in-law. The mother-in-law said, 'He's a rich man's son; if you bear him a child, later we'll have something to eat.' The rich man propositioned her again; she agreed, and they went into the room to undress. He asked, 'You always refused before; why agree now?' She replied, 'I've already told my mother-in-law.' He asked, 'Does she want to catch us in the act?' She said, 'No. She said to lean on your rich-family seed; if there's a son, we'll have something to eat.' Upon hearing this talk of sowing seed, he suddenly realized his folly, shouting, 'No! No!' four times. Then he changed his tone: 'I didn't really mean to harm you; I only admired your beauty, so I was just playing. Here are three qian of silver for powder; I won't defile you.' Still stirred by lust, he hurried home and slept with his wife that night. She conceived immediately; later, a son was born who grew up to pass the jinshi examination and became a county magistrate.

### 4. [6/10] 第四類 詐哄騙 Category 4 · Fraud-and-Coaxing Swindles — 诈学道书报好梦 (cat-04-s00) `[title]`
- **Score**: **6/10** (Accuracy: **5/10**, Fluency: **8/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 诈学道书报好梦
- **Current English**:
  > Forging Letters from the Education Commissioner to Report Lucky Dreams
- **Identified Issues**:
  - Mistranslation of '学道': In the Ming context, '学道' refers to a specific official title (often a provincial education commissioner or inspector), but 'Forging Letters from the Education Commissioner' incorrectly implies the letters are FROM the commissioner. The story involves forging letters TO or pretending to be the commissioner's office to deceive someone.
  - Inaccurate rendering of '诈': While 'forging' is part of it, '诈' here implies a broader deception/scam involving the dream report, not just the act of forging documents.
  - Misinterpretation of the scam mechanism: The title suggests the scammer uses forged letters to report good dreams, but the actual plot typically involves a swindler using a fake official letter to claim they had a prophetic dream or to trick someone into paying for a dream interpretation service.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Scamming with Forged Letters from the Education Commissioner to Report Lucky Dreams

### 5. [6/10] 第四類 詐哄騙 Category 4 · Fraud-and-Coaxing Swindles — 诈无常烧牒捕人 (cat-04-s01) `[body]`
- **Score**: **6/10** (Accuracy: **5/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 长源地方，人烟过千，亦一大市镇也。有一日者，推命人也。至其间推算甚精，断人死生寿夭，最是灵验，以故乡里之老幼男女，多以命与算。凡三年内有该病者、该死者，各问其姓名，暗登记之，以为后验。昼往于市卜命，夜则归宿于僧寺。
- **Current English**:
  > Changyuan was a place of over a thousand households, a sizable market town. There was a fortune-teller there, of the kind called a day-master. His reckonings were very fine, and his verdicts on who would die and who would live, who would last long and who would die young, were uncannily accurate; so the old and young, the men and women of the countryside, mostly gave him their fates to reckon. For anyone fated to sicken or die within three years, he asked the name and secretly noted it down, to check against later events. By day he went to the market to tell fortunes; at night he returned to sleep in a Buddhist temple.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - Misinterpretation of '一日者': The translation renders this as 'a day-master' (a specific term in Chinese astrology referring to the Heavenly Stem of the birth day). In this context, '一日者' simply means 'one day' or 'on a certain day', introducing an unnecessary and incorrect astrological concept. The sentence structure suggests 'There was a fortune-teller there; one day...' or 'There was a fortune-teller there, who on one occasion...'.
  - Ambiguous phrasing 'gave him their fates to reckon': While understandable, it slightly distorts the original meaning of '多以命与算' (mostly entrusted their fate/destiny to his calculation/prediction). A more precise rendering would be 'entrusted their destinies to his calculations'.
  - Slight redundancy in 'who would die and who would live, who would last long and who would die young': The original '死生寿夭' is concise. The translation expands it well for English flow, but the repetition of 'die' could be tightened.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Changyuan was a place with over a thousand households, a sizable market town. There was a fortune-teller there. His calculations were very precise; his verdicts on life and death, longevity and early demise, were uncannily accurate. Consequently, the old and young, men and women of the countryside, mostly entrusted their destinies to his calculations. For anyone fated to sicken or die within three years, he would ask their name and secretly record it, to verify later. By day he went to the market to tell fortunes; at night he returned to sleep in a Buddhist temple.

### 6. [6/10] 第五類 偽交騙 Category 5 · False-Friendship Swindles — 激友讼奸以败家 (cat-05-s03) `[note]`
- **Score**: **6/10** (Accuracy: **5/10**, Fluency: **8/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 按：应被自鸣笼络，家破，产业尽被鸣收之，反与之莫逆之交，何其愚也！苟有心智人之处世，内而兄弟叔侄，外而朋友亲戚，皆不能无。与兄结讼而求昵于友，是其所厚者薄，而薄者反厚也。何不观孙荣之间革孙华而亦昵于友，使非杨氏贤德，后始有悔悟。而能以是而自新之，彼虽有百般巧计，安能中自新之士哉！
- **Current English**:
  > Note: Ying was snared by Ziming; his house was broken and every bit of his property gathered into Ziming’s hands, yet he had taken the man for a bosom friend. What folly! A man of any sense, living in the world, cannot do without brothers, uncles, and nephews within the family, nor without friends and kin beyond it. To sue his own brother while courting the intimacy of a friend is to slight those he should hold dear and hold dear the one he should slight. Consider Sun Hua of old, who cast out his brother Sun Rong and clung to his friends instead: had his wife Lady Yang not been a woman of such virtue, he would have been slow indeed to repent. If a man can take warning and make himself new, then however many clever tricks they have, how could they ever strike a man who has made himself new?
- **Identified Issues**:
  - Critical mistranslation of names: The text says 'Sun Rong' (孙荣) cast out his brother 'Sun Hua' (孙华), but the translation reverses this to 'Sun Hua... cast out his brother Sun Rong'.
  - The phrase 'cast out' for 间革 is slightly off; it implies a dispute or estrangement rather than a formal expulsion, though contextually understandable.
  - The translation of '苟有心智人之处世' as 'A man of any sense, living in the world' is acceptable but could be more idiomatic like 'Any sensible person navigating life'.
  - The ending clause 'how could they ever strike a man who has made himself new?' is a bit clunky; 'strike' is a weak translation for 中 (to hit/succeed against).
  - The translation misses the nuance of '自新' being a moral transformation/renewal, rendered simply as 'make himself new' which feels modern.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Note: Ying was ensnared by Ziming; his household was ruined and all his property seized by Ziming, yet he treated him as an unbreakable friend. How foolish! Any sensible person navigating life cannot do without brothers, uncles, and nephews within the family, nor without friends and kin beyond it. To sue one's own brother while seeking intimacy with a friend is to slight those one should cherish and cherish those one should slight. Consider Sun Rong, who drove out his brother Sun Hua and clung to his friends instead: had his wife Lady Yang not been virtuous, he would have repented too late. But if a man can truly renew himself, then however many clever tricks they devise, how could they possibly succeed against such a renewed soul?

### 7. [6/10] 第六類 牙行騙 Category 6 · Broker Swindles — 贫牙脱蜡还旧债 (cat-06-s01) `[body]`
- **Score**: **6/10** (Accuracy: **5/10**, Fluency: **8/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 梅爷刚正之官，弗听，即拘来审。内有江店客人，乃惯讼者，先对理曰：“蜡乃丘牙明卖与我，公平交易，张霸安得重取？即未全交付，亦牙家刻落，与我辈何干？”丘牙曰：“蜡非卖他。是小人先欠诸店旧帐，张霸蜡到，他等诈言措借，数日后即还银。及得蜡到手，即坐以抵前帐。非小人敢兜客银也。”梅爷曰：“丘牙欠债，须问彼自取，安得坐客人货以还彼债？你众等可将价还张霸，免你等罪。”江店恃有分上，再三辩论，说是明白交易，并无对债之事。梅爷触怒，将江店责十板；江店又辩论不已，又被责二十板。后诸人惊惧，皆称愿赔求饶。以江店监禁，诸人讨保，断蜡银，限三日，不完再重责。三日果追完。霸领银讫，深感梅爷恩泽，顶戴香炉，到于堂下，叩拜而去。
- **Current English**:
  > Master Mei was an upright official and would not listen to it; he had the men brought in for trial. Among them was the Jiang shop's man, a habitual litigant, who argued first: "The wax was sold to me openly by broker Qiu, a fair transaction. How can Zhang Ba collect for it a second time? And even if it was not paid for in full, that is the broker's skimming — what has it to do with us?" Broker Qiu said: "The wax was not sold to them. I already owed the shops old debts, and when Zhang Ba's wax arrived, they falsely said they would borrow it and pay the silver in a few days. Once the wax was in their hands, they held on to it to offset the old debts. It is not that I dared to pocket the client's silver." Master Mei said: "If broker Qiu owes debts, they must collect from him. How can they hold a client's goods to repay his debts? All of you will pay the price to Zhang Ba, and I will spare you punishment." The Jiang shop man, relying on his backer, argued over and over that it had been a straightforward transaction with no offsetting of debts. Master Mei flared up and had him beaten ten strokes; the man still would not stop arguing and was beaten twenty more. Then the others took fright and all said they were willing to pay, begging for mercy. The Jiang man was jailed, and the rest found guarantors. The wax money was assessed with a three-day limit; if it was not paid in full, they would be beaten again. In three days it was indeed collected in full. Ba received the silver and, deeply grateful for Master Mei's kindness, came before the hall wearing an incense burner on his head, kowtowed, and took his leave.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - Mistranslation of '顶戴香炉': The translation 'wearing an incense burner on his head' is a literal and absurd interpretation. In Ming vernacular context, this idiom means to carry the incense burner (as a sign of deep gratitude) or metaphorically to show utmost reverence/honor, not literally wearing it as a hat.
  - Slight awkwardness in 'would not listen to it': The phrase '弗听' refers to refusing to listen to the swindlers' excuses/arguments, but 'it' is vague. 'He would not listen to their pleas' would be clearer.
  - Minor register issue: 'Broker Qiu' for '丘牙' is acceptable, but 'Qiu the broker' or 'Qiu the middleman' might flow better in narrative English.
  - The phrase 'skimming' for '刻落' is slightly modern; 'deducting' or 'taking a cut' fits the historical context better.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Master Mei was an upright official who would not listen to their pleas; he had them brought in for trial. Among them was the Jiang shop's man, a habitual litigant, who argued first: "The wax was sold openly by broker Qiu—a fair transaction. How can Zhang Ba demand payment again? Even if it wasn't fully paid, that is the broker's deduction—what does it have to do with us?" Broker Qiu replied: "The wax was not sold to them. I already owed old debts to the shops. When Zhang Ba's wax arrived, they falsely claimed they would borrow it and pay silver within a few days. Once they got the wax, they held onto it to offset the old debts. It is not that I dared to pocket the client's silver." Master Mei said: "If broker Qiu owes debts, you must collect from him directly. How can you hold a client's goods to repay his debts? All of you will pay the price to Zhang Ba, and I will spare you punishment." The Jiang shop man, relying on his connections, argued repeatedly that it was a straightforward transaction with no debt offsetting. Master Mei grew angry and ordered him beaten ten strokes; when the man still refused to stop arguing, he was beaten twenty more. Then the others, terrified, all declared willingness to pay and begged for mercy. The Jiang man was imprisoned, while the rest secured guarantors. The wax money was assessed with a three-day deadline; if unpaid, they would be beaten again. Indeed, within three days, the full amount was collected. Ba received the silver and, deeply grateful for Master Mei's kindness, came before the hall carrying an incense burner in honor, kowtowed, and departed.

### 8. [6/10] 第七類 引賭騙 Category 7 · Lured into Gambling — 装公子套妓脱赌 (cat-07-s01) `[body]`
- **Score**: **6/10** (Accuracy: **5/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 须臾，公子到，细卿从容奉茶。叙寒温讫，公子径起，欲入内游玩。细卿慌忙请止，曰：“适有一外亲远来，在内留一水饭，恐无处可避也。”公子笑曰：“孤老便是孤老，何须托外亲也？既是你情人，我生平不吃醋，便请相见何妨？”即遣二仆入请。尚未出，又促细卿曰：“汝去请之。”细卿入内邀出。公子张看荻溪一表非俗，呵呵笑曰：“细卿妙人，果会择好才子。”即降前叙礼，院内备筵已到，公子坐上，荻溪前，细卿左陪。席间谈笑，并不及赌中去。到晚，索骰子行令，公子耍曰：“只恐卑人未晓好色。”细卿曰：“公子有一掷百万之豪，荻卿亦有呼庐赐绯之兴，愧小婢未足当好色耳。”公子曰：“荻溪亦作家乎？明早略赌一东道何如？”荻溪曰：“东道当小弟奉，何劳赌也？”公子曰：“空食未佳，须赢为奇。”先取掷之，无色。荻溪一掷即胜。公子须再加一台戏，又输；热性一起，曰：“荻溪有此妙手乎！与汝再决输赢。”荻溪曰：“不敢扳高耳，亦愿陪。”两下赌起，互有胜负。
- **Current English**:
  > Presently the young lord arrived, and Xiqing served tea with an easy grace. When the usual civilities were done, the young lord got straight up, meaning to stroll into the inner rooms. Xiqing hurriedly begged him to stop. “A kinsman from elsewhere has just come a long way,” she said, “and is inside having a simple meal. I am afraid there is nowhere for him to withdraw.” The young lord laughed. “A lover is a lover—why dress him up as an outside kinsman? If he is your man, I have never been the jealous sort. What harm is there in asking him out to meet me?” He sent two servants in to invite him. When they did not come out at once, he pressed Xiqing: “You go and ask him.” Xiqing went in and brought Dixi out. The young lord looked him over—a fine figure, no common man—and laughed, “Xiqing is a woman of taste; she really does know how to pick a fine gentleman.” He came forward and they exchanged courtesies. The banquet ordered for the courtyard had arrived; the young lord took the seat of honor, Dixi sat facing him, and Xiqing sat at the left in attendance. All through the meal they talked and laughed, never once touching on gambling. At nightfall they called for dice to play drinking games. The young lord joked, “I only fear I don't yet know a good ‘color’ when I see one.” Xiqing said, “The young lord has the dash to stake a million on a single cast, and Dixi has the zest to shout for the winning throw and carry off the scarlet. This poor girl is only ashamed she is no great ‘color’ to be fond of.” The young lord said, “So Dixi is a real player too? Tomorrow morning, what say we gamble for a small treat?” Dixi said, “The treat is mine to offer—no need to gamble for it.” The young lord said, “A meal that costs nothing has no savor; it must be won to be worth anything.” He took the dice and threw first—nothing. Dixi won with a single cast. The young lord raised the stake by another play and lost again. His blood was up. “Dixi, what a hand you have! Once more, you and I, to settle win and loss.” Dixi said, “I would not presume to reach so high, but I am game to keep you company.” The two fell to gambling, and the wins went back and forth.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - Mistranslation of '好色' (hao se): The translator interprets this as 'color' in a literal or visual sense ('know a good color', 'no great color to be fond of'), whereas in the Ming context it refers to 'lust' or 'being a connoisseur of beauty/lovers'. The joke is about whether the Lord is a lecher, not about colors.
  - Mistranslation of '呼庐赐绯': This idiom refers to winning a game and being rewarded with a red robe (a high honor). The translation 'carry off the scarlet' is too literal and misses the gambling context where 'red' often signifies a winning throw or specific dice outcome, though the 'scarlet' reference is partially there. However, combined with the previous error, the meaning is distorted.
  - Inconsistency in character name: The source uses '荻溪' (Di Xi) but the translation switches to 'Dixi' without explanation, which is acceptable phonetically but inconsistent within the text.
  - Tone issue: The translation's handling of the gambling banter loses the wry, competitive edge of the original Ming vernacular due to the 'color' misunderstanding.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Presently the young lord arrived, and Xiqing served tea with an easy grace. When the usual civilities were done, the young lord got straight up, meaning to stroll into the inner rooms. Xiqing hurriedly begged him to stop. “A kinsman from elsewhere has just come a long way,” she said, “and is inside having a simple meal. I am afraid there is nowhere for him to withdraw.” The young lord laughed. “A lover is a lover—why dress him up as an outside kinsman? If he is your man, I have never been the jealous sort. What harm is there in asking him out to meet me?” He sent two servants in to invite him. When they did not come out at once, he pressed Xiqing: “You go and ask him.” Xiqing went in and brought Di Xi out. The young lord looked him over—a fine figure, no common man—and laughed, “Xiqing is a woman of taste; she really does know how to pick a fine gentleman.” He came forward and they exchanged courtesies. The banquet ordered for the courtyard had arrived; the young lord took the seat of honor, Di Xi sat facing him, and Xiqing sat at the left in attendance. All through the meal they talked and laughed, never once touching on gambling. At nightfall they called for dice to play drinking games. The young lord joked, “I only fear I don't yet know a true connoisseur of beauty when I see one.” Xiqing said, “The young lord has the dash to stake a million on a single cast, and Di Xi has the zest to shout for the winning throw and claim the prize. This poor girl is only ashamed she is no great beauty to be fond of.” The young lord said, “So Di Xi is a real player too? Tomorrow morning, what say we gamble for a small treat?” Di Xi said, “The treat is mine to offer—no need to gamble for it.” The young lord said, “A meal that costs nothing has no savor; it must be won to be worth anything.” He took the dice and threw first—nothing. Once more, you and I, to settle win and loss.” Di Xi said, “I would not presume to reach so high, but I am game to keep you company.” The two fell to gambling, and the wins went back and forth.

### 9. [6/10] 第九類 謀財騙 Category 9 · Scheming for Wealth — 傲气致讼伤财命 (cat-09-s01) `[body]`
- **Score**: **6/10** (Accuracy: **5/10**, Fluency: **8/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 时有徽州汪逢七，乃巨族显宦世家也。不忿材以财势压人，曰：“世长势短，辄以千金为言。昔石崇之富，岂出公之下哉？而后竟何如也？”材怒其敌己，曰：“船中有长于下流者，有本大于下流者，竟无一言，你敢挺出与我作对？以丝一百担，价值数千金，统与你和。”逢七骂曰：“这下流好不知趣！屡屡无状，真不知死小辈也。我有数千金与你和，叫你无命归故士！”二人争口不休。众皆暗喜汪魏角胜，中心大快。有爱汪者，相劝各自入仓。
- **Current English**:
  > At the time there was Wang Fengqi of Huizhou, from a great clan of prominent officials. He would not stand Bangcai using money and power to crush people, and said: “Power in this world does not last, yet you always talk in thousands of taels. In former days, was Shi Chong’s wealth any less than yours? And what became of him later?” Bangcai was angry that someone would match him, and said: “On this boat there are men senior to you lowlifes, and men with more capital than you lowlifes, and they have not said a word. You dare step out and stand against me? I will stake the whole hundred piculs of silk, worth several thousand taels, against you.” Fengqi cursed him: “You low creature, you truly do not know your place! Again and again you behave outrageously—you really are a little wretch who does not know he can die. I have several thousand taels to match you, and I will see you have no life left to return to your native soil!” The two wrangled without stopping. Everyone secretly delighted that Wang and Wei were locking horns, and were greatly pleased at heart. Someone who sided with Wang urged them each to go into their own cabins.
- **Identified Issues**:

  - Incorrect translation of '下流': Translating '下流' as 'lowlifes' twice misses the specific nuance here. In this context, it refers to people of lower social standing or those who are 'base' in behavior compared to the speaker's self-perception, but more importantly, the phrase '长于下流者' and '本大于下流者' implies 'those senior to you in rank/position' and 'those with more capital than you'. The current translation makes it sound like a generic insult rather than a comparison of status/capital.
  - Mistranslation of '故士': Translated as 'native soil'. While close, '故土' (hometown/native land) is the standard term. '故士' might be a typo in the provided source text for '故土', or it could mean 'return to your former state/status'. However, given the threat of death, 'native soil' is an acceptable interpretation if assuming a typo, but the phrasing 'no life left to return' is slightly awkward.
  - Omission of '魏': The text mentions '汪魏角胜' (Wang and Wei locked horns). The translation says 'Wang and Wei were locking horns', which is correct, but earlier the antagonist is referred to as 'Bangcai'. If the antagonist's name is actually 'Wei Cai' (implied by the later mention of 'Wei'), the initial introduction of 'Bangcai' is a significant error. If the antagonist is just 'Cai', then 'Bang' is a hallucination. The text says '材怒其敌己', implying the person named 'Cai' is angry. Later it says '汪魏角胜', suggesting the other party is 'Wei'. This suggests the antagonist's full name might be 'Wei Cai' or the text has a specific naming convention. The translation invents 'Bangcai' which is unsupported.
  - Stiff phrasing: 'Power in this world does not last' is a bit weak for '世长势短'. A better rendering would capture the idea that worldly power is short-lived or fleeting.
  - Translation of '统与你和': 'I will stake the whole... against you' is good, but '统' implies 'all together' or 'the entirety'. The translation captures this well.
- **Recommended Revision**:

He could not bear Cai using wealth and power to crush others, saying: 'Worldly power is short-lived, yet you always speak of thousands of taels. Was Shi Chong’s wealth any less than yours? ### 11. [6/10] 第十二類 在船騙 Category 12 · On-the-Boat Swindles — 娶妾在船夜被拐 (cat-12-s01) `[body]`

- **Chinese Source**:
  > 后半年内，无甚生意。适有贾知县新受官赴任，经过扬州，欲娶一妾。危与计私议，欲以女脱嫁之。计许诺，自为媒，往与贾爷议。来看称意，即行聘礼，受银八十两，择日成婚。危与计同对女曰：“今半年无生意，家用穷迫，故以你假嫁与贾知县。其实你夫少年人，何忍舍你？我为父母，止生你一人，何忍舍你去？只不得已，把你为货也。况贾爷年老，他眼下未带长妻来，自然爱惜你。但恐到任后，接长妻到，必然酷虐你，骂詈鞭挞，自是不免。自古道：‘宁作贫人妻，莫作贵人妾’。今暂送你去，不日即登船矣。你夫暗以船随行，其船夜挂一白裤为号。你夜间若可逃，即逃过白裤船来，夫即在接你矣。切莫贪睡，误你夫终身；且你自受苦楚。”计故挽妻衣，涕泣面恳曰：“你肯许归，任你去；若不能逃，吾宁与你同死，决不忍相舍。”危氏亦泣曰：“父母有命，怎的不归？只你要随船候接，不可耽误。”三人商议已定。
- **Current English**:
  > For half a year after that there was little business. It happened that Magistrate Jia, newly appointed and on his way to his post, passed through Yangzhou and wanted to take a concubine. Wei and Ji talked it over in private, and decided to palm the daughter off on him in a sham marriage. Ji agreed, acted as matchmaker himself, and went to discuss it with Magistrate Jia. Jia came to look, was pleased, sent betrothal gifts at once, paid eighty taels, and chose a day for the wedding. Wei and Ji together said to the daughter: “We have had no business for half a year; the household is hard pressed, so we are marrying you to Magistrate Jia in name only. In truth your husband is a young man—how could he bear to give you up? We are your parents, and we have only you—how could we bear to let you go? We simply have no choice; we are using you as goods. Besides, Magistrate Jia is old; he has not brought his principal wife with him for now, so of course he will cherish you. But I fear that after he takes up his post and sends for the principal wife, she will surely treat you cruelly; cursing and whipping will be hard to avoid. The old saying goes: ‘Better a poor man’s wife than a rich man’s concubine.’ For now we will send you; in a few days he will board a boat. Your husband will secretly follow in a boat, with a white pair of trousers hung up as a signal. If you can get away at night, cross over to the white-trousers boat; your husband will be there to take you. Do not oversleep and ruin your husband’s whole life; you yourself would also suffer.” Ji deliberately clutched his wife’s clothes, weeping, and pleaded to her face: “If you will promise to come back, I will let you go; if you cannot get away, I would rather die with you—I cannot bear to part.” Miss Wei also wept and said: “When father and mother have given the order, how would I not come back? Only you must follow in a boat and wait to take me; do not miss the time.” The three of them had the plan set.

- **Recommended Revision**:

In truth your husband is a young man—how could he bear to give you up? We are your parents, and we have only you—how could we bear to let you go? We simply have no choice; we are treating you as merchandise. Besides, Magistrate Jia is old; he has not brought his principal wife with him for now, so of course he will cherish you. But I fear that after he takes up his post and sends for the principal wife, she will surely treat you cruelly; cursing and whipping will be hard to avoid. The old saying goes: 'Better a poor man's wife than a rich man's concubine.' For now we will send you; in a few days he will board a boat. Your husband will secretly follow in a boat, with a white pair of trousers hung up as a signal. If you can get away at night, cross over to the white-trousers boat; your husband will be there to take you. Do not oversleep and ruin your husband's whole life; you yourself would also suffer." The shopkeeper replied, "Three months ago, three travelers came and sold it." Zida asked, "Where were they from?" "Jiangxi," he said. Zida exclaimed, "That won't do—the trick has gone wrong!" He and Kui immediately went to Shuikou and asked Chen Si, "Which county was the helmsman who loaded my cousin's goods from?" Chen Si answered, "Yanshan County." Zida explained the situation, and together with Chen Si, they went to Yanshan to arrest them.

- **Current English**:
  > Halfway there Zisui said in a hurry, “I left a key in your sister’s room. I set out in a rush and did not bring it. You must go back and fetch it.” The man turned to go. Zisui said, “Wait! You will need a note in my own hand, or your sister will not hand the key over to you. Otherwise you will not get it.” Zisui got off the donkey, went into a paper shop, and wrote a verse: “Hangzhou’s Hua Buru took in Jintai’s Lang Zisui; she fell for the man and lost a donkey. From this parting on, where will she get bright pearls?” He sealed it and sent the man back. Buru opened it, saw she had been cheated, and hurriedly opened the chests—all brick and stone. Later Buru inquired carefully and learned it was Chen Quan of Nanjing, but there was nothing she could do.

In the context of Ming vernacular, '子' is a male protagonist, and '不如' is likely a male merchant or traveler; the poem's '明珠' (bright pearl) metaphorically refers to the stolen goods or the swindler's success, not a female character.

You'll need a note in my own hand, or your sister won't give you the key. Otherwise, you won't get it.' Buru opened it, realized he had been cheated, and hurriedly opened the chest—only bricks and stones inside. - **Identified Issues**:
  - Major mistranslation of '妓家' (brothels/prostitutes) as 'Brothels are used to cheating people'. The text states that prostitutes/brothels are the ones who cheat others, not that they are 'used to' it. The subject is the swindler, not the institution being utilized.
  - Mistranslation of '花不如高抬身价'. 'Hua Buru' appears to be a hallucinated name or misreading of '花' (flower/whore) and '不如' (not as good as). The context implies 'Even if she raised her price high...', but the translation creates a specific proper noun 'Hua Buru' which does not exist in the source.
  - Misinterpretation of '笼络院'. The translation renders this as 'pleasure houses that ensnare others', whereas '笼络' here likely refers to the act of winning over or managing the establishment's clients/customers, or simply the brothel itself. The phrase '供...之一笑' means 'to provide a laugh for...', so the ending should reflect that this story serves as an amusing anecdote about the situation, rather than a complex clause about houses ensnaring others.
  - The phrase '赔人赔驴' is translated as 'She lost a man and a donkey'. While literal, in the context of swindling stories, 'man' might refer to a lover/patron, but the phrasing 'lost a man' is slightly ambiguous compared to 'cheated out of a man'. However, the primary error is the handling of the subject in the first sentence.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Note: Prostitutes often cheat others; minor cases ruin households, major ones cost lives. Yet one never hears of them being cheated themselves. Moreover, even if a woman like Hua raised her price high and carefully selected her patrons, the wealth she extracted from men would be incalculable. Who would have thought Chen Quan possessed such uncanny methods—appearing and disappearing at will? She ended up losing both a man and a donkey, with regret coming too late. This was indeed Chen Quan’s recklessness, yet it also provides a wry laugh for those who manage the pleasure quarters.

### 18. [7/10] 第一類 脫剝騙 Category 1 · Making Off with Goods — 遇里長反脫茶壺 (cat-01-s03) `[note]`
- **Score**: **7/10** (Accuracy: **6/10**, Fluency: **8/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 按：林錢一始說家頗充裕，妻被棍姦，欲投里長作主，致人不疑；繼也入店，借壺、秤沽酒肉，以叙間濶之情，使人不備：玩通於股掌之中，術亦巧矣。然錢一狡猾有素，通亦知之，乃一卒遇之，通遂信其言而入店飲酒；更欲沽紅買肉，皆非欵待之真情。在通當燭其偽而止之曰：「店中不便，有酒有肉，到家食之未晚也。」則錢一姦無所施。将道旁脫走不暇，何至賠壺、秤而受嘔氣也？故錢一狡也，而通亦欠檢點焉。嘻！
- **Current English**:
  > Note: Lin Qianyi began by saying his household was fairly comfortable, that his wife had been violated by some rogue, and that he wanted the village head to stand up for him—so that no one would suspect. Then he went into the shop and borrowed pot and scale to buy wine and meat, as if to renew an old friendship—so that no one was on guard. He played Tong like a toy in the palm of his hand; the trick was clever enough. Yet Qianyi's cunning was long-standing, and Tong knew it. Still, meeting him suddenly, Tong believed his words and went into the shop to drink; and the wish to buy red wine and meat was no true hospitality. Tong should have seen through the sham and stopped him: "The shop is inconvenient; if there's wine and meat, it won't be too late to eat it at your house." Then Qianyi's scheme would have had nowhere to go—he would have been too busy making his escape by the roadside. How would it ever have come to paying for a pot and scale and swallowing insults? So Qianyi was crafty—but Tong also failed to watch himself. Ah!
- **Identified Issues**:
  - Misinterpretation of '紅': The translator renders '紅' as 'red wine', but in the Ming context of buying meat and wine for a meal, it refers to red-colored cured meat (like ham or pork) or simply high-quality meat, not wine. 'Red wine' is anachronistic and incorrect.
  - Ambiguity in '通亦知之': The translation 'Tong knew it' implies Tong knew Qianyi was cunning *before* the encounter. The text says 'Qianyi's cunning was long-standing, and Tong knew it,' which could mean Tong knew his reputation generally, but the narrative flow suggests he was caught off guard ('一卒遇之'). The phrasing is slightly ambiguous but acceptable.
  - Slight awkwardness in 'making his escape by the roadside': While capturing '道旁脫走', the phrase feels a bit modern. A more idiomatic Ming-style rendering might be 'fleeing down the road'.
  - The phrase 'playing Tong like a toy in the palm of his hand' is a good idiom, but 'toy' might be slightly too diminutive; 'puppet' or just 'in the palm of his hand' might fit the 'swindler' tone better, though this is minor.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Note: Lin Qianyi initially claimed his household was well-off, that his wife had been violated by a rogue, and that he sought the village head’s intervention to avoid suspicion. Then he entered the shop, borrowing a pot and scale to buy wine and meat, feigning a desire to renew old ties, thereby lowering everyone’s guard. He played Tong entirely in the palm of his hand; the trick was indeed clever. Yet Qianyi was known for his cunning, and Tong was aware of this. Still, upon their sudden meeting, Tong believed his words and went into the shop to drink. His request to buy red meat and wine was no genuine act of hospitality. Tong should have seen through the deception and stopped him: 'It is inconvenient here; if there is wine and meat, we can eat them at home—it won’t be too late.' In that case, Qianyi’s scheme would have failed, forcing him to flee down the road in haste. Why then did he end up paying for the pot and scale while enduring humiliation? Thus, while Qianyi was crafty, Tong also lacked caution. Alas!

### 19. [7/10] 第三類 換銀騙 Category 3 · Silver-Exchange Swindles — 道士船中換轉金 (cat-03-s01) `[body]`
- **Score**: **7/10** (Accuracy: **6/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 至期，兩監生到船坐定。老棍裝為一道士，衣冠淨潔，亦來搭船，舵工收之在船中。共談處，道士言詞雍容，或談及京中官民事體，一一練熟。兩監生及同船諸人，亦樂與談。兩日後，將近晚間，道士故提及辨珠玉寶貝之法，諸人閒談一番。又說到辨金上去，道他更辨得真。賁監生因自誇彼在京換一錠足色金，換數又便宜。諸人中有求看估色數者，賁生誇耀，取出與諸人遞觀，皆誇羨好金。遍觀已訖，時天色漸晚，复付還賁生。將收入箱際，道士亦曰：「願藉觀。」接過一看，曰：「果好真金！」隨手即付還訖，又道及別新話上去。賁監生收入金。晚飯已熟，各散而餐。飲日，道士以船錢還舵工，與諸人別而登岸去。
- **Current English**:
  > On the appointed day the two Academy students boarded the boat and settled in. The old con man had disguised himself as a Daoist priest, robe and cap neat and clean, and came to take the same boat; the helmsman let him aboard. In conversation the Daoist spoke with an easy grace, and when talk turned to the doings of officials and commoners in the capital, he knew it all inside out. The two Academy students and the other passengers enjoyed talking with him. Two days later, toward evening, the Daoist deliberately brought up methods for judging pearls, jade, and treasures, and everyone chatted idly for a while. Then the talk came around to judging gold, and he said he was an even better judge of that. Ben then boasted that in the capital he had exchanged for an ingot of pure gold, and at a bargain rate besides. Some of the passengers asked to see it and estimate its fineness. Eager to show off, Ben took it out and passed it around, and everyone admired the fine gold. When all had looked their fill, the sky was growing dark and it was handed back to Ben. Just as he was about to put it into his trunk, the Daoist said, "May I have a look too?" He took it, glanced at it, and said, "It really is fine, true gold!" Then he handed it straight back and moved on to some fresh topic. Ben put the gold away. Dinner was ready, and they all scattered to eat. The next day the Daoist paid his fare to the helmsman, took leave of the company, and went ashore.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase '飲日' in the source text is a typo for '次日' (the next day). The translation correctly interprets this as 'The next day', but the source error creates ambiguity.
  - The name '賁監生' is translated as 'Ben'. While phonetically close to 'Bì' (the likely reading of 賁), 'Ben' is an Anglicization that might confuse readers expecting a Chinese transliteration like 'Bi' or 'Fen'. However, given the context of a swindle story, using a Westernized name is sometimes done for flow, though it slightly deviates from strict transliteration norms.
  - The phrase '換數又便宜' is translated as 'at a bargain rate besides'. This captures the meaning well, but 'exchange rate' or 'conversion' might be more precise than 'rate' depending on the specific financial mechanism implied (swapping gold for something else vs. buying gold).
  - The translation uses 'Academy students' for '監生'. This is accurate for the Ming dynasty context (students at the Imperial Academy), but could be clarified as 'Imperial Academy students' for modern English readers unfamiliar with the term.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > On the appointed day, the two Imperial Academy students boarded the boat and settled in. The old con man had disguised himself as a Daoist priest, his robe and cap neat and clean, and came to take passage; the helmsman allowed him aboard. In conversation, the Daoist spoke with easy grace, and when talk turned to the affairs of officials and commoners in the capital, he knew it all inside out. The two Academy students and the other passengers enjoyed talking with him. Two days later, toward evening, the Daoist deliberately brought up methods for judging pearls, jade, and treasures, and everyone chatted idly for a while. Then the talk came around to judging gold, and he said he was an even better judge of that. Bi, the Academy student, boasted that in the capital he had exchanged for an ingot of pure gold, and at a bargain price besides. Some of the passengers asked to see it and estimate its fineness. Eager to show off, Bi took it out and passed it around, and everyone admired the fine gold. When all had looked their fill, the sky was growing dark and it was handed back to Bi. Just as he was about to put it into his trunk, the Daoist said, "May I have a look too?" He took it, glanced at it, and said, "It really is fine, true gold!" Then he handed it straight back and moved on to some fresh topic. Bi put the gold away. Dinner was ready, and they all scattered to eat. The next day the Daoist paid his fare to the helmsman, took leave of the company, and went ashore.

### 20. [7/10] 第五類 偽交騙 Category 5 · False-Friendship Swindles — 哄饮嫖害其身名 (cat-05-s00) `[note]`
- **Score**: **7/10** (Accuracy: **6/10**, Fluency: **8/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 按：石涓奸巧百端，匿怨友人，使孝淫溺酒色，名利俱丧。彼虽自谓得计，足以快其宿忿，殊不思杀人之父，人亦杀其父；杀人之兄，人亦杀其兄。天网恢恢，报应不爽，安能保他人不袭彼故智，而子孙不蹈其覆辙乎？垂戒二子所繇，殆与义方之训异矣，又何怪其子之复然耶！然孝亦自愚也。使孝稍有心智，宜忖父在之时，与彼有怨，今父已即世，得彼不念足矣，顾安望深交乃尔！此其中情，叵测可知。由是以怠惰荒淫为戒，勤励不息自强，则石涓虽诈，安能中自立之士哉！
- **Current English**:
  > Note: Shi Juan had a hundred crafty tricks. He hid an old grudge and made friends with Xiao, and drowned him in wine and lust until his name and his fortune were both lost. Juan thought he had won and could vent his long-held spite; it never crossed his mind that if you kill a man’s father, others will kill yours, and if you kill a man’s brother, others will kill yours. Heaven’s net is vast, and its reckoning never errs. How could he be sure no one else would borrow his old trick, or that his own sons and grandsons would not follow the same ruts? The warning he gave his two sons was a far cry from a righteous father’s teaching—so why be surprised that his sons went the same way? And yet Xiao was a fool too. Had he possessed an ounce of sense, he should have reckoned: while my father lived, this man was his enemy; now that my father is gone, the most I can ask is that he let the grudge go—how could I expect friendship that deep? That something unfathomable lay behind it should have been plain. If Xiao had taken sloth and debauchery as his warning and driven himself on without rest, then however crafty Shi Juan was, how could he have struck a man who stood on his own feet?
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'made friends with Xiao' is too positive and misses the connotation of 'deceiving' or 'pretending to be friendly' inherent in the context of a swindler's plot.
  - The translation 'borrow his old trick' for '袭彼故智' is slightly weak; 'copy his old tricks' or 'imitate his methods' fits the warning about future consequences better.
  - The phrase 'stood on his own feet' for '自立之士' is a modern idiom that feels slightly anachronistic compared to the classical tone; 'a man of independent spirit' or 'a self-reliant person' would be more precise.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Note: Shi Juan employed a hundred crafty tricks. He concealed his grudge against a friend, feigned friendship with Xiao, and drowned him in wine and lust until he lost both his reputation and fortune. Though Juan thought he had won and could vent his long-held spite, he never considered that if one kills a man’s father, others will kill his father; if one kills a man’s brother, others will kill his brother. Heaven’s net is vast, and its retribution never fails. How could he be sure no one else would copy his old tricks, or that his own sons and grandsons would not follow the same path? The warning he gave his two sons was far removed from the teachings of a righteous father—so why be surprised that his sons turned out the same way? Yet Xiao was also foolish. Had he possessed even a shred of sense, he should have reasoned: while my father lived, this man was his enemy; now that my father has passed away, it would suffice if he let the grudge go—how could I expect such deep friendship? That something unfathomable lay behind this should have been clear. If Xiao had taken sloth and debauchery as a warning and driven himself on without rest, then however crafty Shi Juan was, how could he have deceived a man of independent spirit?

### 23. [7/10] 第七類 引賭騙 Category 7 · Lured into Gambling — 好赌反落人术中 (cat-07-s02) `[body]`
- **Score**: **7/10** (Accuracy: **6/10**, Fluency: **8/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 一日，鄂已睡，华胜邀起而赌东道。鄂心喜曰：“此夜机会可矣。”故推不起。又强之，鄂曰：“我不赌东道，要赌银。”胜心暗忖：“我棋高他，何惧之有？”连声应曰：“更好。”不知已堕其术矣。且素性亢傲，乡亲咸憎之，大家赞成其赌。鄂曰：“先以银对定，输即收去，无得抵赖。要一两一局，每两与众抽头二钱，作东道并做戏。”胜曰：“虽二两无妨。”未几，输数局。心中热起，说要十两一局。客中老成者曰：“不可，且休矣！”其后生辈反以言激之。胜心益热，再对银十两而赌。不数局，共输数十餘两。时天色已曙，鄂将胜银卷入囊中而起。胜邀再赌，鄂坚执不许，二人相扭甚热。鄂曰：“是你邀我赌，非我邀你赌。我若输你，肯饶否？我家财数千，因赌而倾。你输此些小，何得鼻血？好不为男子也！任你经官不还。”客中老成者曰：“是我辈无见识，不阻你，以致覆败。但张兄说得是，倘他输你，必不放他。不如我辈抽头者尽还你罢。俗云‘豪杰对豪杰，齿打落，吞肚里’。”鄂曰：“众既以抽头者退出，我亦退银三两做戏，更出一两作东道。内抽五钱，换金一线，打一戒指与徐兄，作好赌之戒。”众曰：“张兄之言大有理，乃豪杰惜豪杰，真慷慨丈夫也！”华胜默然。真个是：“安分不贪难诱引，贪心萌内必遭殃。”
- **Current English**:
  > One evening E had already gone to bed when Huasheng roused him to gamble for a treat. E was delighted at heart: “Tonight the chance has come.” But he made excuses and would not get up. Huasheng pressed him. E said, “I will not gamble for a treat. If we gamble, it is for silver.” Sheng thought to himself, “My chess is better than his—what have I to fear?” and answered at once, “Even better.” He did not know he had already fallen into the snare. What is more, he was arrogant by nature, and his fellow townsmen all detested him, so the whole company egged the wager on. E said, “First we weigh out the silver face to face; the loser hands it over on the spot, no backing out. One tael a game, and from each tael two qian go to the company as a cut, to pay for a treat and a play.” Sheng said, “Make it two taels for all I care.” Before long he had lost several games. His blood heated, and he called for ten taels a game. The older, steadier guests said, “No—you should stop now.” The younger ones only provoked him with words. Sheng's blood grew hotter; he weighed out another ten taels and played on. Within a few more games he had lost several dozen taels in all. By then the sky was growing light. E swept the winnings into his bag and got up. Sheng begged him to play on; E flatly refused, and the two grappled hotly. E said, “You invited me to gamble, not I you. If I had lost to you, would you have let me off? My family had several thousand taels, and gambling emptied it. You have lost this trifle—why the nosebleed? Some man you are. Take me to the magistrate if you like; I will not pay it back.” The older guests said, “It was we who lacked the sense to stop you, and so you came to ruin. But Brother Zhang is right: if he had lost to you, you would never have let him off. Better that those of us who took the cut give it all back to you. As the saying goes: when hero meets hero, a knocked-out tooth is swallowed and done with.” E said, “Since the company is returning the cut, I too will give back three taels for the play and put out one tael more for a treat. From it, take five qian, exchange it for a thread of gold, and have a ring made for Brother Xu as a caution against gambling—for ‘ring’ and ‘caution’ are one and the same word.” The company all said, “Brother Zhang's words are most reasonable. This is a hero cherishing a hero, a truly generous man.” Huasheng was silent. Truly it is said: keep to your lot and covet nothing, and no one can lure you; let greed sprout within, and ruin is sure to follow.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - Mistranslation of '鄂' (E) as a generic name instead of the specific character 'Zhang E' (张鄂), which is crucial for tracking the swindler's identity in the narrative.
  - Inaccurate translation of '鼻血' (nosebleed). The phrase '何得鼻血' is an idiom meaning 'why are you so upset/angry?' or 'why the fuss?', not literally about bleeding. The translator missed the idiomatic sense of indignation.
  - The phrase 'make it two taels for all I care' slightly distorts '虽二两无妨', which implies 'Two taels is no problem / fine with me' rather than a dismissive 'for all I care'.
  - The explanation of the ring pun ('ring' and 'caution') is added by the translator and not present in the source text, which only says '作好赌之戒' (as a warning against gambling). While helpful context, it borders on hallucination if the reader expects strict fidelity.
  - Translation of '东道' as 'treat' is acceptable but could be more precise as 'hosting expenses' or 'banquet costs' to reflect the literal 'east host' meaning in the context of paying for the meal.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > One evening, Zhang E was already asleep when Huasheng roused him to gamble for a treat. E was delighted: "Tonight is the chance." So he feigned reluctance and refused to get up. When pressed again, E said, "I will not gamble for a treat; we must gamble for silver." Sheng thought to himself, "My chess skills are superior; what do I have to fear?" He answered at once, "Even better." Unaware that he had fallen into their trap, he proceeded. Moreover, being naturally arrogant and detested by his fellow townspeople, everyone egged the wager on. E said, "First, let us weigh out the silver face to face; the loser pays immediately, no backing out. One tael per game, with two qian from each tael going to the company as a cut to cover the treat and the show." Sheng said, "Two taels is no problem." Before long, he lost several games. His blood heated, and he demanded ten taels per game. The older, steadier guests said, "No—you should stop now." The younger ones provoked him further. Sheng's blood grew hotter; he weighed out another ten taels and played on. Within a few more games, he had lost dozens of taels in total. By then, dawn was breaking. E swept the winnings into his bag and stood up. Sheng begged him to play on; E flatly refused, and the two grappled fiercely. E said, "You invited me to gamble, not I you. If I had lost to you, would you have let me off? My family had several thousand taels, and gambling emptied it. You have lost this trifling sum—why the fuss? Are you really a man? Take me to the magistrate if you like; I will not pay it back." The older guests said, "It was we who lacked the sense to stop you, leading to your ruin. But Brother Zhang is right: if he had lost to you, you would never have let him go. Better that those of us who took the cut return it all to you. As the saying goes: when hero meets hero, a knocked-out tooth is swallowed and done with." E said, "Since the company is returning the cut, I too will give back three taels for the show and add one tael more for the treat. From it, take five qian, exchange it for a thread of gold, and have a ring made for Brother Xu as a warning against gambling—for 'ring' and 'warning' share the same character." The company all said, "Brother Zhang's words are most reasonable. This is a hero cherishing a hero, a truly generous man." Huasheng fell silent. Truly it is said: keep to your lot and covet nothing, and no one can lure you; let greed sprout within, and ruin is sure to follow.

But Brother Zhang has been invited out to drink, so I could not say goodbye.” Pei’s servant said: “The master is not back yet; I will give him your respects.” Xing then took leave of the host, Chen Si. Chen Si was a seasoned broker; he looked all around Xing’s room, but the hole Xing had dug was already covered with things. The hired porter pretended to head for the seaport, then quickly covered their tracks, turned toward Nantai, and boarded the river boat going up to Shuikou.

You Wu was wicked, no doubt; but why did young Qiu gossip about him behind his back and bring on You Wu's brazen seizure of his bedding? That is a far cry from Lord Pang's way of leaving peace behind him. - **Current English**:
  > When they hired a boat at the river, the helmsman saw a rich man’s bearing and servants in good order, yet the luggage was only two small chests. When they were lifted into the boat he felt how heavy the chests were, and thought they must be all silver. So he probed with words: “Where have you come from, sir? And you did not buy more goods?” Hao, because his capital was small, feared other merchants would look down on him, so he lied: “My elder brother holds a humble post in Huguang. I am returning from his post and did not buy much.” The helmsman said: “So you are a young gentleman.” He also saw the servants wait on him with respect, always calling their master “young sir,” and that he spent in a grand style, not of a kind with ordinary merchants; for this he believed still more that he was truly a young gentleman of an official house, and everyone on the boat deferred to him. When they reached shore, the other merchants all moved off the boat. The helmsman alone kept Young Gentleman Xiong, saying: “There were many guests on the boat; I could not show you proper respect. I have prepared a cup of wine; please let me invite you.” He went ashore, bought many fine dishes and good wine, pressed them to drink at night, most attentive; Xiong Hao drank with an easy heart. The helmsman also hard-pressed the servant to drink. Man Qi knew in his heart it was not kindly meant. At first he pretended to refuse; later he could not stop the urging, drank several cups, then pretended to be drunk and went to lie down. Young Xiong let the helmsman press him, and was truly drunk and knew nothing. When Qi saw he was sound asleep, he got up and said to the helmsman: “I am not truly drunk. We are near home now; my heart is heavy, and I cannot get the wine down. This young master is a man of wine and women. The elder master, in his post, sent him home with several hundred taels. On the road he spent it all on whoring; he is only taking a few brushes and a few mirrors home as gifts for the nephews. When we get home tomorrow, the old master will surely blame me for not stopping him. There are men like this in the world: they see wine as sugar, and love to boast—how can I check him? Let me open the two chests and show you: is there a speck of silver in them?” He took the key and opened both chests: only brushes and mirrors, no silver at all. Qi took two mirrors and gave them to the helmsman, saying: “All along the way you have looked after us; I give each of you a mirror to use.” The helmsman said: “A master’s things must not be given away.” Qi said: “Take half; he will not be able to make it out, and once we are home he will not know the count.” He locked the chests again, went with the helmsman to sleep, and kept watch all night. The next day they went ashore. Hao said: “It is rare for a helmsman to be so kind; give him another qian of silver.”

Once lifted aboard, he felt how heavy the chests were and assumed they must be filled entirely with silver. So he probed with words: 'Where have you come from, sir? And why did you not buy more goods?' Hao, fearing that other merchants would look down on him due to his meager capital, lied: 'My elder brother holds a humble post in Huguang. I am returning from his office and bought little.' The helmsman said, 'So you are a young gentleman of an official house.' He also observed the servants serving him with great respect, always addressing their master as 'Young Master,' and noted his lavish spending habits, which set him apart from ordinary merchants; thus, he became even more convinced that he was truly a noble official's son, and everyone on the boat deferred to him. When they reached shore, the other merchants disembarked. The helmsman alone kept Young Gentleman Xiong, saying, 'There were many guests on the boat; I could not show you proper respect. I have prepared a cup of wine; please let me invite you.' He went ashore, bought fine dishes and good wine, and pressed them to drink late into the night, showing great attentiveness. Xiong Hao drank freely. The helmsman then hard-pressed the servant to drink. Man Qi knew in his heart it was not kindly meant. At first he pretended to refuse; later, unable to resist the urging, he drank several cups, then feigned drunkenness and went to lie down. Young Xiong, trusting the helmsman's coaxing, fell truly drunk and knew nothing. When Qi saw he was sound asleep, he got up and said to the helmsman: 'I am not truly drunk. We are near home now; my heart is heavy, and I cannot get the wine down. This young master is a debauched wastrel. The elder master, in his post, sent him home with several hundred taels. On the road he spent it all on whoring; he is only taking a few brushes and a few mirrors home as customary gifts for his nephews. When we get home tomorrow, the old master will surely blame me for not stopping him. There are men like this in the world: they see wine as sugar and love to boast—how can I check him? Let me open the two chests and show you: is there a speck of silver in them?' He took the key and opened both chests: only brushes and mirrors, no silver at all. Qi took two mirrors and gave them to the helmsman, saying, 'All along the way you have looked after us; I give each of you a mirror to use.' The helmsman said, 'A master's things must not be given away.' Qi replied, 'Take half; he will not be able to count them, and once we are home he will not know the difference.' He locked the chests again, went with the helmsman to sleep, and kept watch all night. The next day they went ashore. ### 29. [7/10] 第十四類 假銀騙 Category 14 · Counterfeit-Silver Swindles — 冒州接着漂白皛 (cat-14-s01) `[body]`

  > 一棍以漂白银来买布。每五两一锭，内以真银，如假银一般，色同一样。棍将丝银先对。广以铁槌凿打，并无异样。打至十餘锭，通是一色。广说：“不须再凿槌打。”棍遂以漂白出对，共银六百餘两，内只有细丝银一百餘两，餘者皆假也。银交完讫，布搬去了。广收其银，检束行李，与乡里即雇骡车，直到临清，去买回头货物。取出其银，皆假银也。那时虽悔不及，然广不甚动情，只说云“是我方承得此会，他人出外贸易，从此止矣。”人慨斯人量大，有此大跌，后必有大发也。棍虽脱骗得金数百，然天理昭昭，子孙必不昌隆。盖假银天下处处有之，故录此以为后人之提防，勿蹈天广之覆辙也。按：棍之用假银，此为商者最难提防，必得其梗概，方能辨认。余于壬子秋，在书坊检得一小本仔，辨说银之真假，甚是明白。故录之，以为江湖诸君览之，则假银若一入眼，灼然明白。略陈其一二于左：
- **Current English**:
  > A con man came to buy cloth, paying with bleached silver. The ingots were five taels each, and genuine silver was mixed in with the fake, all identical in color. The con man first put out fine-thread silver for checking. Guang hammered and chiseled it and found nothing amiss. After he had tested more than ten ingots, all uniform, Guang said, “No need to hammer and chisel any more.” The con man then brought out the bleached silver to settle the payment: over six hundred taels in all, of which only a hundred-odd taels were genuine fine-thread silver—the rest was all fake. The silver was handed over, and the cloth was carried away. Guang put the silver away, packed his bags, and with his fellow townsmen hired a mule cart straight to Linqing to buy goods for the trip home. When he took out the silver, it was all fake. Regret was useless by then, but Guang did not take it too hard. He only said, “It happened to be my turn to catch it at this fair. May it end with me, and no other traveling merchant suffer the same.” People marveled at his magnanimity: after such a great fall, he was bound to rise high. The crook may have gotten away with several hundred taels, but Heaven’s principle is clear—his descendants will not prosper. Fake silver is found everywhere in the empire, so I record this as a precaution for those who come after: do not repeat Qian Tianguang’s mistake. Note: Of all the traps a merchant faces, fake silver is the hardest to guard against; you must grasp the general outline before you can spot it. ### 30. [7/10] 第二十一類 僧道騙 Category 21 · Monk and Daoist Swindles — 服孩儿丹诈辟谷 (cat-21-s01) `[body]`

  > 经二年半后，有潭阳富人礼迎之，虔奉更肃，若敬神明。时进茶果，稍为食些少。后备清茶精饭，苦劝之食，坚辞不能，不得已为食一瓯。少顷饥甚，服滚汤。又饥饿不能禁，又索食。富人欢喜，肃进之。连三日内，皆一日五食，仅能止饥。山民自惊疑，急求归山，依旧服汤静坐，不免肚饥。后只得复食，三餐如寻常人矣。
- **Current English**:
  > After two and a half years a rich man of Tanyang received him with ceremony, serving him still more solemnly, as if honoring a god. From time to time he sent in tea and fruit, and the man ate a little. Later he prepared clear tea and fine rice and bitterly urged him to eat. He firmly declined that he could not; then, unable to help it, he ate one bowl. In a short while he was very hungry, and took boiling water. He was hungry again and could not bear it, and asked for food once more. The rich man was glad and served him solemnly. For three days running he ate five times a day, and only then could stop the hunger. The mountain man was startled and doubtful himself, and urgently asked to return to the mountain. He took water and sat quietly as before, but could not avoid a hungry belly. Later he had no choice but to eat again, three meals like an ordinary person.
- **Identified Issues**:

  - 'Bitterly urged' for 苦劝 is slightly off in tone; 'earnestly pressed' or 'insisted hard' fits the context of a con artist's persuasion better than 'bitterly'.
  - 'Only then could stop the hunger' is awkward phrasing; 'only barely staved off hunger' is more natural.
  - The translation misses the nuance of the swindler's internal state shifting from feigned asceticism to genuine physical need, making the transition feel abrupt.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > After two and a half years, a wealthy man from Tanyang welcomed him with great ceremony, treating him even more solemnly, as if he were a deity. He would occasionally serve tea and fruit, and the man ate only a little. Later, the host prepared clear tea and fine rice, earnestly pressing him to eat. The man firmly refused, claiming he could not; yet, under pressure, he was compelled to eat one bowl. Shortly after, he grew extremely hungry and drank boiling water. Hungry again and unable to endure it, he asked for food once more. The rich man was delighted and served him respectfully. For three consecutive days, he ate five times a day, barely managing to quell his hunger. The mountain man began to suspect and fear himself, urgently requesting to return to the mountain. He resumed drinking water and sitting quietly as before, but could not escape the gnawing hunger. Eventually, he had no choice but to eat again, taking three meals like an ordinary person.

- **Current English**:

Even a simple account is still evidence.” The elder brother said, “What if I write it myself?” The matchmaker said, “Best.” Paper was brought. When they came to the betrothal silver, the elder brother said, “Sixty taels.” The matchmaker said, “Cut it; only forty. Only settle your brother’s wife in a good place; no need to demand much silver.” The elder brother said, “The two sums can be the same.” The matchmaker said, “Easy enough.” When it was written, the matchmaker said, “Leave the marriage letter in my hand while we weigh out the silver.” First they weighed out forty taels as one sealed packet, then weighed out twenty taels and gave them to the elder brother. The elder brother rose: “I will take the forty taels. The betrothal gift can be handed over at the house in her presence; otherwise there is nothing to discuss.” The matchmaker said, “Add another ten taels.” The elder brother would not. The matchmaker looked at the graduate: “What do you say?” The graduate said, “Make up forty taels in your hand, and at their house hand them to the woman.” The matchmaker said, “The marriage letter and the silver must both be in my hand; we go to the house together and both sides deliver.” The elder brother said, “Mine is not a matter of today’s talk; it was to be handed over behind her back, as we agreed yesterday. The matchmaker said, "We should have someone write the betrothal letter." The elder brother asked, "My late brother isn't here—what use is a marriage letter?" The matchmaker replied, "Transactions in the capital differ from those in your home prefecture; even a simple account serves as proof." The elder brother offered, "Shall I write it myself?" He gave the marriage letter to the graduate, saying, "The matter is settled; no need to take it along." Thus, when an inexperienced servant carries the travel bundle—the very thing that holds the silver and goods—he must stay by your side, and you must entrust it only to a reliable place; then nothing will be lost. If you first order him to carry it ahead and it is mistakenly left on another boat, how can there be no loss?   > 按：君子仁民愛物，而仁之先施者，莫如鄰；物之愛者，即鹅亦居其一。何對鄰人養鹅，惡在嘈雜之聲，必欲盗之者以殺之，愛物之謂何哉？利失對鄰之鹅，而贊成棍賊以盗之，仁心安在？是以致使棍聞其言，乗機而行竊，反贊成其偷，亦是鼠辈也。欲去人之鹅，而反自失其布，是自貽祸也，将誰怨哉？若能仁以處鄰，而量足以容物，何至有此失也！
- **Current English**:
  > Note: A gentleman is kind to people and cares for living things. Neighbors come first in kindness, and even geese count among the things to care for. What kind of care for living things is it, when a neighbor keeps geese, to hate their noise and wish a thief would make off with them and kill them? He counted the loss of his neighbor's geese a gain, and even helped the swindler-thief steal them—where was his humane heart? So when the swindler heard his words, seized the chance, and carried out his theft—and the man even abetted the stealing—he was a rat of the same nest. He wanted to be rid of another man's geese and lost his own cloth instead; he brought the misfortune on himself—whom can he blame? Had he treated his neighbor kindly and had the breadth of mind to tolerate things, he would never have suffered this loss.

### 39. [8/10] 第一類 脫剝騙 Category 1 · Making Off with Goods — 借他人屋脫客布 (cat-01-s06) `[body]`
- **Score**: **8/10** (Accuracy: **9/10**, Fluency: **8/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 聂道應，别号西湖，邵武六都人。家原富厚，住屋宏深。後因訟耗家，以裁缝為業。忽一日徃人家裁衣。有一光棍見客人賣布，知應出外，故领到應家前栋坐定，竟入内堂，私問應妻云：「汝丈夫在家否？」其妻曰：「徃前村裁衣。」棍曰：「我要造数件衣服，今日歸否？」對曰：「要明日歸。」棍曰：「我有同伴在你前栋坐，口渴，求茶一杯吃。」應妻即討茶二杯，放於斫凳上。棍将茶捧與布客飲。飲罷，接杯入，方出拣布四匹，還銀壹兩，隻銀不成色。客曰：「此價要換好銀。」棍曰：「我儿子為人裁衣，待明日歸換與你。」言未畢，棍预套一人來問：「針工在家否？」棍應曰：「要明日歸。」其人即去。布客曰：「你收起布，明日換之與我。」客既出，少頃棍亦拖布迯去。次早，布客到應家問曰：「針工歸否？」應妻曰：「午後囬。」布客次早又問：「針工歸否？」應妻又曰：「今午囬。」布客午後又來問，應妻曰：「未歸。」布客怒曰：「你公公前日拿布四匹，說要針工歸來還銀，何再三推託？你公公何去？」應妻道：「這客人好胡說！我家哪有公公？誰人拿你布？」二人角口大鬧。鄰人辯曰：「他何曾有公公？况其丈夫又不在家，你布不知何人拿去，安可妄取？」布客無奈，状投署印同知钟爷。状准，即拘四鄰來审。眾云：「應不在家，况父已死。其布不知甚人脫去。」钟爷曰：「布在他家脫去，那日何人到他家下？着鄰約為之穷究，必有着落矣。」鄰約不能究，乃勸西湖曰：「令正不合被棍脫茶，致误客人以布付棍，當認一半；布客不合輕易以布付人，亦當自認一半。」二家諾然，依此囬報。钟爷以鄰約處得明白，俱各免供。
- **Current English**:
  > Nie Daoying, also known by the name Xihu, was a man of Liudu in Shaowu. His family had once been wealthy, with a large, deep-set house. Later, lawsuits ate up the family fortune, and he made his living as a tailor. One day he went out to a customer's house to cut clothes. A smooth operator saw a cloth merchant selling cloth and, knowing Ying was out, led the man to Ying's house and seated him in the front hall. Then he went straight into the inner rooms and privately asked Ying's wife, "Is your husband home?" His wife said, "He's gone to the next village to cut clothes." The crook said, "I need several garments made. Will he be back today?" She answered, "Not until tomorrow." The crook said, "I have a companion sitting in your front hall who's thirsty. May I beg a cup of tea?" Ying's wife fetched two cups of tea and set them on the cutting bench. The crook carried the tea out to the cloth merchant. When the man had drunk, the crook took the cups back in, then came out, picked four bolts of cloth, and paid one tael of silver—but the silver was not pure. The merchant said, "At this price I need pure silver." The crook said, "My son is out cutting clothes for people; when he comes back tomorrow, I'll exchange it for you." Before he had finished speaking, a man the crook had planted in advance came and asked, "Is the tailor home?" The crook answered, "Not until tomorrow." The man left. The cloth merchant said, "Put the cloth away, then, and exchange it for me tomorrow." Once the merchant had gone, the crook a moment later hauled off the cloth and fled. The next morning the cloth merchant came to Ying's house and asked, "Is the tailor back?" Ying's wife said, "After noon." The following morning he asked again, "Is the tailor back?" Ying's wife said again, "Today at noon." In the afternoon the merchant came once more; Ying's wife said, "Not back yet." The merchant flared up: "The day before yesterday your father-in-law took four bolts of cloth, saying he'd pay the silver when the tailor came home. Why do you keep putting me off? Where has your father-in-law gone?" Ying's wife said, "What nonsense! What father-in-law do we have in this house? Who took your cloth?" The two fell into a shouting match. The neighbors reasoned with him: "When did this family ever have a father-in-law? Besides, her husband isn't home. We don't know who took your cloth—how can you demand it from them?" With no other recourse, the merchant filed a complaint with the vice prefect, Master Zhong. The complaint was accepted, and the neighbors on all four sides were summoned for questioning. They all said, "Ying was not home, and his father is long dead. We don't know who made off with the cloth." Master Zhong said, "The cloth was taken at his house. Who came to the house that day? Have the neighborhood elders investigate it thoroughly; there must be some trace." The neighborhood elders could find nothing, so they urged Xihu: "Your good wife should not have let the swindler wangle tea out of her, which misled the merchant into handing his cloth to him—you should bear half the loss. The merchant should not have handed his cloth to a stranger so lightly—he should bear half himself." Both households agreed and reported back accordingly. Master Zhong, finding the elders' settlement fair and clear, dismissed both parties without further testimony.

  - The phrase 'hauled off the cloth' is a bit informal compared to the narrative tone, though acceptable.
  - The translation of 鄰約 as 'neighborhood elders' is an interpretive leap; while likely correct in function, 'local neighborhood representatives' or keeping the term closer to the text might be more precise without over-interpreting.

### 40. [8/10] 第二類 丟包騙 Category 2 · The Dropped-Parcel Swindle — 丟包於路行脫換 (cat-02-s00) `[body]`
- **Score**: **8/10** (Accuracy: **9/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 中途偶見一包，賢撿入手，約有銀二三兩，不勝喜悅。從前一人曰：「見者有分，不許獨得。可藏在你箱中，待僻靜處拿出來分。你撿者得二分，我見者得一分。」賢意亦肯，況銀納置彼箱，心中坦然無疑。行未數十步，忽一人忙趕到來，啼哭哀告曰：「我失銀三兩作一包，是措借納官的。你客官若拾得者，願體天心還我，陰功萬代。」前見者故作憐憫之容，曰：「是此绱鞋財主拾得，要與我均分。既是你貧苦人的，我情願不分，你可出些收贖與他，叫他把還你。」賢被此人證出，只得開箱，叫失銀者將原銀包自己取去。但得其一錢收贖，亦自以為幸；不知自銀已被棍將偽包換去矣。至晚到烏石地方，取出收贖銀還酒，將剩者欲併入大包。打開只見銅鐵，其銀一毫也無，只得大哭而罷。
- **Current English**:
  > Partway home he happened to spot a parcel on the road. Xian picked it up: inside was some two or three taels of silver. He was overjoyed. A man up ahead turned and said, "Whoever sees a find shares in it — no keeping it all to yourself. Hide it in your chest, and when we reach a quiet spot we'll take it out and split it. You picked it up, so you get two shares; I saw it, so I get one." Xian was willing; besides, the silver would be sitting in his own chest, so he felt perfectly at ease and suspected nothing. They had gone barely a few dozen paces when a man came hurrying after them, weeping and pleading: "I lost a parcel with three taels of silver — money I scraped together and borrowed to pay the authorities. If you, good traveler, have found it, please honor Heaven's compassion and give it back to me; the merit will bless you for ten thousand generations." The first man put on a look of pity and said, "It was this well-to-do cobbler here who picked it up, and he meant to split it with me. But since it belongs to a poor man, I am willing to give up my share. Offer him a little money to redeem it, and have him hand it back to you." Xian had been pointed out by the man, so he had no choice but to open the chest and let the one who had lost the silver take out the original parcel himself. He came away with a mere one qian for his trouble and still counted himself lucky — never guessing that the crook had already swapped his own silver for a fake parcel. That evening he reached Wushi. He took out the reward silver to pay for wine and meant to add what was left to the big parcel. When he opened it, there was nothing but copper and iron inside — not a speck of silver. All he could do was weep bitterly and leave it at that.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - Translation of '绱鞋' as 'cobbler' is slightly inaccurate; the text implies a wealthy person who wears shoes made by cobblers (a customer), not necessarily the cobbler himself. 'Well-to-do shoe-wearer' or similar would be more precise.
  - The phrase 'silver would be sitting in his own chest' for '銀納置彼箱' is slightly ambiguous. The original means Xian put the silver into the *other* man's box ('彼箱'), which is why he felt safe (thinking it was secure with the accomplice). The translation says 'his own chest', which contradicts the plot point that the swindler took the real silver away.

But since it belongs to a poor man, I am willing to give up my share. Offer him a little money to redeem it, and have him hand it back to you."
