### 41. [8/10] 第三類 換銀騙 Category 3 · Silver-Exchange Swindles — 成錠假銀換真銀 (cat-03-s00) `[body]`
- **Score**: **8/10** (Accuracy: **9/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 泉州府客人孫滔，為人誠實，有長者風。帶銀百餘兩，往南京買布。在沿山搭船，陡遇一棍名汪蘭，詐稱興化府人，鄉語略同，因與孫同船數日，甚歡。習知滔樸實人，的可騙也。因言他故，往蕪湖起岸買貨。舟中說他尚未傾銀，有銀一錠，細絲，十二兩重，若有便銀，打換為妙，意在就孫換之。孫因請看，汪欣然取出真銀。孫接過手，曰：「果是金花細絲。」汪欲顯真銀，因轉在孫手接出，遍與舟中客人看，問：「好否？」都道：「是細絲。」遂因舟上有筆硯在，汪微微冷笑，將此銀寫「十二兩足在風窠底」。孫心中道：「此人輕薄，有銀何必如此翻弄？」因潛對汪曰：「出來人，謹慎些。」汪曰：「無妨。」孫因問：「要換折多少？」汪曰：「弟只零買雜貨，憑兄銀色估折便是。」孫因取出小八九錢重的，只九一二成色。汪看喜曰：「此銀九四五傾來麼？俱一樣如此即好矣。」蓋汪重估孫銀水，使孫樂換。孫取天平兩對，估折明白。汪即箱中取出白綿紙，與孫麵包作兩包。汪因佯起，轉身一回，故意誤收原銀入袖，曰：「此包是我的了。」孫曰：「不是，這包是你的。」汪即替出那假，亦綿紙包，與真銀一樣，交與孫收。孫接過手，亦微開包緊，見銀字無異，概不深省，即鎖封笥篋中。汪須臾起岸分別。孫一向到南京，取出前銀，乃是錫，懊恨無及，始知被他替包騙去矣。
- **Current English**:
  > A merchant from Quanzhou Prefecture named Sun Tao was an honest man with the bearing of an elder. Carrying more than a hundred taels of silver, he was traveling to Nanjing to buy cloth. At Yanshan he boarded a boat, where he chanced to meet a swindler named Wang Lan. Wang falsely claimed to be from Xinghua Prefecture, and since their local speech was much alike, he shared the boat with Sun for several days, and they got along well. He came to see that Sun was a simple, honest man who could certainly be fooled. Making some excuse, he said he would go ashore at Wuhu to buy goods. On the boat he mentioned that he had not yet had his silver melted down: he had one ingot of fine-thread silver, twelve taels in weight, and if anyone had loose silver at hand, a swap would suit him best. His real aim was to swap with Sun. Sun asked to see it, and Wang readily took out a real silver ingot. Sun took it in hand and said, "It really is golden-flower fine-thread." Wanting to show off that the silver was genuine, Wang took it back from Sun's hand and passed it around to everyone on the boat, asking, "Is it good?" They all said, "It's fine-thread." Since there happened to be a brush and inkstone on board, Wang gave a faint cold smile and wrote on the ingot: "Twelve taels, full weight, at the bottom of the wind-nest." Sun thought to himself, "What a flighty man—if you have silver, why fiddle with it like that?" So he quietly said to Wang, "A man out traveling should be more careful." Wang said, "No harm done." Sun then asked, "What discount do you want on the swap?" Wang said, "I'm only buying sundry goods in small lots. Whatever fineness you judge it, brother, set the rate as you see fit." Sun took out small pieces of eight or nine qian each, only about ninety-one or ninety-two percent pure. Wang looked at them and said happily, "Was this silver cast at ninety-four or ninety-five? If it's all like this, that will do nicely." Wang was deliberately overestimating the fineness of Sun's silver to make him eager to swap. Sun fetched a balance, they weighed the two lots against each other, and the discount was settled clearly. Wang then took white cotton paper from his trunk, and together with Sun wrapped the silver into two parcels. Pretending to get up, he turned away for a moment and deliberately "mistook" the parcels, slipping the original silver into his sleeve and saying, "This parcel is mine." Sun said, "No—this parcel is yours." Wang then switched in the fake, also wrapped in cotton paper and looking just like the real silver parcel, and handed it to Sun to keep. Sun took it, and though he slightly opened the tight wrapping and saw the writing on the silver looked the same, he did not examine it closely and locked it away in his trunk. Soon afterward Wang went ashore and they parted. Sun traveled on to Nanjing, and when he took out the silver from before, it was tin. He was sick with regret, but too late—only then did he realize the man had switched parcels and cheated him.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'silver from before' in the final sentence is slightly ambiguous; it should explicitly refer to the silver Sun received (the fake one) rather than his original silver, though context makes this clear.
  - The translation of '風窠底' as 'bottom of the wind-nest' is a literal calque that loses the likely meaning of a specific location or mark on the boat/ingot. In Ming contexts, this often refers to a specific spot on the boat's hull or a hidden compartment, but without more context, 'wind-nest' sounds obscure to modern readers. A note or slight adjustment might help.

Sun took out small pieces weighing eight or nine qian each, about ninety-one or ninety-two percent pure. Wang looked at them and said happily, "Was this silver cast at ninety-four or ninety-five? If it's all like this, that will do nicely." Wang deliberately overestimated the fineness of Sun's silver to make him eager to swap. Sun fetched a balance, they weighed the two lots against each other, and the discount was settled clearly. Wang then took white cotton paper from his trunk and, together with Sun, wrapped the silver into two parcels. Pretending to get up, he turned away for a moment and deliberately "mistook" the parcels, slipping the original silver into his sleeve and saying, "This parcel is mine." Sun said, "No—this parcel is yours." Wang then switched in the fake, also wrapped in cotton paper and looking just like the real silver parcel, and handed it to Sun to keep. Sun took it, and though he slightly opened the tight wrapping and saw the writing on the silver looked the same, he did not examine it closely and locked it away in his trunk. Soon afterward, Wang went ashore and they parted. Sun traveled on to Nanjing, and when he took out the silver he had received, it was tin. He was sick with regret, but too late—only then did he realize the man had switched parcels and cheated him.

### 42. [8/10] 第四類 詐哄騙 Category 4 · Fraud-and-Coaxing Swindles — 巷门口诈买脱布 (cat-04-s03) `[body]`

  > 忽日，有一棍在亭坐，见客负布而来，认非本城之人，心知其可哄，即叫曰：“买布！客人入亭来。”棍取其布，反复拣择，拿六匹在手，曰：“要买三匹，我拿六匹入内去拣。”即转入巷路，从后大街逃矣。布客在巷凳坐许久，时有一二行路者过此，心疑之，因随其后而入。转一曲墙路，见两旁并无人家，直前则出大路，心方知是被棍脱出。只问街两旁人曰：“方才有一人拿布六匹而来，兄曾见否？”旁人曰：“此巷往来极多，哪知甚人拿布？”布客道其哄买之由，旁人曰：“此是棍明骗去矣。”布客只得大骂，懊恨而去。
- **Current English**:
  > One day a crook was sitting in the pavilion when he saw a trader coming with cloth on his back. Recognizing him as no man of this city, he knew the fellow could be gulled. "Buying cloth!" he called. "Bring it into the pavilion, friend." The crook took the cloth and turned it over, picking and choosing, then gathered six bolts in his arms. "I mean to buy three," he said; "let me take six inside to choose among them." With that he turned into the alley and fled out the back street. The cloth seller sat on the bench in the alley for a long while. When one or two passersby came through, suspicion finally stirred, and he followed them in. Rounding the bend of the walled lane, he saw there were no houses on either side; straight ahead, the lane came out on the main road. Only then did he understand that the crook had walked off with his cloth. He could only ask the people along the street, "A man came this way just now carrying six bolts of cloth—did any of you see him?" The bystanders said, "This alley sees crowds all day—how would we know who was carrying cloth?" The cloth seller told them how the man had gulled him with a sham purchase. "A swindler has cheated you in broad daylight," the bystanders said. The cloth seller could only curse at the top of his voice and go off sick with regret.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'walked off with his cloth' is a weak translation for the Ming vernacular idiom '脱出', which implies a deliberate escape or making off with the goods after a trick; 'made off with' or 'got away with' is more precise.
  - The final clause 'go off sick with regret' introduces a medical condition ('sick') not present in the source text. The Chinese '懊恨而去' means to leave feeling remorseful and angry, without implying physical illness.
  - The translation of '转一曲墙路' as 'Rounding the bend of the walled lane' is slightly interpretive; the text suggests turning into a curved path along a wall, but the current phrasing is acceptable.

  > 将及月餘，和密报其夫曰：“松弟与我至知，今闻与令正有情，我屡谏不听。闻你欲捕之，若捕得，可轻打些。彼必叫我解交，我谕他多送你些银，以绝他后日妄为。慎勿害他性命。”林远闻言，怒气填胸。次日即托言外出，须三日后方归。松专瞰远去向，闻其出外，即往其家，搂罗氏入房调耍。林远从密处突出，打入房中，二人已解衣在床。远揪松于床下凶打，罗氏拼命拿住夫手，远不能多打。松求放曰：“愿以银赎免。”远曰：“要何人来保认？”松曰：“叫我和兄来。”远正合意，即遣人呼和至。和曰：“不行正路，以至于此。须召见你亲兄来。”松曰：“勿召我兄，只你代我出银与之，后日即还。”和曰：“我代议事，怎好出银？但今事急矣，我若不出银，此事无由解释。然必有实物相当方可。”松因写前毗连之田契卖之。和曰：“只可少作价，多则亦为林远所得。”止作价四十两。和归，取银三十两相付。远曰：“须六十两。”和曰：“奸情被获，合输妇价一半。纵令正美貌，可值六十金，此已一半矣。”远再三不肯。和曰：“彼田价四十两，我手中无现银，不如约一月后，再在我手接十两。”远要约批。和曰：“若他人议事，须加二抽头，我已该八两矣。今为你息事，何逼我约批乎？”遂无约批，放松同归。
- **Current English**:
  > When nearly a month had passed, He secretly tipped off the husband: “Song is my dearest friend, but now I hear he is carrying on with your wife. I have warned him again and again and he will not listen. I hear you mean to catch them. If you do, give him only a light beating. He is sure to call me in to mediate, and I will make him pay you a good sum of silver to put an end to his mischief. Whatever you do, do not take his life.” Lin Yuan heard this and rage filled his chest. The next day he announced he was going out and would not be back for three days. Song had been watching for Yuan’s departure; the moment he heard the man was away, he went to the house, pulled Luo into the bedroom, and began to fool around. Lin Yuan burst out of hiding and charged into the room, where the two had already undressed on the bed. Yuan hauled Song off the bed and beat him savagely; Luo threw herself on her husband’s arms with all her strength, so Yuan could not land many blows. Song begged for release: “Let me buy my way out with silver.” Yuan said: “Who will stand surety for you?” Song said: “Fetch my cousin He.” That was exactly what Yuan wanted, and he sent for He at once. He said: “You strayed from the straight path, and it has come to this. We must call your own elder brother.” Song said: “Don’t call my brother. Just put up the silver for me and I will pay you back in a few days.” He said: “I came to mediate—how can I be the one paying? But the matter is urgent. If I do not put up the silver, there is no way out of this. Still, there must be real property to match it.” So Song wrote out a deed selling He the adjoining field. He said: “You can only price it low; any more would just end up in Lin Yuan’s pocket.” They set the price at a mere forty taels. He went home, brought back thirty taels, and handed them over. Yuan said: “I want sixty.” He said: “For adultery caught in the act, the forfeit is half the wife’s value. Even granting your wife is a beauty worth sixty taels, this is already half.” Yuan refused again and again. He said: “His field is priced at forty taels, and I have no more ready silver on hand. Better to agree that a month from now he collects another ten taels from me.” Yuan demanded a written pledge. He said: “If anyone else had mediated, he would take a twenty percent cut—I would already be owed eight taels. Here I am settling your affair for nothing, and you press me for a note?” So no note was written, and He took Song home with him.

  - The translation of '约批' as 'written pledge' is acceptable but could be more specific to the context of a formal receipt or IOU note common in Ming financial disputes.

### 45. [8/10] 第五類 偽交騙 Category 5 · False-Friendship Swindles — 累算友财倾其家 (cat-05-s02) `[body]`
- **Score**: **8/10** (Accuracy: **9/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 从宇思曰：“人言慈不掌兵，义不掌财。我观起予慈善好义，诚直无智。何彼铺买卖与我相并也？当以智术笼络之。”以故伪相交密，时节以物相馈送，有庆贺礼，皆相请召。起予只以金为好意，皆薄来厚往以答之。从宇曰：“此人好酒，须以酒误之。”乃时时饮月福、打平和、邀庆纲，招饮殆无虚日。有芳辰佳景，邀与同游；夜月清凉，私谈竟夕。起予果中其奸，日在醉乡，不事买卖。从宇虽日伴起予游饮，彼有弟济宇在店，凡事皆能代理。起予一向闲游，店中虚无人守。有客来店者，寻之不在，多往济宇铺买。由是金铺日盛，洪铺日替，起予渐穷于用。从宇随取随与之，每一半九成，一半七八成；银又等头轻少，不索其借批，但云“须明白记帐也”。不四五年间，陆续借上六百餘两。乃使济宇往取之，起予别借二百两以还。后算过帐，尚欠四百餘两。逼其写田宅为当，方思还债取田。起予一皆从言。再过两年，本息合四百五十餘两矣。济宇力逼全收。起予求从宇稍宽。从宇曰：“吾银本与舍弟相共，彼在家尝怨我不合把银借你。今我不理，任你两下何如？”此时金宅有新立当契在手，起予推延不过，只得将产业尽数写契填还之。他债主知其落寞，都来逼取。千餘金家，不两三栽，一旦罄空。皆金从宇倾陷累算之故也。
- **Current English**:
  > Congyu thought: “As the saying goes, the kind cannot command troops and the upright cannot hold on to money. I see that Qiyu is charitable and fond of doing good, honest and without guile. Why should his shop’s trade rival mine? I must snare him with craft.” So he feigned close friendship: he sent gifts at every season, and whenever there was a celebration he invited Qiyu. Qiyu took Jin’s attentions as goodwill and always returned small gifts with generous ones. Congyu said: “The man loves wine; wine will be his ruin.” So he kept up a round of monthly blessing feasts, split-the-bill dinners, and guild celebrations—there was hardly a day without a drinking invitation. On fine days with lovely views he took Qiyu roaming; on cool moonlit nights they talked in private until dawn. Qiyu duly fell for the trap: he spent his days in a drunken haze and gave no thought to business. Congyu might spend his days drinking and roaming with Qiyu, but he had a younger brother, Jiyu, in the shop who could manage everything. Qiyu idled away his time while his own shop stood empty and unwatched. Customers who came looking for him and found him gone mostly went to buy at Jiyu’s shop instead. So Jin’s shop flourished by the day while Hong’s declined by the day, and Qiyu gradually ran short of funds. Congyu gave him money whenever he asked—but each time half of it was ninety-percent silver and half only seventy or eighty, and the weight came up light on the scale. He never asked for a loan note, only saying, “Just keep a clear account.” In less than four or five years the loans had mounted to more than six hundred taels. Then he sent Jiyu to collect. Qiyu borrowed two hundred taels elsewhere to repay, and when the accounts were settled he still owed more than four hundred. They pressed him to sign over his fields and houses as a mortgage—Qiyu meaning to repay the debt later and redeem the land. Qiyu went along with everything they said. Two more years passed, and principal and interest together came to more than four hundred fifty taels. Jiyu pressed hard to collect in full. Qiyu begged Congyu for a little leniency. Congyu said: “My silver is held in common with my younger brother. At home he has often complained that I should never have lent it to you. I will stay out of it now—settle it between the two of you.” By then the Jin household held the freshly written mortgage deed. Qiyu could stall no longer and had to sign over every property he owned to cover the debt. His other creditors, seeing him ruined, all came pressing for payment. A household worth more than a thousand taels was emptied in a stroke within two or three years. It was all the work of Jin Congyu’s entrapment and compounded scheming.

  - The phrase '金宅有新立当契在手' is translated as 'the Jin household held the freshly written mortgage deed.' The text implies the *debt* was secured by the deed, but the phrasing could be slightly sharper to reflect that the deed itself was now in their possession as leverage.

### 46. [8/10] 第五類 偽交騙 Category 5 · False-Friendship Swindles — 激友讼奸以败家 (cat-05-s03) `[body]`
- **Score**: **8/10** (Accuracy: **9/10**, Fluency: **8/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 后自鸣往小户人家取债，见其妇幼美，归家向应前夸曰：“我今往某家取债，其媳妇生甚美貌，女流中西施也。我以目挑之，俯首而过。其屋只一楹，数往来于前。我神魂飘荡，不能自禁。又以笑语挑之，此妇亦笑脸回答，似亦可图。只怕其夫姑有碍，未敢施为。至今挂恋在心，寤寐思服。”应曰：“此家是我甲首，又系佃户，图亦何难？我必先取之。”自鸣激之曰：“汝若能得，我输你一大东道。依我说，勿去惹此愚夫，若捉住，彼粗拳，真打死也。”应曰：“未闻佃客敢殴主人者。”次日，即往其家收条编。一见其妇，即挑之，遣其婆出外曰：“可外出觅菜来作午。”婆方出，即强抱其妇入房。妇在从否之间，见隔壁一妇窥见躲开。妇指之曰：“某姆在隔壁窥见，你勿为此。”哪肯休，只以为推托也。相缠已久，婆在外归。妇只得叫妈妈曰：“主人如此野意。”婆作色叱。怒，先往县呈其拖欠条编，反允殴里长。其佃人以强奸诉官拘审，邻妇窥见，亲姑捉获。其妇又貌美倾城，满堂聚观，啧啧叹赏。因审作强奸，应拟死罪。后投分上，改作戏奸未就。而家业尽倾，田宅皆卖与自鸣。（自鸣）反责曰：“我当初叫你勿为，你不听吾言，以至于此。”应曰：“你口虽叫我勿为，先已造桥，送我在桥中去矣，难回步也。今欲怪你，又怪不得。孟子谓‘非之无可举，刺之无可刺’，正你这样人也。”
- **Current English**:
  > Later Ziming went to a humble household to collect a debt and saw that the wife was young and beautiful. He came home and boasted to Ying: “Today I went to such-and-such a house to collect a debt. The daughter-in-law is a rare beauty, a veritable Xi Shi among women. I made eyes at her and she passed with her head bowed. The house is only one bay wide, so I strolled back and forth in front of it several times. My soul went adrift and I could not help myself. Then I teased her with laughing words, and she answered with a smile—she seemed there for the taking. My only fear was that her husband and his mother would be in the way, so I dared not act. I have been pining for her ever since, waking and sleeping.” Ying said: “That household is one of my tithing heads (jiashou), and they are my tenants besides. What is hard about having her? I will take her first.” Ziming provoked him: “If you can have her, I will stand you a grand feast. But take my advice: do not provoke that dolt of a husband. If he catches you, those coarse fists of his will beat you to death for real.” Ying said: “I have never heard of a tenant daring to lay a hand on his landlord.” The next day he went to the house to collect the consolidated tax. The moment he saw the wife he began teasing her, and sent her mother-in-law out: “Go find some vegetables for lunch.” As soon as the old woman left, he seized the wife and dragged her into the bedroom. She wavered between yielding and refusing; then she saw a woman next door peek in and duck away. She pointed: “Auntie next door saw us—you mustn’t.” He would not stop, taking it for a mere excuse. They had been grappling for a good while when the old woman came back. The wife could only cry out, “Mother! The master is forcing himself on me!” The old woman flared up and scolded him. Enraged, Ying went straight to the county and filed a complaint that the household had defaulted on the consolidated tax, and even accused them of assaulting the village head (lizhang). The tenant in turn accused Ying of rape, and the officials arrested the parties for trial. The neighbor woman had seen it, and the wife’s own mother-in-law had caught them. The wife was, moreover, a city-toppling beauty; the whole courtroom crowded in to stare, clucking in admiration. The case was tried as rape, and Ying was sentenced to death. Later he called in favors and had the charge reduced to seduction in sport, not consummated. But the family estate was ruined; the fields and houses were all sold to Ziming. And Ziming had the gall to scold him: “I told you at the start not to do it. You would not listen, and it has come to this.” Ying said: “Your mouth told me not to, but you had already built the bridge and walked me out onto it—there was no turning back. I want to blame you, yet I cannot quite pin it on you. Mencius said, ‘Condemn him and there is nothing to cite; censure him and there is nothing to seize.’ That is exactly a man like you.”
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'city-toppling beauty' for '倾城' is a literal calque; while understandable, it lacks the idiomatic punch of 'a beauty who could topple a city' or simply 'a stunning beauty'.
  - The translation of '造桥，送我在桥中去矣' as 'built the bridge and walked me out onto it' is slightly clunky. The idiom implies setting a trap where the victim has no choice but to walk into it; 'laid a trap that left me no way back' might be more natural.
  - The quote from Mencius ('非之无可举，刺之无可刺') is translated somewhat loosely. While the meaning is captured, the specific phrasing 'nothing to cite/seize' feels a bit weak compared to the original's rhetorical force about having no concrete fault to point at.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Later Ziming went to a humble household to collect a debt and saw that the wife was young and beautiful. He came home and boasted to Ying: “Today I went to such-and-such a house to collect a debt. The daughter-in-law is a rare beauty, a veritable Xi Shi among women. I made eyes at her and she passed with her head bowed. The house is only one bay wide, so I strolled back and forth in front of it several times. My soul went adrift and I could not help myself. Then I teased her with laughing words, and she answered with a smile—she seemed there for the taking. My only fear was that her husband and his mother would be in the way, so I dared not act. I have been pining for her ever since, waking and sleeping.” Ying said: “That household is one of my tithing heads (jiashou), and they are my tenants besides. What is hard about having her? I will take her first.” Ziming provoked him: “If you can have her, I will stand you a grand feast. But take my advice: do not provoke that dolt of a husband. If he catches you, those coarse fists of his will beat you to death for real.” Ying said: “I have never heard of a tenant daring to lay a hand on his landlord.” The next day he went to the house to collect the consolidated tax. The moment he saw the wife he began teasing her, and sent her mother-in-law out: “Go find some vegetables for lunch.” As soon as the old woman left, he seized the wife and dragged her into the bedroom. She wavered between yielding and refusing; then she saw a woman next door peek in and duck away. She pointed: “Auntie next door saw us—you mustn’t.” He would not stop, taking it for a mere excuse. They had been grappling for a good while when the old woman came back. The wife could only cry out, “Mother! The master is forcing himself on me!” The old woman flared up and scolded him. Enraged, Ying went straight to the county and filed a complaint that the household had defaulted on the consolidated tax, and even accused them of assaulting the village head (lizhang). The tenant in turn accused Ying of rape, and the officials arrested the parties for trial. The neighbor woman had seen it, and the wife’s own mother-in-law had caught them. The wife was, moreover, a stunning beauty; the whole courtroom crowded in to stare, clucking in admiration. The case was tried as rape, and Ying was sentenced to death. Later he called in favors and had the charge reduced to attempted seduction, not consummated. But the family estate was ruined; the fields and houses were all sold to Ziming. And Ziming had the gall to scold him: “I told you at the start not to do it. You would not listen, and it has come to this.” Ying said: “Your mouth told me not to, but you had already laid a trap that left me no way back—I walked right into it. I want to blame you, yet I cannot quite pin it on you. Mencius said, ‘Condemn him and there is nothing to cite; censure him and there is nothing to seize.’ That is exactly a man like you.”

### 47. [8/10] 第六類 牙行騙 Category 6 · Broker Swindles — 贫牙脱蜡还旧债 (cat-06-s01) `[body]`

- **Chinese Source**:
  > 张霸，四川人。为人机关精密，身长力勇。一日，买蜡百餘担，往福建建宁府丘店发卖。此牙家盆彻骨，外张富态，欠前客货银极多。霸蜡到，即以光棍顶作鬼名来借蜡，约后还银。数日后，霸往街游玩，其蜡遍在诸铺。及问其姓名，皆与帐名不同。霸心疑必有弊，故回店讯问牙人曰：“你脱我蜡去还前帐，可一一实报帐来。若不实言，你乘我几拳不得。”丘牙哑口无应。霸轮拳擒打，如鹰擒雀，如踢戏球。丘牙连忙求饶，曰：“公神人也。此蜡真还前客旧帐，并家用去矣，何能问各店重取？”张霸曰：“你将还人的及各店买去的，都登上帐，只说他揭借去，俱未还银。我将帐去告，取你硬作证，怕他各店不再还我？”丘牙依言，一一写成发货帐。
- **Current English**:
  > Zhang Ba was a native of Sichuan, a shrewd and meticulous man, tall and strong. One day he bought more than a hundred loads of wax and went to sell it at the Qiu shop in Jianning Prefecture, Fujian. This broker's house was poor to the bone but put on a wealthy look outside, and it owed a great deal of silver for earlier clients' goods. When Ba's wax arrived, the broker had smooth operators come under false names to "borrow" the wax, promising to pay the silver later. A few days later Ba went strolling in the streets and saw his wax spread through all the shops. When he asked for the buyers' names, none matched the names on the account. Suspecting a trick, he went back to the shop and questioned the broker: "You diverted my wax to pay off old accounts. Give me a truthful report of every account. If you don't tell the truth, you won't be able to take a few of my punches." Broker Qiu was struck dumb and had no answer. Ba swung his fists and pummeled him, like a hawk seizing a sparrow, like kicking a ball around. Qiu hastily begged for mercy: "You must be superhuman, sir. The wax truly went to pay earlier clients' old accounts, and some went for household use. How could I ask the shops to give it back?" Zhang Ba said: "Write down what you repaid to people and what the shops bought, and just record that they took it on credit and have not yet paid the silver. I will take the account and file a complaint, with you standing firm as my witness. Why should I fear the shops won't pay me a second time?" Qiu did as he was told and wrote out the delivery ledger item by item.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'broker's house was poor to the bone' is a slight misinterpretation of '盆彻骨'. In this context, '盆' (pén) likely refers to the broker's character or nature being thoroughly stingy/cruel ('bone-deep'), not necessarily that his physical house was poor. The translation conflates the metaphorical description of the person with the physical location.
  - The term 'smooth operators' for '光棍' is acceptable but slightly modernized; 'sharpers' or 'con men' might better fit the Ming vernacular tone, though 'smooth operators' conveys the meaning well enough.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Zhang Ba was a native of Sichuan, shrewd and meticulous in his schemes, tall and strong. One day he bought over a hundred loads of wax and went to sell it at Qiu's shop in Jianning Prefecture, Fujian. This broker was stingy to the bone yet put on a wealthy appearance, owing vast sums of silver to previous clients. When Ba's wax arrived, the broker sent sharpers under false names to borrow the wax, promising to pay the silver later. A few days later, Ba strolled through the streets and found his wax spread across all the shops. When he asked for the buyers' names, none matched those on the account. Suspecting foul play, he returned to the shop and questioned the broker: "You have made off with my wax to settle old debts. Report every account truthfully. If you do not speak plainly, you will not escape a few punches." Broker Qiu fell silent. Ba swung his fists, pummeling him like a hawk seizing a sparrow, like kicking a ball about. Qiu hastily begged for mercy: "Sir, you are a god! That wax truly went to pay earlier clients' old accounts and for household use; how could I ask the shops to return it?" Zhang Ba said: "Write down what you repaid to people and what the shops bought, recording only that they borrowed it on credit and have not yet paid the silver. I will take this ledger to file a complaint, using you as my witness. Why should I fear the shops won't pay me again?" Qiu did as told and wrote out the delivery ledger item by item.

### 48. [8/10] 第七類 引賭騙 Category 7 · Lured into Gambling — 好赌反落人术中 (cat-07-s02) `[body]`

- **Chinese Source**:
  > 闽人徐华胜，号含秀。为人矜夸骄亢，酷好赌钱。一日，买纸往京卖。有张鄂号叔真者，先富，因赌倾折，后有餘囊数百两，亦买纸往京卖。二人同县异乡，托处共店。鄂心怀一术，每在店中与华胜着棋，或赌东道，或赌时果。鄂棋本能让华胜一车，鄂乃孤客，徐姓众多，鄂每诈输东道，暗结华之乡亲，使钳其口，无得而议论之。故输五而赢二，而华胜不知鄂怀锋而之。
- **Current English**:
  > Xu Huasheng of Fujian, styled Hanxiu, was boastful and overbearing by nature, and he loved gambling fiercely. One day he bought a stock of paper to sell in the capital. There was also a man named Zhang E, styled Shuzhen, who had once been rich, had been ruined by gambling, and still had a few hundred taels left in his purse; he too had bought paper to sell in the capital. The two were from the same county but different townships, and they lodged at the same inn. E had a scheme in his heart. He often played chess with Huasheng at the inn, sometimes betting a treat, sometimes betting seasonal fruit. In truth E's chess was strong enough to give Huasheng a chariot, but E was a lone traveler and the men of the Xu surname were many. So E often lost the treat on purpose, while secretly cultivating Huasheng's fellow townsmen, making them hold their tongues and say nothing about it. He would lose five games and win two, and Huasheng never guessed that E had come with a hidden blade.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'come with a hidden blade' is a literal translation of the idiom '怀锋' (huái fēng) and does not capture the intended Ming vernacular meaning of 'concealing one's true skill or intent to deceive.' A more accurate rendering would be 'hid his sharp edge' or 'kept his true strength concealed.'
  - The phrase 'different townships' for '异乡' is slightly imprecise; in this context, it likely means they were from different places within the same county or simply different hometowns, making 'different hometowns' or 'different villages' more natural.
  - The translation 'cultivating Huasheng's fellow townsmen' is a bit stiff; 'winning over' or 'bribing' might better convey the active manipulation implied by '暗结'.
  - The phrase 'lose five games and win two' is clear but could be phrased more idiomatically as 'lost five and won only two' to emphasize the deception.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Xu Huasheng of Fujian, styled Hanxiu, was boastful and arrogant by nature, and he loved gambling fiercely. One day, he bought a stock of paper to sell in the capital. There was also a man named Zhang E, styled Shuzhen, who had once been rich but was ruined by gambling; he still had a few hundred taels left in his purse and had also bought paper to sell in the capital. The two were from the same county but different hometowns, lodging at the same inn. E had a scheme in mind. He often played chess with Huasheng at the inn, sometimes betting on a treat, sometimes on seasonal fruit. In truth, E's chess was strong enough to give Huasheng a chariot, but E was a lone traveler while the Xu clan was numerous. So E often lost the treat on purpose, while secretly winning over Huasheng's fellow townsmen, silencing them so they would say nothing about it. He would lose five games and win only two, yet Huasheng never guessed that E was hiding his true sharpness.

He looked all around; there was no trace. Chen Si went into Xing’s room and looked closely, saw the wall dug open, and said: “We cannot let this rest. There is nothing for it now. In a little while they saw two herd-boys bringing cattle home, and asked: “There were three men ahead with three loads—did you young fellows see them?” The boys said: “Those three went into Shangyuan Gully.” They asked: “What villages are in that mountain gully?” The boys said: “None.   > 逢七将材丝挑去一半藏讫，以材买丝底帐、各处税票悉皆灭矣。自己货发落在牙人张春店内。材上船见丝搬去，乃大与逢七殴；即补状，复告抢丝五十担，以一船客伙、艄公作证。逢七以猪血涂头，令二人抬入衙内，告急救人命事抵。即将银一百两，投本县抽丰官客，系本县霍爷母舅。材将银一百五十两，投本县进士魏贤及春元九位。逢七又将银二百两，亦投此数人。进士魏贤等先见本县为魏，又后催书，言辞支离，两下都不合矣。及审一起干证，艄公齐说：“相殴是实，未见搬丝。”本县判断担丝情捏，只以争殴致讼，俱各不合。材不甘，又赴本道告，批与本府推官陈爷审问。二人俱有分上，依县原审回招。材又奔大巡军门各司道告，及南京刑部告。然文状不离原词，皆因（袭）原断。
- **Current English**:
  > Fengqi had half of Bangcai’s silk carried off and hidden, and destroyed Bangcai’s silk-purchase ledgers and the tax receipts from every station. His own goods he lodged at broker Zhang Chun’s shop. When Bangcai went on board and saw the silk gone, he fought Fengqi hard; he filed a supplementary plaint, further charging robbery of fifty piculs of silk, and named a boatful of fellow merchants and the boatman as witnesses. Fengqi smeared pig blood on his head, had two men carry him into the yamen, and filed a countercharge of a life-and-death emergency. He then sent a hundred taels to a hanger-on who lived off official connections—Magistrate Huo’s maternal uncle. Bangcai sent a hundred and fifty taels to the county’s jinshi, Wei Xian, and to nine juren. Fengqi then also sent two hundred taels to the same men. Wei Xian and the others first went to the magistrate on Wei’s behalf; later their urging letters came, the language became disjointed, and they no longer fitted either side. When the whole set of witnesses was examined, the boatman and they all said: “The fighting was real; we did not see silk being moved.” The county judged the claim about piculs of silk to be fabricated; it was only a quarrel that led to a suit; both sides were in the wrong. Bangcai would not accept it, and appealed to the circuit; the case was assigned to Judge Chen of the prefecture. Both men had connections; the report followed the county’s original finding. Bangcai then ran to the grand inspector, the military governor, every circuit office, and even the Nanjing Ministry of Justice. But the papers never left the original wording, so all followed the original judgment.

### 54. [8/10] 第九類 謀財騙 Category 9 · Scheming for Wealth — 轿抬童生入僻路 (cat-09-s02) `[body]`
- **Score**: **8/10** (Accuracy: **9/10**, Fluency: **8/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 超世材，建阳人也。年方垂髫，往府应茂才之选，未取而归。以行李三担，雇挑费大，乃寄船中，命仆护之。己独于陆路轿行，只一日可归。在路雇轿时，打开银包，取二钱碎银与之。两轿夫从旁看窥，有银一大锭。不行上三十里，扛入山僻路去。超生曰：“我昨从船往府，此陆路虽今日初行，但官路须是往来通途，不当在此偏僻去处。”轿夫曰：“正是此去，望前便大官道矣。”又行，更入山径。超生心悟，即呼曰：“我知此不是大路。你们不过是要银，我身上只一锭银三两。我家富万金，只我一人，便把此三银子送你不妨，何必要起歹意？”二轿夫放下，曰：“如此便把来与我，免你一命。”超生笑解付之，曰：“此何大事，而作此举动？好小器！可送我还大路。”二轿夫不顾，得了银，径从山路奔去。
- **Current English**:
  > Chao Shicai was a man of Jianyang. He was still a boy with his hair in tufts. He went to the prefecture for the xiucai selection, was not chosen, and headed home. He had three loads of luggage; hiring porters would cost a great deal, so he left them on a boat and ordered a servant to watch them. He himself went by land in a sedan chair; it was only one day’s trip home. When he hired the chair on the road, he opened his silver packet and took out two qian of broken silver to pay them. The two chair men peeked from the side: there was one large ingot. Before they had gone thirty li, they carried him onto a lonely mountain path. Young Chao said: “Yesterday I went to the prefecture by boat. Though I am on this land road for the first time today, an official road should be a through-route with traffic; we should not be in this out-of-the-way place.” The chair men said: “This is the way; look ahead and you will hit the main official road.” They went on, still further into mountain tracks. Young Chao understood, and called out: “I know this is not the main road. You only want the silver. I have on me only one ingot of three taels. My family is worth ten thousand taels, and I am an only son, so giving you these three taels is no matter—why turn to harm?” The two chair men set him down and said: “Then hand it over, and we will spare your life.” Young Chao smiled, undid it and gave it to them, saying: “What a fuss over so little—how petty! Now take me back to the main road.” The two chair men paid no heed; once they had the silver they ran off through the hills.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'hair in tufts' is a literal translation of '垂髫' (tufted hair) which, while accurate to the visual, feels slightly stiff compared to the more natural Ming vernacular equivalent 'a boy just coming of age' or 'a youth'.
  - The translation 'look ahead and you will hit the main official road' for '望前便大官道矣' is slightly awkward; 'just ahead lies the main thoroughfare' flows better.
  - The term 'broken silver' for '碎银' is acceptable but 'small change' or 'loose silver' might be more idiomatic in this context.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Chao Shicai was from Jianyang. A youth just coming of age, he went to the prefecture for the xiucai selection but failed and returned home. He had three loads of luggage; hiring porters would cost too much, so he left them on a boat and ordered a servant to watch them. He himself traveled by land in a sedan chair; it was only a one-day trip home. When he hired the chair on the road, he opened his silver packet and took out two qian of loose silver to pay them. The two chairmen peeked from the side: they saw a large ingot. Before they had gone thirty li, they carried him onto a lonely mountain path. Young Chao said: "Yesterday I went to the prefecture by boat. Though I am on this land road for the first time today, an official road should be a through-route with traffic; we should not be in this out-of-the-way place." The chairmen said: "This is the way; just ahead lies the main thoroughfare." They went on, still further into mountain tracks. Young Chao understood, and called out: "I know this is not the main road. You only want the silver. I have on me only one ingot of three taels. My family is worth ten thousand taels, and I am an only son, so giving you these three taels is no matter—why turn to harm?" The two chairmen set him down and said: "Then hand it over, and we will spare your life." Young Chao smiled, undid it and gave it to them, saying: "What a fuss over so little—how petty! Now take me back to the main road." The two chairmen paid no heed; once they had the silver they ran off through the hills.

### 55. [8/10] 第九類 謀財騙 Category 9 · Scheming for Wealth — 轿抬童生入僻路 (cat-09-s02) `[note]`
- **Score**: **8/10** (Accuracy: **9/10**, Fluency: **8/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 按：超生初未晓此路程，但见扛入山僻，即知非是大路。察两人谋害之情，便捐银与之，免遭毒手。不然，命且不保，安能存银？又知寻本乡店主，托雇轿送归，方保泰然无危。此其年虽幼稚，而才智过人远矣。诗曰：“书显官人才，书添君子智。”令超生非读书明理，几何不蹈于陷井？
- **Current English**:
  > Note: Young Chao did not know this route at first, but as soon as they carried him into the hills he knew it was not the main road. He saw that the two meant harm, so he gave up the silver and escaped with his life. Otherwise he might not have kept his life—how could the silver remain? He also knew to find an innkeeper from his own county and have chairs hired to send him home, and so came through in safety. Young in years, his resourcefulness far surpassed others. As the verse says: “Books make talented men into officials; books add wisdom to a gentleman.” Had young Chao not read and understood principle, how far would he have been from stepping into the pit?
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The translation of '捐银' as 'gave up the silver' is slightly passive; in this context of a swindler's story, it implies a strategic payment to buy safety ('paid them off' or 'handed over the silver') rather than just giving it up.
  - The phrase 'how far would he have been from stepping into the pit' is an awkward calque of the rhetorical question '几何不蹈于陷井'. A more natural English idiom for this warning would be 'how likely was he to fall into the trap?' or 'he would surely have fallen into the pit.'
  - The verse translation 'Books make talented men into officials' is a bit literal. While accurate to the characters, the intended meaning is often about cultivation and elevation of character/status through learning.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Note: Young Chao did not know this route at first, but as soon as they carried him into the hills, he knew it was not the main road. Seeing that the two meant harm, he paid them off with silver to escape their deadly hands. Otherwise, he might not have kept his life—how could the silver remain? He also knew to find an innkeeper from his own county, hire chairs, and send himself home safely. Though young in years, his resourcefulness far surpassed others. As the verse says: 'Books cultivate talent into officialdom; books add wisdom to the gentleman.' Had young Chao not read and understood principle, how likely was he to avoid falling into the trap?

- **Current English**:
  > The day after that, the young master sent a servant to fetch a cook and lay on a return feast. One table was for the two young masters of the house; one table was sent into the inner hall for the mistress, with her maid sitting by. He ordered a boy to go in, pour wine, and wait on them. A small table treated the two stewards, with the four servants sitting with them. Each party drank until the second watch. The young master said: “Open the wine we brought and drink that.” In a little while it was warmed and brought; it tasted fragrant and sweet, and was not too strong—excellent drinking. The young master poured two large cups and offered them to the two sons: “This wine is a little easier on the palate; take three cups each.” The two sons each drank. Inside, the boy also poured for the mistress. The four servants also pressed the two stewards to drink. When the second watch was over, they paid off the cook, tidied up, and shut the doors. In the wine served later they had put datura blossoms. When the drug took hold, the whole household collapsed in a stupor. The fake young master and his six servants bound the widow and the rest, searched their goods through the night, and packed everything into five or six loads. At the first stroke of the morning bell they opened the great gate and carried it all out in full view; no one knew.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'lay on a return feast' is slightly awkward; 'host a return banquet' or 'hold a farewell feast' would be more natural.
  - 'Treated the two stewards' is a bit passive; 'served the two stewards' or 'set up a table for the two stewards' fits the context better.
  - 'Pressed the two stewards to drink' implies coercion that isn't explicitly in the text; 'urged' or 'offered' is closer to the original tone of polite but persistent serving.
  - 'Packed everything into five or six loads' loses the specific imagery of 'five or six dan' (a traditional unit of weight/capacity), though acceptable for general readers.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > The day after that, the young master sent a servant to fetch a cook and host a return banquet. One table was set for the two young masters of the house; another was carried into the inner hall for the mistress, with her maid seated beside her. He ordered a boy to enter, pour wine, and serve them. A separate table was set for the two stewards, attended by four servants. Each group drank until the second watch. The young master said: "Bring out the wine we brought and let us drink." Soon it was warmed and served; it tasted fragrant and sweet, not too strong—excellent drinking. The young master poured two large cups and offered them to his two sons: "This wine is quite refreshing; each take three cups." The two sons each drank their share. Inside, the boy also poured wine for the mistress. The four servants urged the two stewards to drink as well. When the second watch ended, they paid off the cook, tidied up, and shut the doors. Later, datura blossoms had been added to the wine served. As the drug took effect, the entire household collapsed into a stupor. The fake young master and his six servants bound the widow and the rest, searched their belongings through the night, and packed everything into five or six loads. At the first stroke of the morning bell, they opened the great gate and carried it all away openly; no one knew.

### 57. [8/10] 第十類 盜劫騙 Category 10 · Theft and Robbery Swindles — 诈脱货物劫当铺 (cat-10-s01) `[body]`

  > 县衙边有一大典当铺，贮积货物巨万。人以物件当者，不拘多少，皆能收之。一日，有客人容貌雄伟，径入堂内相拜，屏人语曰：“不敢相瞒，吾是异府人，常做君子生意，屡年积得器物甚多。前月拦得赃官七个柜，多有宝贝器玩。今幸藏到贵县，一时难以变卖。尊府若能收当，愿面估其值，以十分之一先交与我，待你卖后均分其价，每千两各得五百，明年对月来支。”店主曰：“愿借货物一看。”贼曰：“货物极多，共九大柜，外面难以开看。今夜须吩咐守城者勿闭门，待人定后，你雇十八人在船边来抬入宝店，当面看定，估计价值，两相交付。先求些现，餘者明年找完。”店主曰：“可。”夜间吩咐守城者留门，雇十八人往江边扛货。果抬九柜入店。
- **Current English**:
  > Beside the county yamen there was a large pawnshop, with goods stored by the tens of thousands. When people brought things to pawn, no matter how much, it could take them all in. One day a man of imposing looks went straight into the hall, made a bow, sent the others out, and said: “I will not hide it from you. I am from another prefecture. I make my living as a highwayman, and over the years I have piled up a great many objects. Last month I intercepted seven chests belonging to a corrupt official; there are many treasures and curios among them. Now luckily I have hidden them in your county, but I cannot sell them all at once. If your house can take them in pawn, I am willing to estimate the value face to face; give me one tenth first. After you sell, we split the price evenly—five hundred of every thousand taels each—and next year in the same month I will come to collect.” The shopkeeper said: “I would like a look at the goods.” The thief said: “There are a great many goods, nine large chests in all; they cannot be opened out in the open. Tonight you must tell the city-gate guards not to shut the gate. After the streets have grown quiet, hire eighteen men to come to the boat and carry them into your shop. We will look them over on the spot, estimate the value, and settle the exchange. I ask some cash first; the rest we will settle next year.” The shopkeeper said: “Agreed.” That night he told the gate guards to leave the gate open, and hired eighteen men to go to the river and carry the goods. Sure enough they carried nine chests into the shop.

After you sell, we split the price evenly—five hundred of every thousand taels each—and next year in the same month I will come to collect.” The shopkeeper said: “I would like a look at the goods.” The thief said: “There are a great many goods, nine large chests in all; they cannot be opened out in the open. Tonight you must tell the city-gate guards not to shut the gate. After the streets have grown quiet, hire eighteen men to come to the boat and carry them into your shop. We will look them over on the spot, estimate the value, and settle the exchange. I ask some cash first; the rest we will settle next year.” The shopkeeper said: “Agreed.” That night he told the gate guards to leave the gate open, and hired eighteen men to go to the river and carry the goods. ### 58. [8/10] 第十類 盜劫騙 Category 10 · Theft and Robbery Swindles — 诈脱货物劫当铺 (cat-10-s01) `[body]`

The thief took a key and unlocked all nine chests, then shouted: “Come out at once!” Two men from each chest, each with a short blade, burst out, bound the shopkeeper, and said: “Make a sound and we kill you!” Nineteen men rushed inside, bound all the men and women, then packed the shop’s goods into the nine chests. The nineteen split the loads and carried them out of the city, then told the gate guards: “You may lock the gate now.” Through the night they carried them onto a boat. After midnight someone gradually worked his bonds loose, then went and freed the rest of the household. They ran to the city gate; the gate was already shut. They asked: “Did you see the men carrying chests?” The gate guard answered: “The chest-carriers left the city a good while ago.” At the fifth watch the gate opened. They searched as far as the river; the thieves had cast off in the night, and no one knew where they had gone.

  > They paid off the porters and shut the outer door. The thief took a key and unlocked all nine chests, then shouted: “Come out at once!” Two men from each chest, each with a short blade, burst out, bound the shopkeeper, and said: “Make a sound and we kill you!” Nineteen men rushed inside, bound all the men and women, then packed the shop’s goods into the nine chests. After midnight, one man gradually worked his bonds loose, then went and freed the rest of the household. They ran to the city gate; it was already shut. They asked: “Did you see the men carrying chests?” The gate guard answered: “The chest-carriers left the city a good while ago.” At the fifth watch, the gate opened. They searched as far as the river; the thieves had cast off in the night, and no one knew where they had gone.

### 59. [8/10] 第十類 盜劫騙 Category 10 · Theft and Robbery Swindles — 京城店中响马贼 (cat-10-s02) `[title]`

### 60. [8/10] 第十一類 搶劫騙 Category 11 · Robbery Swindles — 私打印记占铺陈 (cat-11-s00) `[body]`
- **Score**: **8/10** (Accuracy: **9/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 船晚至岸，各收拾自己行李而去。尤刁民尾丘生之后，行至府前，在仆担头把铺陈抢下，曰：“多劳你挑，我自拖去。”丘生来抢，曰：“是我铺陈，你拖何去？”二人互争不开，打入府堂上去。尤曰：“是我物，他强争。”丘亦曰：“是他争我物。”太爷曰：“你两人互争，各有甚记号？”丘曰：“我自买来的，未作记号。”尤曰：“我毡条内打有印记。”当堂开视，尤取衣带中木印对之，果相同。太府曰：“此是尤某之物，丘何得冒争！”将丘打十板，令尤领铺陈去，各赶出府外。丘骂曰：“你这贼是何人，敢如此骗我？后必报之！”尤五曰：“适船间你说尤刁民者，即是我。我与你何干，而终日道我刁？故教训你，刁人是这等做耳！”丘心中方悔：“是我妄称人恶，故致此失也。”
- **Current English**:
  > In the evening the boat reached the shore, and each man gathered his luggage and left. You the Shyster trailed along behind young Qiu. When they reached the front of the prefectural offices, he snatched the bedding from the servant's carrying pole, saying: “Much obliged for the carrying — I'll take it from here.” Young Qiu lunged to grab it back: “That's my bedding — where do you think you're taking it?” The two of them grappled, neither giving way, until the quarrel carried them right into the prefectural court. You said: “It's mine — he's trying to force a claim.” Qiu said: “He's seizing my property.” The prefect said: “You both claim it. What identifying mark does each of you have?” Qiu said: “I bought it myself and made no mark.” You said: “I stamped a seal inside the felt.” They opened it up right there in court; You took the wooden seal from his sash and matched it to the mark, and sure enough they were the same. The prefect said: “This is You's property. How dare Qiu falsely claim it!” He had Qiu given ten strokes of the bamboo, ordered You to take the bedding away, and drove them both out of the yamen. Qiu cursed him: “Who are you, you thief, that you dare cheat me like this? I will pay you back for this!” You Wu said: “That ‘You the Shyster’ you were talking about on the boat — that's me. What was it to you, going on all day about how shifty I am? So I taught you a lesson: this is how a shyster operates.” Only then did Qiu feel regret: “I spoke ill of a man recklessly, and so brought this loss on myself.”
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The translation uses 'You' as a proper name for the character '尤', which is confusing and incorrect. The character's surname is You (尤), but in the text he is referred to as '尤刁民' (You the Shyster) or '尤五' (You Wu). Using 'You' alone creates ambiguity with the second-person pronoun.
  - The phrase 'Much obliged for the carrying' is slightly anachronistic and overly polite for a swindler snatching goods; a more natural phrasing like 'Thanks for the carry' or 'I'll take it from here' fits the tone better.
  - The translation of '太爷' as 'prefect' is acceptable, but 'magistrate' might be more precise for the local official context, though 'prefect' is not wrong.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > In the evening the boat reached the shore, and each man gathered his luggage and left. You the Shyster trailed behind young Qiu. When they reached the front of the prefectural offices, he snatched the bedding from the servant's carrying pole, saying: 'Thanks for the carry — I'll take it from here.' Young Qiu lunged to grab it back: 'That's my bedding — where do you think you're taking it?' The two of them grappled, neither giving way, until the quarrel carried them right into the magistrate's court. You said: 'It's mine — he's trying to force a claim.' Qiu said: 'He's seizing my property.' The magistrate said: 'You both claim it. What identifying mark does each of you have?' Qiu said: 'I bought it myself and made no mark.' You said: 'I stamped a seal inside the felt.' They opened it up right there in court; You took the wooden seal from his sash and matched it to the mark, and sure enough they were the same. The magistrate said: 'This is Your property. How dare Qiu falsely claim it!' He had Qiu given ten strokes of the bamboo, ordered You to take the bedding away, and drove them both out of the yamen. Qiu cursed him: 'Who are you, you thief, that you dare cheat me like this? I will pay you back for this!' You Wu said: 'That 'You the Shyster' you were talking about on the boat — that's me. What was it to you, going on all day about how shifty I am? So I taught you a lesson: this is how a shyster operates.' Only then did Qiu feel regret: 'I spoke ill of a man recklessly, and so brought this loss on myself.'

### 61. [8/10] 第十二類 在船騙 Category 12 · On-the-Boat Swindles — 娶妾在船夜被拐 (cat-12-s01) `[body]`
- **Score**: **8/10** (Accuracy: **9/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 次日，贾知县遣人迎婚，计为媒送去，贾与危氏在店成亲。又次日，危亦备席待婿，兼为起程。第四日，贾同妻收拾上船，危、计二人送别殷勤。船行一日无恙，次日泊于洲渚。计暗以船随，挨附其旁，挂一白裤于上。危氏同贾夫出船头观玩，见白裤船在旁，知计夫在候矣。夜与贾宿，着意绸缪，尽云雨之欢。贾以暮年新娶，夜夜不虚；况此夜船中又尽兴一次，帖然鼾睡矣。危氏遂密起，爬过有白裤船。计夫早已在候，相见欢甚，正似花再重开月再圆也。夤夜撑船逃回。
- **Current English**:
  > The next day Magistrate Jia sent men to receive the bride. Ji as matchmaker saw her off; Jia and Miss Wei consummated the marriage at an inn. The day after that, Wei also laid a feast for his son-in-law, and for the start of the journey as well. On the fourth day Jia and his wife packed and boarded; Wei and Ji saw them off with great care. The boat went one day without incident. The next day it moored at a sandbar. Ji secretly followed in a boat, drew up alongside, and hung a white pair of trousers on it. Miss Wei went out to the bow with husband Jia to look about, saw the white-trousers boat beside them, and knew husband Ji was waiting. That night she slept with Jia, made a point of being tender, and they made love to the full. Jia was old and newly married, and had not missed a night; besides, this night on the boat he spent himself once more, and then slept snoring, dead to the world. Miss Wei then rose in secret and crawled across to the boat with the white trousers. Husband Ji had long been waiting; they were overjoyed to see each other, as if a flower had bloomed twice and the moon were full once more. In the night they poled away and fled home.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'Ji as matchmaker saw her off' is slightly ambiguous; the original implies Ji (the swindler) was part of the farewell party alongside Wei, not just acting as a matchmaker in that specific moment.
  - The translation 'Miss Wei went out to the bow with husband Jia' uses 'husband Jia' which is acceptable but could be smoother as 'her husband Jia' or 'Jia' for better flow.
  - 'Husband Ji' is used repeatedly; while accurate to the context of the story where he is referred to as '计夫', varying it to 'Ji' or 'the con man' might improve readability without losing meaning.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > The next day Magistrate Jia sent men to fetch the bride. Ji, acting as the matchmaker, saw them off; Jia and Miss Wei consummated their marriage at an inn. The following day, Wei hosted a feast for his son-in-law to see him off on his journey. On the fourth day, Jia and his wife packed and boarded the boat, while Wei and Ji saw them off with great diligence. The boat traveled one day without incident, then moored at a sandbar the next. Ji secretly followed in another boat, drew up alongside, and hung a white pair of trousers from its mast. Miss Wei went out to the bow with her husband Jia to look around; seeing the boat with the white trousers nearby, she knew Ji was waiting. That night, she slept with Jia, being particularly tender, and they enjoyed themselves fully. Being older and newly married, Jia had not missed a night's pleasure; moreover, having spent himself once more on the boat that night, he fell into a deep, snoring sleep. Miss Wei then rose quietly, crawled across to the boat marked by the white trousers. Ji had been waiting long; they were overjoyed to meet, like a flower blooming again and the moon becoming full once more. Under cover of night, they poled away and fled home.

### 62. [8/10] 第十二類 在船騙 Category 12 · On-the-Boat Swindles — 买铜物被艄谋死 (cat-12-s02) `[body]`
- **Score**: **8/10** (Accuracy: **9/10**, Fluency: **8/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 罗四维，南京凤阳府临淮县人。同仆程三郎，带银一百餘两往松江买梭布，往福建建宁府卖，复往崇安买笋。其年笋少价贵，即将银在此处买走乌铜物，并三夹杯盘诸项铜器，用竹箱盛贮，并行李装作三担。崇安发夫，直到水口陈四店写船。陡遇表亲林子达，亦在此店中。达问：“买甚货物？”维曰：“只买些铜器去，更带杯盘等，欲留家用。”达同牙人陈四，代讨一箭船。舵公赖富二、水手李彩、翁暨得搬其行李上船，甚重，舵公疑是金银，乃起不良心，一上船后，再不搭人。维曰：“我要速去，何如不搭人？”舵公曰：“今将晚矣。明日随搭数人，便开船。”维叫三郎买些酒菜，今晚饮用。舵工与水手三人商议：“今晚错过机会，明日不好动手。”维与仆饮醉熟睡。半夜后，舵公将船移于闲处，三人将他主仆以刀砍死，丢尸于江。打开打看，乃是铜物，止现银壹十五两。富二曰：“我说都是银子，三人一场富贵。原来是这东西！”彩曰：“有这等好货物，也多值银。”富二曰：“发在何处去卖？”彩曰：“何愁无卖处？可安船在一处，沿途发卖，岂无人买？”
- **Current English**:
  > Luo Siwei was a man of Linhuai County, Fengyang Prefecture, in the Nanjing region. With his servant Cheng Sanlang he took a little more than a hundred taels, went to Songjiang to buy cotton cloth, sold it in Jianning Prefecture in Fujian, then went on to Chong’an to buy bamboo shoots. That year shoots were scarce and dear, so he used the silver there to buy black copperware to take away, along with laminated cups and plates and other copper pieces. He packed them in bamboo chests, and with the luggage made three loads. From Chong’an he hired porters all the way to Chen Si’s shop at Shuikou to book a boat. There he suddenly met a cousin, Lin Zida, also in this shop. Zida asked: “What goods did you buy?” Siwei said: “Only some copperware to take, and also cups and plates I want to keep for the house.” Zida and the broker Chen Si hired a slim riverboat for him. The helmsman Lai Fuer and the sailors Li Cai and Weng Jide moved his luggage on board; it was very heavy. The helmsman suspected it was gold and silver, and a bad thought rose in him. Once they were on board he would take no more passengers. Siwei said: “I want to leave quickly—why take no one else?” The helmsman said: “It is almost evening now. Tomorrow we will take a few more and then cast off.” Siwei told Sanlang to buy some wine and food for tonight. The helmsman and the two sailors talked it over: “If we miss the chance tonight, tomorrow we cannot act.” Siwei and his servant drank themselves drunk and slept soundly. After midnight the helmsman moved the boat to a quiet spot; the three men cut master and servant to death with knives and threw the bodies in the river. They broke open the loads and looked: it was copperware, and only fifteen taels of ready silver. Fuer said: “I said it was all silver—a fortune for the three of us. And it turns out to be this stuff!” Cai said: “Goods this fine are still worth a good deal of silver.” Fuer said: “Where can we send them to sell?” Cai said: “Why worry about a place to sell? Moor the boat somewhere and sell along the way—will no one buy?”
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'a little more than a hundred taels' is slightly imprecise for '一百餘两', which should be rendered as 'over a hundred taels' or 'more than a hundred taels'.
  - 'Black copperware' is an acceptable translation for '乌铜', but 'blackened copper' might better convey the specific material description in a Ming context.
  - 'Slim riverboat' for '一箭船' is a creative interpretation of the boat's shape/speed, but 'one-arrow boat' (a specific type) or 'fast narrow boat' might be more accurate depending on historical context; however, 'slim' captures the visual well enough.
  - 'Copper pieces' for '诸项铜器' is slightly weak; 'various copper vessels' or 'other copper items' would be more precise.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Luo Siwei was from Linhuai County, Fengyang Prefecture, Nanjing. Accompanied by his servant Cheng Sanlang, he took over a hundred taels to Songjiang to buy cotton cloth, sold it in Jianning Prefecture, Fujian, then went to Chong'an to buy bamboo shoots. That year, shoots were scarce and expensive, so he used the silver there to purchase blackened copperware, along with laminated cups, plates, and other copper vessels. He packed them in bamboo chests, making three loads with his luggage. From Chong'an, he hired porters all the way to Chen Si's shop at Shuikou to book a boat. There, he unexpectedly met his cousin Lin Zida, also staying at this shop. Zida asked: "What goods have you bought?" Siwei replied: "Just some copperware to take away, plus cups and plates I intend to keep for household use." Zida and the broker Chen Si arranged a fast narrow boat for him. The helmsman Lai Fuer and sailors Li Cai and Weng Jide loaded his luggage onto the boat; it was very heavy. The helmsman suspected it contained gold and silver, and a wicked thought arose. Once aboard, he refused to take any other passengers. Siwei said: "I want to leave quickly—why not take others?" The helmsman replied: "It is nearly evening. Tomorrow we will take a few more passengers before setting sail." Siwei instructed Sanlang to buy wine and food for the night. The helmsman and the two sailors discussed: "If we miss the chance tonight, tomorrow it will be too late to act." Siwei and his servant drank until drunk and fell into deep sleep. After midnight, the helmsman moved the boat to a secluded spot; the three men stabbed the master and servant to death with knives and threw their bodies into the river. They broke open the loads and inspected: it was copperware, with only fifteen taels of ready silver remaining. Fuer said: "I insisted it was all silver—a fortune for us three! And now it turns out to be this stuff!" Cai said: "Such fine goods are still worth a considerable amount of silver." Fuer asked: "Where can we sell them?" Cai replied: "Why worry about a place to sell? Mooring the boat somewhere and selling along the way—who wouldn't buy?"

### 63. [8/10] 第十二類 在船騙 Category 12 · On-the-Boat Swindles — 带镜船中引谋害 (cat-12-s03) `[body]`
- **Score**: **8/10** (Accuracy: **9/10**, Fluency: **8/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 熊镐，章富人，乃世家子也。力足扼虎，兼习棍棒。尝月夜挟二婢往后园，遇一虎跳墙入，即退入家，各持钢叉、大杖出。虎对面扑来，镐以叉抵，顺放于地，急打一下。虎复再扑，镐又叉放之，再打一下，虎遂回身而去。镐从后赶打，虎为之倒。疾呼二婢曰：“速来助。”二婢各以大杖以鏖之，虎立死杖下。时称之曰“打虎镐四官”。
- **Current English**:
  > Xiong Hao of Zhangfu was a son of a hereditary official house. He was strong enough to hold a tiger, and had also practiced staff and club. Once on a moonlit night he took two maids to the back garden and met a tiger that had jumped over the wall. He went back into the house at once; each took a steel fork or a heavy staff and came out. The tiger sprang at them face to face; Hao blocked it with the fork, pinned it to the ground, and struck once. The tiger sprang again; Hao again pinned it with the fork and struck once more; the tiger turned and left. Hao chased it from behind and beat it; the tiger fell. He quickly called the two maids: “Come help at once.” Each maid used a heavy staff and they laid on together; the tiger died under the staves. People at the time called him “Fourth Master Hao the Tiger-Beater.”
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'hold a tiger' for '扼虎' is slightly literal; 'strangle a tiger' or 'wrestle a tiger' better captures the physical feat implied by '力足'.
  - 'Staff and club' for '棍棒' is acceptable but could be more idiomatic as 'staff fighting' or 'martial arts' to reflect the context of a martial practitioner.
  - 'Met a tiger that had jumped over the wall' is a bit passive; 'encountered a tiger leaping over the wall' flows better narratively.
  - 'Each took a steel fork or a heavy staff' creates ambiguity about who took what; the original implies they each took one (or both types were available), so clarifying 'each armed with a steel fork or a heavy staff' would be clearer.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Xiong Hao of Zhangfu was a scion of a hereditary official family. He possessed strength sufficient to wrestle a tiger and was skilled in staff fighting. One moonlit night, he took two maids into the back garden when a tiger leaped over the wall. He immediately retreated indoors, where each person grabbed a steel fork or a heavy staff before rushing out. The tiger sprang at them head-on; Hao blocked it with his fork, pinned it to the ground, and struck once. The tiger lunged again; Hao pinned it once more with the fork and struck another blow, after which the tiger turned and fled. Hao chased from behind and beat it until it fell. He urgently called to the two maids: "Come help at once!" Each maid wielded a heavy staff, and together they battered the beast until it died beneath their blows. People of the time hailed him as "Fourth Master Hao the Tiger-Beater."

### 64. [8/10] 第十二類 在船騙 Category 12 · On-the-Boat Swindles — 带镜船中引谋害 (cat-12-s03) `[body]`

  > 后思遍游各胜处，故脱兄云：“将出外买卖。”兄阻之曰：“汝刚而无谋，莫思赚钱，还恐生祸。”镐曰：“老仆满起有力多智，与我同去，何妨？”兄不能阻。镐带百餘金行，曰：“吾出外相机置货，虽不得利，岂折本乎？有谁人欺得我者！”及游浙粤，有货可买者，仆满起曰：“此价甚廉，买归必得利。”镐曰：“吾远到此，未遍览此中景致，若遂置货，安能轻身自由？”仆累禀几次，皆不见听，知其志在浪游，不思利也，后只任之，主饮亦饮，主行亦随。不半年，本去三分之二矣。起复曰：“不归将无盘缠。”镐曰：“本虽少，亦要置些货归，可当远回人事相送者。”又挨两月，到湖州，起又催归。镐曰：“买何物好？”起曰：“笔墨上好。”镐曰：“不在行不会拣择，恐受人亏。亦须更买甚物，与母嫂及我妻者？银本已折，省他辈多口。”起曰：“细缎、镜好。”镐曰：“细缎无多本，不是这般客。不如买十两笔墨，十两镜罢。”起曰：“亦好。”催趱买归，只两小箱。镐曰：“此货甚妙，又简便易带。”
- **Current English**:
  > Later he wanted to tour all the famous places, so he told his elder brother, as a pretext: “I am going out to trade.” His brother stopped him: “You are headstrong and have no cunning. Do not think of making money; I fear you will only make trouble.” Hao said: “The old servant Man Qi has strength and much wit; if he goes with me, what harm?” His brother could not stop him. Hao took a little more than a hundred taels and set out, saying: “I will go out and buy goods as the chance comes. Even if I make no profit, how should I lose the capital? Who can take advantage of me?” When they toured Zhejiang and Guangdong, and there were goods worth buying, the servant Man Qi said: “This price is very cheap; if we buy and take it home we will surely profit.” Hao said: “I have come a long way and have not yet seen all the sights here. If I buy goods at once, how can I travel light and free?” The servant reported it several times and was never heard; he knew his master’s mind was on idle touring, not on profit, and afterward only let him be: when the master drank, he drank; when the master went, he followed. In less than half a year two thirds of the capital was gone. Qi said again: “If we do not head home we will have no travel money.” Hao said: “Though the capital is small, we still must buy some goods to take back, to serve as gifts after a long journey.” They delayed another two months and reached Huzhou; Qi again urged him to head home. Hao said: “What should we buy?” Qi said: “Brushes and ink are best.” Hao said: “I am not in that trade and cannot choose; I fear I will be taken advantage of. We also need to buy something for Mother, my sister-in-law, and my wife. The capital is already lost; let us spare them extra talk.” Qi said: “Fine silks and mirrors would be good.” Hao said: “We have not much capital for silks; we are not that kind of merchant. Better buy ten taels’ worth of brushes and ink and ten taels’ worth of mirrors.” Qi said: “That will do too.” They hurried the purchase for the return; there were only two small chests. Hao said: “These goods are excellent, and also light and easy to carry.”

Hao said: "These goods are excellent, and also light and easy to carry."

### 66. [8/10] 第十二類 在船騙 Category 12 · On-the-Boat Swindles — 行李误挑往别船 (cat-12-s04) `[body]`

  > 陆梦麟，江西进贤人。往福建海澄县买胡椒十餘担，复往芜湖发卖。有一客伙，将硼砂一担对换，餘者以银伐之。次日，叫店家写舵公陈涯四船，直到建宁。诸货都搬入船，只一仆詹兴，挑实落行李一担，跟梦麟同行。途中陡遇一乡亲，动问家中事务，语喇喇不能休。乃命仆先担行李上船，再来此听使用。仆挑往别船去，收在船舱已讫，再来寻主，尚与乡亲谈叙未决。见仆来，即差之别干，始辞乡亲到船。查行李未见，即将家人打骂；又坐舵公偷去，状告本县胡爷，言舵公盗他卖胡椒银一百餘两，以店家祝念九作证。舵公诉：“船中有客商十数伙，哪见他仆挑行李上船？”胡爷拘来审问，同船众商都谈，未曾见挑甚行李。胡爷曰：“船不漏针。别货物都在，独行李有银便会失落？”将舵公敲夹，不认，曰：“是他仆詹兴见囊中有银，自盗去以陷我；或错担别船去，以致有失。小人虽夹死难招！”胡爷又审詹兴曰：“想是你错认别人船为己船，忙中有失，非你背主，好好招来免夹。”詹兴不认，乃夹敲一百。受苦不过，只得招认：“是主人路遇乡亲谈话，我自担上船去，藏入船舱讫，再回听主差唤。及到船，并未见行李。是我一时错认，以致有失。恐主人加罪于我，我故不敢承。望老爷救小人一命。”胡爷将詹兴责三十板，劝梦麟曰：“是你自错。凡出外为商，银物不可离身。当担行李时，须叫詹兴看守，待你到船，然后差别干；纵错上别船，亦不会失。今若此，是你命该失财，岂可以怨仆乎？”各发出免供。
- **Current English**:
  > Lu Menglin was a man of Jinxian in Jiangxi. He went to Haicheng County in Fujian, bought more than ten loads of pepper, then went to Wuhu to sell it. A fellow merchant swapped a load of borax; the rest was paid in silver. The next day he had the innkeeper book Helmsman Chen Yasi’s boat, going all the way to Jianning. All the goods were moved onto the boat; only one servant, Zhan Xing, carried a load of the real luggage and followed Menglin. On the way they suddenly met a fellow townsman; they asked about family affairs, and the talk rattled on and would not stop. So he ordered the servant to carry the luggage onto the boat first, then come back here and wait for orders. The servant carried it onto another boat, stowed it in the cabin, then came back to find his master, who was still talking with the townsman and had not finished. When he saw the servant come, he sent him on another errand, then took leave of the townsman and went to the boat. He checked and did not see the luggage, so he beat and cursed the servant; he also pinned the theft on the helmsman, and filed a charge with Magistrate Hu of the county, saying the helmsman had stolen more than a hundred taels of silver from the pepper sales, with innkeeper Zhu Nianjiu as witness. The helmsman stated: “There are a dozen-odd merchants on the boat; who saw his servant carry luggage on board?” Magistrate Hu summoned them to hear the case; all the fellow merchants on the boat said they had not seen any luggage carried on. Magistrate Hu said: “A boat does not lose so much as a needle. The other goods are all here; only the luggage, which had silver, happens to be missing?” He put the helmsman in the press and beat him. The man would not confess, and said: “It was his servant Zhan Xing who saw there was silver in the bundle, stole it himself to trap me; or he carried it onto the wrong boat, and so it was lost. Even if you press this little man to death I will not confess!” Magistrate Hu then examined Zhan Xing: “I think you mistook another man’s boat for your own, and in the hurry something was lost; it was not that you turned on your master. Confess properly and you will be spared the press.” Zhan Xing would not confess, so he was pressed and beaten a hundred blows. He could not bear the pain and had to confess: “My master met a fellow townsman on the road and was talking. I carried it onto a boat myself, stowed it in the cabin, then went back to wait for my master’s orders. When we reached the boat, the luggage was not to be seen. I mistook it for a moment, and so it was lost. I feared my master would add to my punishment, so I dared not admit it. I beg Your Honor to spare this little man’s life.” Magistrate Hu had Zhan Xing given thirty strokes, and advised Menglin: “This was your own mistake. Whenever you go out to trade, silver and goods must not leave your person. When the luggage was to be carried, you should have told Zhan Xing to watch it, and only sent him on other errands after you reached the boat; even if it went onto the wrong boat, it would not have been lost. Now that it has come to this, your fate was to lose the money—how can you resent the servant?” They were each sent out and spared further depositions.

  - 'Pressed and beaten a hundred blows' conflates two distinct actions (the press/夹 and beating/敲) mentioned in the source text as separate punitive measures.

Unable to endure the pain, he finally confessed: "My master met a fellow townsman on the road and was talking. ### 67. [8/10] 第十二類 在船騙 Category 12 · On-the-Boat Swindles — 脚夫挑走起船货 (cat-12-s05) `[body]`

  > 黄三密访两日，不能得。第三日，公差来拿。到半途，见一脚夫柳五，将银三钱换钱用，随即买鱼肉等归家。黄三再拿到衙，禀曰：“并访不得。只今遇柳五换钱，多买鱼肉，事有可疑。”捕衙立差四人，同田秀才、黄三直往柳五家搜。只一间小房，搜果见赃。拿到捕衙，柳五供曰：“银物现在。前五日未敢出门，今日止用银三钱换钱，买物作欢。拐盗是实。”捕衙发打二十板，曰：“你二脚夫拐资客货，各该拟徒。但黄三捕出柳五，以此赎罪，再打十板释放，以惩后日。姑念柳五穷汉，只拟不应罪，纳完发放。”再叫田秀才具领状来，尽将原物领去。不数日，拿得真赃正犯，非有治才，安能如此哉！
- **Current English**:
  > Huang San inquired in secret for two days and could not find him. On the third day runners came to seize him. Halfway, he saw a porter, Liu Wu, changing three qian of silver into cash, then at once buying fish, meat, and such to take home. Huang San had him seized as well and taken to the yamen, and reported: “I could find nothing. Only just now I met Liu Wu changing money and buying a good deal of fish and meat; the matter looks suspicious.” The constable at once sent four men, who went with Licentiate Tian and Huang San straight to Liu Wu’s house to search. There was only one small room; they searched and indeed found the loot. They took him to the constable’s yamen. Liu Wu stated: “The silver and goods are here. For the past five days I dared not go out. Today I only used three qian of silver to change money and buy things to make merry. The theft is real.” The constable ordered twenty strokes, and said: “You two porters made off with a traveler’s goods; each of you ought to be sentenced to penal servitude. But Huang San caught Liu Wu, so that redeems his crime; give him ten more strokes and release him, as a warning for later days. I will be lenient to Liu Wu as a poor fellow: sentence him only for doing what he ought not, and when the fine is paid let him go.” He then told Licentiate Tian to file a receipt, and all the original goods were given back to him. In a few days they had caught the true thief with the true loot. Without a talent for governing, how could it have been done like this?

### 68. [8/10] 第十四類 假銀騙 Category 14 · Counterfeit-Silver Swindles — 设假元宝骗乡农 (cat-14-s00) `[body]`

  > 昔有一人，本农家者流也。辛苦耕田，服食淡薄，而性甚悭吝，家颇充裕。外地有骗棍到此地方，知这乡农性贪识惘，遂探其某日当在某处耕田，预将假元宝二个重一百两，埋藏其处。俟乡农正在力耕之时，贼棍故意在其山畔，作左寻右寻状。乡农问曰：“你这人在此寻甚么？”棍云：“我在此寻些东西，你问我则甚？”乡农只得默然。棍又认此树，复认彼树，如有所失状。乡农又曰：“你这人好笑，只管满山认树何为？”棍曰：“实不相瞒，我先父往岁曾被流贼所劫，亦同入伙。后来银子甚多，孤身难带，将银埋在各处，留下一帐登记，欲再来取，不幸死矣。今我依帐来寻此处树下的，不知哪个树是。幸遇你在此，可来助我寻。若寻得，分些与你不妨。”乡农遂带锄同寻。果在一树下，寻得元宝二个。棍佯作喜甚之态，说：“此若寻得，则他处皆可寻了。我实肯分些与你，只是此处无槌凿。”又曰：“此银我无贮藏所在，不如去你家下，代我寻完，分数个元宝谢你，尊意如何？”乡农云：“甚好。”但我与你素不相识，一旦至家下来往，岂不招人疑猜？”棍云：“当诈称是何亲故方好。”乡农云：“有了。我有一妻舅，六七岁时曾卖外江客人，至今并无下落。只认作我妻舅，回来看取姊姊、姊夫，有何不可？”遂将妻父、妻母姓名形状，一一对棍说讫。
- **Current English**:
  > There was once a man, a farmer by birth. He worked his fields hard, ate and dressed simply, and was terribly tight with money, so his household was fairly well off. A con man from out of town came to this district, and learning that the farmer was greedy and dull-witted, he found out which day the farmer would be plowing a certain field and buried two fake yuanbao ingots there ahead of time, a hundred taels in weight. While the farmer was hard at his plowing, the crook deliberately wandered the hillside nearby, pretending to search left and right. The farmer asked, “What are you looking for here?” The con man said, “I am looking for something. What is it to you?” The farmer had to keep quiet. The con man then studied one tree, then another, like a man who had lost something. The farmer spoke again: “You are a strange one—what do you mean by going all over the hill checking trees?” The con man said, “I will not hide it from you. Years ago my late father was captured by roving bandits and joined their gang. Later he came into so much silver that he could not carry it alone, so he buried it in various places and left an account book recording the spots, meaning to come back for it—but he died, poor man. Now I have come with the book to find the one under a tree here, and I cannot tell which tree it is. Lucky for me you are here. Come help me look; if we find it, I do not mind sharing some with you.” The farmer took his hoe and searched with him. Sure enough, under one tree they found two yuanbao. The con man feigned great delight and said, “If we have found this one, all the other spots can be found. I am truly willing to share it with you—only we have no hammer or chisel here.” He went on, “I have nowhere to keep this silver. Why not go to your house? Help me finish the search, and I will thank you with several ingots. What do you say?” The farmer said, “Fine with me. But you and I have never met. If you suddenly start coming and going at my house, will people not grow suspicious?” The con man said, “We should say I am some relative or old friend.” The farmer said, “I have it. I had a brother-in-law, my wife’s brother, who was sold at six or seven to a merchant from outside the province, and nothing has been heard of him since. We will pass you off as my wife’s brother, come back to see his sister and her husband. Why not?” And he told the con man, one by one, the names and looks of his wife’s father and mother.

  - The translation of '妻父、妻母' as 'wife's father and mother' is accurate but could be more naturally phrased as 'his wife's parents' for smoother flow.

  > 过数日，棍将帐与姊夫查，更有元宝十餘个，在某山某庵中。其庵无人居住，姊夫带饮食二盒，挑至庵中。时庵中棍已预令二贼在彼伺候，即将乡农背缚于柱中。其二贼抽出牛尾尖刀，再三要杀之。棍佯劝云：“我受姊夫厚款，吃得他兄弟鸡鱼多，勿杀我姊夫。”三贼将饮食吃了即去。其乡农叫天不应，入地无门。至次日午后，一牧童至，乡农叫救命，得解缚归家。妻子问曰：“何待今日方归？舅何不回？”乡农应曰：“勿说他，勿说他。”至今被人骗者，俗语曰“勿说他”。
