Two accomplices the con man had posted in the chapel were already waiting, and they bound the farmer backward against a pillar. The two drew ox-tail knives and made again and again as if to kill him. The con man pretended to plead for him: “My brother-in-law has treated me generously, and I have eaten plenty of his family’s chicken and fish. Do not kill my sister’s husband.” The three crooks ate the food and left. The farmer cried to heaven and got no answer; there was no door into the earth. Not until the afternoon of the next day did a herdboy pass by. The farmer shouted for help, was untied, and made his way home. His wife asked, “Why are you only back today? Why did my brother not return?” The farmer answered, “Do not speak of him. Do not speak of him.” To this day, when someone has been swindled, the common saying is, “Do not speak of him.”
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'went over the account book' is slightly anachronistic and stiff for Ming vernacular; 'checked the ledger' or 'tallied the accounts' fits the context of a swindler better.
  - The translation 'ox-tail knives' is literal but loses the specific cultural connotation of the weapon type often associated with rural violence in this period; while acceptable, it feels slightly clinical.
  - The phrase 'made again and again as if to kill him' is a bit wordy compared to the punchy nature of the original '再三要杀之'.
  - The translation of '舅何不回' as 'Why did my brother not return?' is accurate regarding the relationship (brother-in-law), but the English phrasing could be more natural as 'Why didn't your brother come back?' to match the wife's colloquial tone.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > A few days later, the con man checked the ledger with his brother-in-law: there were still over a dozen gold ingots hidden in a chapel on such-and-such a hill. The chapel was empty. The brother-in-law packed two boxes of food and drink and carried them there on a pole. Two accomplices the con man had posted in the chapel were already waiting; they bound the farmer backward against a pillar. The two drew ox-tail knives and repeatedly threatened to kill him. The con man pretended to plead for him: "My brother-in-law has treated me generously, and I have eaten plenty of his family's chicken and fish. Do not kill my sister's husband." The three crooks ate the food and left. The farmer cried out to heaven but got no answer; he had no door into the earth. Not until the afternoon of the next day did a herdboy pass by. The farmer shouted for help, was untied, and made his way home. His wife asked, "Why are you only back today? Why didn't your brother come back?" The farmer answered, "Don't speak of him. Don't speak of him." To this day, when someone has been swindled, the common saying is, "Don't speak of him."

### 70. [9/10] 第九類 謀財騙 Category 9 · Scheming for Wealth — 轿抬童生入僻路 (cat-09-s02) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **9/10**, Fluency: **10/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 超生自还寻大路，行至路边店舍，问此处有某县人开店否？人指示之，即入，对店主曰：“我系超某家，因雇轿夫，被其谋去盘缠银，又不能徒步走路。汝若识我家，托代雇两轿夫，送我到家，加还其工钱。”店主曰：“尊府大家，人皆闻名，我岂不知？”即奉上午饭，命两轿夫送回。归家言被谋之事，及某店归之情，家中大喜，曰：“得不遭毒手，幸矣！三两银何足惜！”因厚款二轿夫，仍专人往谢其店。
- **Current English**:
  > Young Chao found his own way back to the main road. He came to a roadside inn and asked whether anyone from his own county kept a shop here. People pointed it out. He went in and said to the innkeeper: “I am of the Chao family. I hired chair men and they plotted and took my travel silver, and I cannot walk the rest of the way. If you know my family, please hire two chair men to send me home; we will pay extra wages.” The innkeeper said: “Yours is a great house; everyone has heard the name. How would I not know?” He served him a midday meal at once and ordered two chair men to take him back. When he reached home he told of the plot and of how that shop had sent him. The family was overjoyed and said: “Lucky you did not fall into their murderous hands! What are three taels worth grieving over?” So they treated the two chair men generously, and still sent someone specially to thank that inn.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'we will pay extra wages' slightly misrepresents the original '加还其工钱', which implies paying back the swindler's wages (or compensating for the loss) rather than just adding a bonus to new hires. The context suggests Chao is offering to cover the cost of the swindled money or pay the new porters well to ensure they don't cheat him too.
  - The translation 'murderous hands' for '毒手' is acceptable but slightly modernized; 'deadly trap' or 'harmful scheme' might fit the swindling context better, though 'murderous' captures the severity.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Young Chao found his way back to the main road. He came to a roadside inn and asked if anyone from his county kept a shop there. People pointed it out. He went in and said to the innkeeper: "I am from the Chao family. I hired chairmen who plotted against me and stole my travel silver, leaving me unable to walk the rest of the way. If you know my family, please hire two chairmen to take me home; I will repay their wages generously." The innkeeper replied: "Your family is a great house; everyone has heard your name. How could I not know?" He served Chao a midday meal at once and ordered two chairmen to take him back. Upon returning home, Chao recounted the plot and how that shop had sent him. His family was overjoyed, saying: "Lucky you escaped their deadly trap! What are three taels worth grieving over?" They treated the two chairmen generously and still sent someone specially to thank the inn.

### 71. [9/10] 第二十類 買學騙 Category 20 · Buying School Places — 诈秋风客以揽骗 (cat-20-s04) `[note]`

  > 按：此棍称学道乡亲，而学道既已来拜，又请吃酒，则的是乡亲矣。况书、皂皆有身役人，为之翼护，人孰疑之？不知真乡亲已去，而此乃其托名者。彼衙门人唯利是图，所敛既多，何惜数十之板？况其顶头银仍在，虽革役，乌足以惩之。今人谓衙役知法，不知侮法者正是知法之人。唯蹈实地行实事，以真学问博真功名，勿萌侥幸，勿图钻刺，棍骗何从入哉？彼遭骗者，皆惰学不肖之徒，自取灾眚者也。
- **Current English**:
  > Note: This crook claimed to be the education commissioner’s hometown relative, and the commissioner had already come to visit and invited him to drink, so he truly seemed a hometown relative. Besides, clerks and runners all had posts, shielding him—who would suspect? They didn’t know the real relative had gone, and this was one borrowing the name. Those yamen men only seek profit; having collected so much, why begrudge a few dozen strokes? Besides, the silver they paid to buy their posts is still there; even dismissed, how is that enough to punish them? People today say yamen runners know the law; they don’t know that those who abuse the law are exactly people who know the law. Only walk on solid ground, do solid things, win a true name with true learning; don’t sprout hopes of a lucky break, don’t plot to buy influence—how would swindles get in? Those who are cheated are all lazy, unworthy students who bring disaster on themselves.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'begrudge a few dozen strokes' is slightly awkward; 'spare' or 'hesitate to inflict' would be more natural in this context.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Note: This crook claimed to be the education commissioner’s hometown relative. Since the commissioner had already visited and invited him to drink, he truly seemed a hometown relative. Besides, clerks and runners all held official posts, shielding him—who would suspect? They didn’t know the real relative had departed, and this was merely someone borrowing his name. Those yamen men care only for profit; having collected so much, why would they hesitate to inflict a few dozen strokes? Moreover, the silver they paid to buy their posts remains intact; even if dismissed, how could that serve as punishment? People today say yamen runners know the law; they fail to realize that those who abuse the law are precisely those who know it best. Only walk on solid ground, do solid things, and win true fame through true learning. Do not sprout hopes of lucky breaks, nor plot to buy influence—how then could swindles take hold? Those who are cheated are all lazy, unworthy students who bring disaster upon themselves.

Mistress Xin's heart was upright. Though the sorcerer's potent art could cloud her mind, she had the wit to warn her maid beforehand, and in the end she escaped the poison of his wicked lust. So however clever the art, it is no match for an upright human heart. If you meet a sorcerer, hold your heart fast and pay him no mind; what can his evil do then?

### 73. [9/10] 第一類 脫剝騙 Category 1 · Making Off with Goods — 假馬脫緞 (cat-01-s00) `[note]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **9/10**, Fluency: **10/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 吾觀作棍亦多術矣。言買馬，非買馬，實欲假馬作囮，為脫緞之術。故先以色服章身，令人信其為真豪富；既而佇立相馬，令人信其為真作家；迨入緞鋪，誑言有馬與夥，令人信其為真實言；至脫緞而走，以一傘貽慶，與緞客爭訟：此皆以巧術愚弄人也。若非府尹明察，断其為假道滅虢，則行人得牛，不幾邑人之災乎？雖然慶未至混跡於縲絏，緞客已被鬼迷於白晝矣。小人之計甚詭，君子之防宜密。庶棍術雖多，亦不能愚弄我也。
- **Current English**:
  > Note: Swindlers have many tricks. He spoke of buying a horse, but it was no horse purchase; what he really wanted was to use the horse as a decoy, a means of making off with silk. So first he dressed in showy clothes to make people believe he was truly rich; then he stood appraising the horse to make people believe he was a real connoisseur; then he entered the silk shop and lied about having a horse and a partner to make people believe his words were solid; and when he made off with the silk and ran, he left an umbrella with Qing so that Qing and the silk dealer would end up in court. All of it was clever trickery to make fools of people. Had the prefect not seen clearly and judged it a case of 'borrowing a path to destroy Guo,' then the passerby who picks up an ox would have become the townsman's calamity—the innocent bystander paying for another man's crime. Though Qing escaped being thrown into fetters, the silk dealer had already been bewitched in broad daylight. The petty man's schemes are crafty indeed; the gentleman's guard must be tight. Then, however many tricks swindlers have, they cannot make a fool of me.
- **Identified Issues**:

While accurate, it may benefit from a brief contextual note or slight phrasing adjustment (e.g., 'a pretext to swallow his neighbor') for immediate clarity to English readers unfamiliar with the specific allusion, though the current version is acceptable for a study crib.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Note: Swindlers employ many tricks. He spoke of buying a horse, but it was no horse purchase; what he really wanted was to use the horse as a decoy to make off with silk. First, he dressed in showy clothes to make people believe he was truly wealthy; then he stood appraising horses to convince them he was a real connoisseur; next, he entered the silk shop and lied about having a horse and a partner to make his words seem solid; finally, after making off with the silk and fleeing, he left an umbrella with Qing so that Qing and the silk dealer would end up in court. First he deposited his own silver with Guang so Guang would not suspect; then he faked sickness to open the door, waited until they were sound asleep, and stole their silver along with his own and fled. His scheme was clever enough: this is 'give a little to take a lot,' a sweet trick in the swindler's repertoire. Who would have known that Guang was an old hand who saw one move ahead? Sheng wanted to profit from another man's goods and ended up losing his own. A green sharp is no match for an old hand on the road. Heaven's justice is clear; let this serve as a warning.

First he deposited his own silver with Guang so Guang would not suspect; then he faked illness to gain entry, waited until they were sound asleep, and stole the silver along with his own deposit before fleeing. ### 75. [9/10] 第四類 詐哄騙 Category 4 · Fraud-and-Coaxing Swindles — 诈学道书报好梦 (cat-04-s00) `[body]`

- **Chinese Source**:
  > 到次年，都银南翮而归，诸春元会时，各述沈道之书叙梦之事，各抚掌大笑曰：“真是好一场春梦也！”此棍真出奇绝巧矣。以此骗人，人谁不乐与之？算其所得，不止百金以上。聊述之以助一笑。

The trickster was astonishingly, surpassingly clever: cheat people like this, and who would not gladly pay? Reckon his takings, and they came to well over a hundred taels. I record the affair briefly, to give my reader a laugh.

While it captures the irony of returning south with diminished status or resources (implied by the swindle), the literal meaning of '翮' (wing/feather) combined with '归' (return) suggests they returned home. The translation adds the specific detail of 'clipped' which isn't explicitly in the text, though contextually implied by the loss of money. A more neutral 'returned south' might be safer, but 'clipped feathers' fits the wry tone well.
  - The translation of '都银' as 'every one of them' is an excellent contextual interpretation. The original likely implies 'all the silver was gone' or 'they all lost their silver', and the translator has chosen to focus on the victims' return rather than the silver itself, which flows better in English.
- **Recommended Revision**:

When the new juren gathered for the spring meeting, each recounted Commissioner Shen's letter and the dream within it, clapping their hands and roaring: "A fine spring dream that was, truly!" This trickster was astonishingly clever—cheat people like this, and who wouldn't gladly pay? His takings must have exceeded a hundred taels. I record this briefly to give my reader a laugh.

### 77. [9/10] 第四類 詐哄騙 Category 4 · Fraud-and-Coaxing Swindles — 诈以帚柄耍轿夫 (cat-04-s02) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **9/10**, Fluency: **10/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 城西驿上至建溪陆路一百二十里，常轿价只一钱六分；或路少行客，则减下一钱四分或一钱二分，亦抬。但先邀轿价入手，便五里一放。略有小坡，又放下不抬。大抵坐轿两分，步走一分。凡往来客旅，无不被其笼络者。或当考期，应试士子归家，轿价便增至二钱四分，至少者二钱。不先秤银不抬。若银揽到手，不抬上二十里，便转雇上路夫去，把好价减，只以一分一铺转雇他人抬之。其下手抬者，仍旧五里一放，动曰：“我未得时价。”士子不得已，又重加之。但士人往来简少，都无与较。
- **Current English**:
  > From the post station west of the city up to Jianxi is a hundred and twenty li by land, and the regular sedan-chair fare is only one qian six fen. When travelers are scarce on the road, the bearers will knock it down to one qian four or one qian two and still carry you. But once they have coaxed the fare into their hands first, they set the chair down every five li; at the least little slope they put it down again and refuse to go on. All told, you ride two parts of the road and walk one, and every traveler passing through falls into their trap. At examination time, when the candidates head home from the exams, the fare jumps to two qian four, two qian at the very least, and they will not lift the poles until the silver is weighed out in advance. Once the silver is clutched in hand, before they have carried you twenty li they subcontract you to bearers bound up the road, shaving your good price down and hiring others at a mere one fen a stage to do the carrying. The men who take over still set you down every five li, forever protesting, "I never got the going rate." The scholars have no choice but to pay more. But scholars seldom travel, and none of them bothers to quarrel over it.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'subcontract you' is slightly anachronistic for the Ming context; 'pass you on' or 'hire others to take over' would be more period-appropriate.
  - 'Forever protesting' adds a slight temporal emphasis not explicitly in the source (which implies habitual action), though it aids readability.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > From the post station west of the city up to Jianxi is a hundred and twenty li by land, and the regular sedan-chair fare is only one qian six fen. When travelers are scarce on the road, the bearers will knock it down to one qian four or one qian two and still carry you. But once they have coaxed the fare into their hands first, they set the chair down every five li; at the least little slope they put it down again and refuse to go on. All told, you ride two parts of the road and walk one, and every traveler passing through falls into their trap. At examination time, when the candidates head home from the exams, the fare jumps to two qian four, two qian at the very least, and they will not lift the poles until the silver is weighed out in advance. Once the silver is clutched in hand, before they have carried you twenty li they pass you on to bearers bound up the road, shaving your good price down and hiring others at a mere one fen a stage to do the carrying. The men who take over still set you down every five li, forever protesting, "I never got the going rate." The scholars have no choice but to pay more. But scholars seldom travel, and none of them bothers to quarrel over it.

### 78. [9/10] 第五類 偽交騙 Category 5 · False-Friendship Swindles — 哄饮嫖害其身名 (cat-05-s00) `[body]`

  > 石涓，湖广麻城人。富而多诈，负气好胜，与族兄石涧尝争买田宅致隙。涧男石孝，读书进学，人品俊秀，性敏能文，人多拟其可中。石涓尝怀妒忌，思“吾生平发财，被涧兄所压。今其子又居士列，是虎而辅翼也。”因思计暗伤涧、孝父子。

Jian’s son Shi Xiao studied, won a place in the county school, and was handsome, quick-witted, and a gifted writer; many predicted he would pass the examinations. Shi Juan nursed his jealousy: “All my life I have made money, yet brother Jian has always kept me in his shadow. Now his son has joined the ranks of the scholars—a tiger grown wings.” So he cast about for a scheme to wound Jian and Xiao, father and son, in secret.

- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Shi Juan was a native of Macheng in Huguang. Rich and full of deceit, he was touchy and hated to lose. He had once clashed with his elder cousin Shi Jian over rival bids for fields and houses, and bad blood remained between them. Jian's son Shi Xiao studied, won a place in the county school, and was handsome, quick-witted, and a gifted writer; many predicted he would pass the examinations. Shi Juan nursed his jealousy: "All my life I have made money, yet brother Jian has always held me down. Now his son has joined the ranks of the scholars—a tiger grown wings." So he cast about for a scheme to wound Jian and Xiao, father and son, in secret.

### 79. [9/10] 第五類 偽交騙 Category 5 · False-Friendship Swindles — 哄友犯奸谋其田 (cat-05-s01) `[body]`

  > 同乡有林远者，性刚而暴。其妻罗氏，貌美好淫，与夫反睦〔目〕。和乘机挑之，遂通往来，情甚密。假意不令松知，实欲使之知之。故遮头露尾，为松觑破。松乃怪和曰：“枉自与你相知。有此美妇人，何不引我一宿，岂便夺你爱乎？”和逊谢曰：“此妇极有情，若引你去，必深相怜爱。恐你往来无节，事机不密，其夫若知，有误身家不便矣。”松只疑其专宠，乃私往挑之，罗氏遂允。后来情更绸缪，每候其夫出外，非和往则松往，甚且三人同床，情如一体。
- **Current English**:
  > In the same village lived a man named Lin Yuan, hard and violent by nature. His wife, born Luo, was beautiful and loose, and she and her husband were at odds. He seized the opening and seduced her; they began an affair and grew very close. He pretended to keep it from Song while in fact wanting Song to find out—half hiding it, half letting it show—until Song saw through him. Song reproached him: “So much for our friendship. With a beauty like that, why not take me along for a night? Do you think I would steal her from you?” He answered with a show of deference: “The woman is full of feeling. If I brought you, she would surely dote on you. But I fear you would come and go without restraint, word would leak out, and if her husband found out, it could cost you your family and your neck.” Song only suspected He of keeping her all to himself, so he went on his own to seduce her, and Luo gave in. After that the affair grew still more tangled: whenever the husband was out, if it was not He who went, it was Song; at times all three even shared one bed, close as a single body.
- **Identified Issues**:

While 'neck' captures the danger of death, 'family' here refers to one's household/property/status rather than literal kin. A more precise rendering would be 'ruin your household' or 'endanger your life and property'.

### 80. [9/10] 第六類 牙行騙 Category 6 · Broker Swindles — 狡牙脱纸以女偿 (cat-06-s00) `[body]`

  > 守训男施钦知之为，本年亦装纸到苏州，往拜翁家，呼翁为“外祖”。翁不瞅睬他。请庶母出见，亦拒不出。众客伙皆怒而嗾曰：“你父以八百两聘礼，止成亲四载，未期服又重嫁他人。今一出见何害？情甚可恶！汝何不鸣官？”钦乃告于巡街蔡御史。时翁滨二得施为婿，复振家风，又发资金千餘，见告，毫无惧意。两下各投分上，讦讼几二年。各司道皆纳分上，附会而判。后钦状告刑部，始获公断曰：“翁滨二以女抵偿八百两，几与绿珠同价矣。但守训自肯，其财礼勿论。今夫服未满重嫁梁客，兜重财物，是以女为货，不顾律法。合责三十板，断身资银一百两，并守训为云英置衣资首饰银五十两，共与施钦领之。”因此积讼连年，滨二之家财尽倾，仍流落于贫矣。
- **Current English**:
  > When Shouxun's son Shi Qin learned of this, that same year he too shipped paper to Suzhou. He went to pay his respects at the Weng house and addressed Weng as "maternal grandfather." Weng ignored him. He asked for his stepmother to come out and see him; she too refused to come out. The other merchants there were angry and egged him on: "Your father paid eight hundred taels in betrothal gifts, they were man and wife only four years, and before the mourning was over she was married off to another man. What harm would it do her to come out and see you once? The whole thing is outrageous! Why don't you report it to the authorities?" Qin then filed a complaint with the street-patrol censor Cai. By then Weng Bin'er, having gained Shi as a son-in-law, had restored the family fortunes and put out another thousand-odd in capital, so when the complaint came he felt not the slightest fear. Both sides enlisted backers, and the litigation dragged on nearly two years. Every office that handled the case took the inducements and stretched the facts to decide it. Only when Qin filed with the Ministry of Justice did he get a fair judgment: "Weng Bin'er paid off eight hundred taels with his daughter — nearly the price of Lüzhu, the most costly concubine of old. But since Shouxun consented of his own accord, we will not go into the betrothal money. Now, before her husband's mourning was complete, she was married off again to the merchant Liang, scooping up a second payment — this is treating a daughter as merchandise, with no regard for the law. He shall be beaten thirty strokes, and we award the body-price of one hundred taels, plus the fifty taels Shouxun spent on clothes and jewelry for Yunying, all to be paid over to Shi Qin." Because the lawsuits piled up year after year, Bin'er's family wealth was entirely drained, and he sank back into poverty.

### 81. [9/10] 第六類 牙行騙 Category 6 · Broker Swindles — 狡牙脱纸以女偿 (cat-06-s00) `[note]`

  > 按：脱骗之害，首侠棍，次狡侩。侠棍设局暗脱，窃盗也。狡侩骗货明卖，强盗也。二者当与盗同科。凡牙侩之弊，客货入店，彼皆作纲抵偿，又多窃取供家，每以后客货盖前客帐，此穷牙常态也。施守训在不早审牙家，致落此坑堑。只可小心逼取，或继以告，不当图其女为妾。夫以六旬上人，岁月几何，纳妾异地，能无后患乎？贻子后讼，所费不资。虽最终取胜，得不偿失矣。独恨翁滨二负心歹汉，以一女而还银八百两，得已过分，又得婿扶以成家；后女虽再嫁，当以身资还施之男，永可无患矣。乃贪心不满，再致倾家，真可为欺心负义之鉴。
- **Current English**:
  > Note: Of the harm done by swindles, the worst offenders are the swaggering sharps, and next the cunning brokers. The swaggering sharps set up schemes and swindle you in the dark — that is theft. The cunning brokers cheat you out of your goods and sell them in broad daylight — that is robbery. Both should be sentenced the same as bandits. As for the usual abuses of brokers: once a client's goods come into the shop, they lump them in to cover their debts, and they often skim off more for their own households; they habitually use a later client's goods to cover an earlier client's account. This is the normal state of a poor broker. Shi Shouxun's mistake was not sizing up the broker early on, so he fell into this pit. He should have pressed carefully for payment, or followed up with a lawsuit; he should not have schemed to take the daughter as a concubine. A man past sixty — how many years could he have left? Taking a concubine in a strange place — how could there not be trouble afterward? He left his son a lawsuit, and the costs were beyond counting. Though he won in the end, the gain did not cover the loss. What is hateful is Weng Bin'er, a heartless scoundrel: with one daughter he repaid eight hundred taels, already more than enough, and on top of that got a son-in-law's help to rebuild his household. Later, even when the girl remarried, he should have returned the body-price to Shi's son, and there would have been no trouble ever after. But his greedy heart was not satisfied, and he brought ruin on his house a second time. Truly this can serve as a warning mirror for those who cheat their conscience and betray what is right.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The translation of '侠棍' as 'swaggering sharps' is slightly interpretive; while 'sharps' fits the context well, 'swaggering' adds a nuance not explicitly in '侠' (which here implies bold/aggressive rather than just swaggering). However, it captures the spirit effectively.

### 82. [9/10] 第六類 牙行騙 Category 6 · Broker Swindles — 贫牙脱蜡还旧债 (cat-06-s01) `[note]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **9/10**, Fluency: **10/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 按：出外为商，以缥缈之身，涉寡亲之境，全仗经纪以为耳目。若遇经纪公正，则货物有主；一投狡侩，而抑货亏价必矣。是择经纪，乃经商一大关系也，可不慎哉！如其人言谈直率，此是公正之人。若初会晤间，上下估看，方露微言，则其心中狡猾可知。若价即言而不远，应对迟慢，心必怀欺。若屋宇精致，分外巧样，多是奢华务外之人，内必不能积聚。倘衣补垢腻，人鄙形猥，肩耸目光，巾帽不称寒暑，此皆贫穷之辈。若巧异妆扮，服色变常，必非创置之人，其内必无财钞。若衣冠不华，惟服布衣，此乃老实本分，不可以斯之曰贫。商而知此，何至如张霸，被牙所脱也？况非刚正之梅爷，肯听分上，几乎素手归矣。故录之，以示为商者。当货物发脱之初，细审经纪对手，发落方可保无虞矣。
- **Current English**:
  > Note: When you go abroad to trade, a lone drifter in a country with few kin, you rely entirely on the broker as your eyes and ears. If you meet an honest broker, your goods have a master; once you fall in with a cunning one, your goods will be held back and your price cheated — that is certain. So choosing a broker is one of the great matters of trade; how can you not be careful? If a man's talk is straight and frank, he is an honest man. If at the first meeting he sizes you up and down and only then lets slip a few words, you can tell his mind is sly. If he names a price readily and it is not far off the mark, yet his replies are slow and halting, his heart must harbor deceit. If his house is finely built and fancier than it needs to be, he is usually a showy, extravagant man who cannot have saved much inside. If his clothes are patched and greasy, his look mean and his frame shabby, his shoulders hunched and his gaze shifty, his cap and kerchief out of season — these are all poor men. If he dresses in clever, outlandish ways and his colors are out of the ordinary, he is surely not a man who has built anything up, and he has no cash inside. If his cap and gown are plain and he wears only cloth, that is an honest, steady man; you cannot call him poor on that account. If a merchant knows this, how would he end up like Zhang Ba, swindled by a broker? And if the judge had not been the upright Master Mei but one willing to listen to backers, he would very nearly have gone home empty-handed. So I record this to show merchants: at the very start, when your goods are about to be released for sale, look carefully at the broker across from you; only then can you let the goods go and be sure of no mishap.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'swindled by a broker' is slightly less precise than the guide's preferred 'get away with' or 'make off with' for the term '脱', though it conveys the general meaning correctly.
- **Recommended Revision**:

If his clothes are patched and greasy, his look mean and his frame shabby, his shoulders hunched and his gaze shifty, his cap and kerchief out of season — these are all poor men. If he dresses in clever, outlandish ways and his colors are out of the ordinary, he is surely not a man who has built anything up, and he has no cash inside. If his cap and gown are plain and he wears only cloth, that is an honest, steady man; you cannot call him poor on that account. If a merchant knows this, how would he end up like Zhang Ba, having been made off with by a broker? So I record this to show merchants: at the very start, when your goods are about to be released for sale, look carefully at the broker across from you; only then can you let the goods go and be sure of no mishap.

- **Current English**:
  > Zhang Shisheng of Juxi was a pampered son of wealth. His father had amassed ten thousand taels and divided them equally among Shisheng and his brothers; the fields and gardens were fertile, and the sons lived at ease on a ready-made estate. When the father died, just as the Wanli coinage was first coming into circulation, swindlers lured Shisheng into gambling. A greenhorn from a rich house, he saw only the bold drinking and swagger of the gambling rooms, a wild and carefree life that suited him. How would he know that money is to be treasured? Within a few months he had lost several hundred taels of silver, yet he still went on gambling happily, with no thought of stopping.

However, this is a minor interpretive nuance rather than an error.

  > 有二三赌伙寻至府，闻荻溪已入妓家，众即划计曰：“如此如此笼络之，可尽夺其金矣。”次日，候荻溪出外寻赌伙，即入对细卿曰：“荻溪只好赌，不好嫖，彼无厚物与你。今依我如此如此行，先送你二十两人事。后赌得的，每一百两复许加二抽。”细卿许诺，午设盛馔，方与荻溪入席饮数杯。忽二家人来送礼物，辉煌熳烂，皆上好物件，约值二十餘金。曰：“公子命送此薄仪，少倾便到。”细卿逐一看过，尽数收起，以茶待二家人于外，复来席陪荻溪，且喜且作懊恼之意。荻溪曰：“是何人送你厚礼，你反似犹闷，何故？”细卿曰：“不问正难开口。此是黄公子送的。旧年在此赌钱，输去银千餘两，我亦得他厚惠。今日将到，望相公赦我，妾须出去迎他。容后日多陪相公几日，以补罪。”荻溪曰：“既是公子，我便出外让他。”细卿喜曰：“相公如此宽容，是妾有二天也。”荻溪将拂衣起，细卿挽住曰：“少坐不妨！更有一件，此人极活泼无崖岸。少间乘机提起，若请相见，或在此同话，为我陪客。得借重高贤，亦为我增声价也。”荻溪本欲避席，只闻公子旧在此赌，心中早已喜十分。使一仆伏侍，在内独酌，叫细卿出外迎客。
- **Current English**:
  > Two or three of his gambling partners tracked him to the prefectural city. Hearing that Dixi had moved in with a courtesan, they laid their plan: “We will rope him in thus-and-so, and we can take every coin he has.” The next day, waiting until Dixi had gone out to look for a game, they went in and said to Xiqing: “Dixi only cares for gambling, not for women; he has no rich gifts for you. Do as we tell you, thus-and-so. We will send you twenty taels as a gift up front, and afterward, of whatever we win at the table, you shall have an extra twenty in every hundred.” Xiqing agreed. At noon she laid out a rich meal, and she and Dixi had drunk only a few cups when two menservants suddenly arrived bearing gifts—dazzling, splendid things, all first-rate, worth more than twenty taels. “Our young lord sends this trifling token,” they said. “He will be here shortly.” Xiqing looked the gifts over one by one and had them all put away, served tea to the two servants outside, then returned to the table to keep Dixi company—half delighted, half putting on a show of vexation. Dixi said, “Someone sends you rich gifts, and you look put out instead. Why?” Xiqing said, “If you had not asked, I could hardly have brought it up. These are from Young Lord Huang. Last year he gambled here and lost more than a thousand taels, and he was generous to me besides. He is due to arrive today. I beg your pardon, sir—I must go out to welcome him. Let me make it up to you with extra days of company afterward.” Dixi said, “If it is a young lord, I will step out and leave the field to him.” Xiqing said brightly, “With such forbearance, sir, you are a second heaven to me.” Dixi made to rise, but she held him back. “Sit a while longer, it does no harm. One thing more: this man is thoroughly easygoing, with no standoffish airs. In a while, if the chance arises and he asks to meet you, or wants to talk here together, keep him company for me. If you lend me your worthy presence, it raises my standing too.” Dixi had meant to leave the table, but the moment he heard that the young lord used to gamble here, his heart was already ten parts pleased. He kept his one servant to wait on him, drank alone in the inner room, and sent Xiqing out to receive the guest.

He did not know that after appeals through county, prefecture, circuit, the offices, and the Ministry of Justice, his capital would all be gone and he still could not win. By then he was a ram with its horns caught in the hedge—stuck, with no way forward or back; gloom made him ill, and regret came too late. If his uncle had not seen the moment to wrap it up and send him home, he would nearly have died of misery abroad and become a ghost in a strange land. “Humility brings benefit; fullness invites loss”—this has been recorded since ancient times. So even if a commoner outdoes you, do not swagger over others on the strength of rank or state. The Sage’s teaching returns to this again and again. Even princes and great men, time after time, have pride purchase their ruin—how much more so ordinary private men! Even in the inner rooms among close kin, arrogance and slackness that go off the path are already wrong—how much more in a place of sojourn! A merchant lodges in a region with few kin, among people from other parts. Keep hardness and softness in balance, stay composed and hold to clarity, and you still fear an unexpected turn—can you then look down on people with pride? So it is said: treat the many with harmony, and all within the four seas are brothers; swell with pride, and even the people in your boat become an enemy state. Merchants should look at this and examine themselves.

### 87. [9/10] 第十二類 在船騙 Category 12 · On-the-Boat Swindles — 船载家人行李逃 (cat-12-s00) `[body]`

- **Chinese Source**:
  > 倪典史不得已，复入京城，向乡知借觅盘缠，欲往在京衙门告捕船贼。同选乡友阻之曰：“凡讨船须在捕头写定，其舵公有姓名可查，方保稳当。若自向江头讨船，彼此不相识，来历无可查，安得不致失误？且江边常有贼船，舵公伪装商贾，打听某船有好货，多致江中劫掠者，皆是在头查访去。若不识者，误上他船，虽主人亦同被害，何况载走一仆乎！今你赴任有限期，岂能在此久待？船贼又无名姓踪影，虽告何从追捕？不如罢休。”倪典史依劝，复在京中再置切要之物，急往赴任也。此不识写船而致误者，故述为舟行之戒。
- **Current English**:
  > Assistant Magistrate Ni had no choice but to go back into the capital and borrow travel money from fellow provincials, intending to file a charge at a capital yamen and have the boat thieves caught. Fellow appointees from his home region stopped him, saying: “Whenever you hire a boat you should book it at the registered wharf, so the helmsman has a name that can be looked up; only then is it safe. If you hire a boat yourself at the river’s edge, neither side knows the other, and there is no background to check—how can you not come to grief? Besides, there are often pirate boats along the river. Their helmsmen disguise themselves as merchants, find out which boat has good cargo, and many then rob it midstream. All of this can be found out by checking at the office first. If you do not know this and board their boat by mistake, even the master himself may be harmed—how much more when they carry off a single servant! You have a deadline to take up your post; how can you wait here long? The boat thieves have no names and leave no trail; even if you file a charge, where would you hunt them? Better drop it.” Assistant Magistrate Ni took the advice, bought the most needful things again in the capital, and hurried off to his post. This is a case of not knowing how to book a boat, and so coming to grief; it is recorded as a warning for those who travel by water.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'fellow appointees' for '乡友' is slightly imprecise; 'fellow provincials' or 'old friends from the home region' better captures the nuance of shared origin rather than official rank.

### 88. [9/10] 第十二類 在船騙 Category 12 · On-the-Boat Swindles — 娶妾在船夜被拐 (cat-12-s01) `[body]`

- **Chinese Source**:
  > 次早，贾知县醒来，不见危氏，心甚疑怪。再差一家人，往危老家报。危家惊异，疑是船中乖争，致逼投水，即赴府具状，告苛逼溺命事。家人数日回报，贾知县欲赴任期，不能久待，亦不往诉辨，自径投任去。三年后，入京朝觐，差家人送些少仪物与危老，见其家有一少妇，抱一幼子，宛似危氏，驰归报主。及贾知县打轿往，并不见踪。问昨妇何人，危云：“妻姨之女。”其妻反出来涕泣诘骂，扭问取人，又被骗银十两，方得脱身。此误娶棍女而人财两空，又受尽多少闲气也。
- **Current English**:
  > The next morning Magistrate Jia woke and did not see Miss Wei; he was much puzzled and suspicious. He then sent a servant to report it at Old Wei’s house. The Wei family acted startled, and suspected a quarrel on the boat had driven her to throw herself in the water. They went at once to the prefecture and filed a charge of cruelty driving a person to drown. After several days the servant came back to report. Magistrate Jia had a deadline to take up his post and could not wait long; he did not go to plead his side, but went straight to his post. Three years later he went to the capital for the imperial audience, and sent a servant with a few ceremonial gifts to Old Wei. The servant saw that the house had a young woman holding a small child, the very image of Miss Wei, and galloped back to tell his master. When Magistrate Jia went in a sedan, there was no trace of her. He asked who yesterday’s woman was; Wei said: “My wife’s sister’s daughter.” Wei’s wife then came out weeping, questioning and cursing, grabbing him and demanding he produce the person; he was cheated of another ten taels before he could get away. This is a case of wrongly taking a crook’s daughter: he lost both the woman and the money, and swallowed no small amount of idle abuse besides.

  - 'Galloped back' for '驰归' is a good dynamic equivalent, though 'rushed back' might be safer if the servant was on foot (though 'galloped' fits the urgency well).

### 89. [9/10] 第十二類 在船騙 Category 12 · On-the-Boat Swindles — 买铜物被艄谋死 (cat-12-s02) `[body]`

- **Chinese Source**:
  > 林子达与四维分袂之后，已三个月矣，始到家中往拜四维。维父曰：“小儿出门，尚未归。”达曰：“差矣！”三月前，我在江西水口，同他在牙人陈四店相会。我与牙人同他去讨船，说他在福建买铜货，以竹箱装作三担，竟归来本处发脱。莫非舵公行歹意乎？”言未毕，父母妻子举家大哭。达曰：“且勿哭，倘在途中发卖也未可知。或舵公行歹意，必以铜物卖各处。试往各店踪迹铜物，问其来历，便见明白。纵铜物无踪，再到水口牙人陈四家，寻舵公问之，必得下落。”维父然之，叫次子罗逵随达去访。
- **Current English**:
  > It was already three months after Lin Zida had parted from Siwei when Zida reached home and went to call on Siwei. Siwei’s father said: “My son went out and has not yet come back.” Zida said: “That cannot be right! Three months ago I met him at broker Chen Si’s shop at Shuikou in Jiangxi. I went with the broker to hire a boat for him. He said he had bought copper goods in Fujian, packed in bamboo chests as three loads, and was coming straight home to this place to sell them. Can it be the helmsman meant harm?” Before he had finished, father, mother, wife, and the whole house burst into tears. Zida said: “Do not cry yet. He may still be selling along the way, for all we know. Or if the helmsman meant harm, he would have to sell the copper in various places. Try tracing the copper in the shops, ask where it came from, and the matter will be clear. Even if the copper leaves no trace, go on to broker Chen Si’s at Shuikou, find the helmsman and question him, and you will surely get to the bottom of it.” Siwei’s father agreed, and told his second son Luo Kui to go with Zida to inquire.

### 93. [9/10] 第八類 露財騙 Category 8 · Displaying Wealth — 诈称公子盗商银 (cat-08-s00) `[note]`

- **Chinese Source**:
  > 噫：棍之设机巧矣！一路妆作公子，商人犹知防之。至拜县丞，而县丞回拜送赆，孰不以为真公子也？又先设机以请商人，则商人备礼以答敬，亦理所必然也。乃故缠饮，困其主仆，则乘夜行窃易矣。故曰其设机最巧也。使栋更能慎防一夜，则棍奸无所施。故慎始不如慎终，日乾更继以夕惕，斯可万无一失。不然，抱瓮汲井，几至井口而败其瓮，与不慎何异？吾愿为商者，处终如谨始可也。
- **Current English**:
  > Note: Ah, how cleverly this swindler laid his trap! All along the road he played the young lord, and the merchant still knew to be on guard. But once he called on the assistant magistrate, and the magistrate returned the call and sent a gift, who would not have taken him for a genuine young lord? And since the trap had been laid by playing host first, the merchant’s return of the courtesy was only natural. Then he deliberately dragged out the drinking to wear down master and servants, and stealing in the night was easy. That is why I call his trap the cleverest kind. Had Dong kept his guard up just one more night, the swindler’s scheme would have had nowhere to bite. So vigilance at the start counts for less than vigilance at the end: diligent by day and watchful by night—only then is nothing left to chance. Otherwise it is like hauling a jar of water up from a well and smashing it just as it reaches the well’s mouth—how is that different from never being careful at all? My wish for merchants is this: be as careful at the end as you were at the beginning.

'Whose house is this?' he asked. His servant Lailu said, 'This whole row is licensed pleasure houses.' Dongxi marched straight in the gate, pointing and cursing. Mama Xun Rong came out in alarm and asked what had happened; learning that her girl Xun Qingyun had carelessly thrown the water on him, she called the girl out at once and beat her with a stick again and again. Qingyun begged for mercy and pleaded for someone to rescue her, but Dongxi paid no heed. Mama Rong said, 'You had better bring a fresh set of clothes for the master to change into, then go to him, kowtow, and beg his pardon—and keep him here to make proper amends, so this does not bring trouble on us later.'

  > 江西有陳姓慶名者，常販馬，徃南京承恩寺前，三山街賣。時有一疋銀合好馬，價約值四十金。忽有一棍，擎好傘，穿色衣，翩然而來，佇立瞻顧，不忍舍去。遂問曰：「此馬價賣幾許？」慶曰：「四十兩。」棍曰：「我買，但要歸家作契對銀。」慶問：「何住？」棍曰：「居洪武門。」棍遂騎銀合馬徃，慶亦騎馬随後。行至半途，棍見一緞鋪，即下馬放傘於酒坊邊，囑慶曰：「代看住，待我買緞幾疋，少頃與你同歸。」慶忖：「此人想是富翁，馬諒買得成矣。」棍入緞鋪，故意與之爭價。待緞客以不識價責之，遂佯曰：「我把與一相知者看，即來還價何如？」緞客曰：「有此好物，憑伊與人看，但不可遠去。」棍曰：「我有馬與夥在，更何慮乎？」将緞拿過手，出門便迯去。緞客見馬與夥尚在，心中安然。慶待至午，杳不見來，意必棍徒也。遂舍其傘，騎銀合，又牵一馬囬店。緞客忙奔前，扯住慶曰：「你夥拿吾緞去，你将焉徃？」慶曰：「何人是我夥？」緞客曰：「適間與你同騎馬來者。你何佯推？定要問你取。」慶曰：「那人不知何方鬼，祇是問我買馬，令我同到他家接銀，故與之同來矣。他說在你店買緞，少頃與我同去。我待乆不見來，故騎自馬囬店。你何得妄纏我乎？」緞客曰：「若不是你夥，何�纏我乎？」緞客曰：「若不是你夥，何呌你看傘與馬？我因見你與馬在，始以緞與他。你何通同粧套，脫我緞去？」二人爭辯不伏，扭在應天府理論。緞客以前情直告。慶訴曰：「慶籍江西，販馬為主，常在三山街，翁春店發賣，何嘗作棍？緣遇一人，問我買馬，必要到他家還銀，是以同行。彼中途下馬，在他店拿緞迯去，我亦不知，怎說我是棍之夥？」府尹曰：「不必言，拘店家來問，即見明白。」其店家曰：「慶常販馬，安歇吾家，乃老實本分人也。」緞客曰：「既是老實人，緣何代那棍看傘與馬？此我明白聽見，况他應諾。」慶曰：「呌我看傘，多因為他買馬故也，豈與之同夥？」府尹曰：「那人去，傘亦拿去否？」緞客曰：「未曾拿去。」府尹曰：「此真是棍了。欲脫你緞，故託買馬，以陳慶為質。以他人之馬，賺你之緞，是假道滅虢術也。此你自遭騙，何可罪慶？」各逐出免供。
- **Current English**:
  > In Jiangxi there was a man surnamed Chen, given name Qing, who regularly dealt in horses, taking them to Nanjing to sell in front of Cheng'en Temple on Sanshan Street. At the time he had a fine silver-gray horse worth about forty taels. Suddenly a con man appeared, holding a fine umbrella and wearing showy clothes, sauntering up with an easy air. He stood there gazing at the horse, reluctant to tear himself away. Then he asked, "How much are you asking for this horse?" Qing said, "Forty taels." The con man said, "I'll buy it, but I have to go back home to draw up the deed and weigh out the silver." Qing asked, "Where do you live?" The con man said, "By Hongwu Gate." So the con man rode off on the silver-gray horse, with Qing following behind on another horse. Halfway there, the con man saw a silk shop. He dismounted, set his umbrella down beside a tavern, and told Qing, "Keep an eye on these for me while I buy a few bolts of silk; in a moment we'll go on together." Qing thought, "This man must be rich—the horse sale is as good as done." The con man went into the silk shop and deliberately haggled over the price. When the silk dealer scolded him for not knowing what the goods were worth, he pretended to relent: "Let me take it to a friend who knows the goods, and I'll come right back with an offer. How about that?" The silk dealer said, "With goods this fine, feel free to show them around—just don't go far." The con man said, "I have a horse and a partner right here—what is there to worry about?" He took the silk in hand, walked out the door, and ran off. The silk dealer, seeing the horse and the partner still there, felt perfectly at ease. Qing waited until noon; the man never came back, and Qing concluded he must be a swindler. So he left the umbrella, mounted the silver-gray, and led his other horse back toward the inn. The silk dealer rushed up and grabbed him: "Your partner made off with my silk—where do you think you're going?" Qing said, "What partner of mine?" The silk dealer said, "The man who rode here with you just now. Why play dumb? I'm taking it out of you." Qing said, "I don't know what devil that man was. He only asked to buy my horse and told me to come to his house to collect the silver, so I came along. He said he was buying silk at your shop and would rejoin me in a moment. I waited a long time and he never came, so I rode my own horse back to the inn. How can you harass me over nothing?" The silk dealer said, "If he wasn't your partner, why did he have you watch his umbrella and horse? I only handed him the silk because I saw you and the horse standing here. How can you two work a setup together and cheat me out of my silk?" The two of them argued, neither giving in, and hauled each other to the Yingtian prefectural office to have the case judged. The silk dealer told the whole story plainly. Qing pleaded, "I am registered in Jiangxi and deal in horses for a living. I regularly sell at Sanshan Street and put up at Weng Chun's inn. When have I ever been a swindler? I happened to meet a man who wanted to buy my horse and insisted I come to his house for payment, so I went with him. Midway he dismounted, took silk from this man's shop, and ran off—I knew nothing about it. How can you say I was his partner?" The prefect said, "Say no more. Bring in the innkeeper and all will be clear." The innkeeper said, "Qing regularly deals in horses and lodges at my place. He is an honest, law-abiding man." The silk dealer said, "If he's so honest, why did he watch the umbrella and horse for that swindler? I heard it plainly, and he agreed to it." Qing said, "He asked me to watch the umbrella because he was buying my horse—how does that make me his partner?" The prefect asked, "When the man left, did he take the umbrella?" The silk dealer said, "He did not." The prefect said, "Then he truly was a swindler. He wanted to make off with your silk, so he pretended to buy a horse and used Chen Qing as a pledge. Using another man's horse to trick you out of your silk—that is the 'borrow a path to destroy Guo' trick, using one thing as a pretext to take another. You fell for the swindle yourself; how can you blame Qing?" He dismissed them both without further testimony.

### 96. [9/10] 第一類 脫剝騙 Category 1 · Making Off with Goods — 先寄銀而後拐迯 (cat-01-s01) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 通州有姓蘇名廣者，同一子販松江梭布，徃福建賣。布銀入手，囬至半途，遇一人姓紀名勝，自稱同府異縣，鄉語相同，亦在福建賣布而歸。勝乃雛家，途中認廣為親鄉里，見廣財本更多，乃以己銀貳拾餘兩寄藏於蘇廣箱内。一路小心代劳，渾如同伴。後至日乆，勝見利而生姦。一夜，佯稱瀉病，連起開門出去数次。不知廣乃老客也，見其開門徃返，疑彼有詐謀；且其來歷不明，「彼雖有銀貳拾餘兩寄我箱内，今夜似有歹意。」乗其出，即潛起來，将己銀與紀勝銀并實落衣物另藏别包袱，置在己身邊；仍以舊衣被包数片磚石放在原箱内，佯作熟睡。勝察廣父子都睡去，将廣銀箱夤夜挑走。廣在床聽勝動静，出門不歸，曰：「此果棍也，非我，險遭此脫迯矣。」次日，廣起，故驚訝勝竊他銀本，将店主扭打，說他通同将我銀偷去。其子弗知父之謀，尤怒歐不已。父密謂曰：「此事我已如此如此。」方止。早飯後，廣曰：「我徃縣告，若捕得那棍，你來作證；不然，定要問你取矣。」廣知勝反中己術，徑從小路潛歸。勝自幸竊得廣銀，茫茫然行至午，路将百里。開其箱，内乃磚石、舊衣也。頓足大恨，復囬原店。却被店主扭打一場，大罵曰：「這賊！你偷人銀，致我被累！」将绳繫頸，欲要送官。隻得吐出真情，叩頭懇免。時勝與廣已隔数日程途，追之不及，徒自悔恨而已。
- **Current English**:
  > In Tongzhou there was a man named Su Guang who, together with his son, dealt in Songjiang loom-woven cloth, taking it to Fujian to sell. With the silver from the cloth in hand, they were on their way back when they met a man named Ji Sheng, who said he was from the same prefecture but a different county—he spoke the same dialect—and was likewise returning from selling cloth in Fujian. Sheng was a green hand. On the road he claimed Guang as a fellow townsman, and seeing that Guang had the larger capital, he deposited twenty-odd taels of his own silver for safekeeping in Su Guang's chest. All along the way he was attentive and took on the chores, just like a traveling companion. But as the days wore on, Sheng saw the profit to be had and his mind turned crooked. One night he faked an upset stomach, getting up and opening the door to go out several times. He did not know that Guang was a seasoned traveler. Watching him open the door and go back and forth, Guang suspected a scheme; besides, the man's background was unclear. "True, he has twenty-odd taels deposited in my chest, but tonight he seems to be up to no good." Taking advantage of one of Sheng's trips out, Guang quietly got up, took his own silver together with Ji Sheng's silver and all the decent clothes and goods, hid them in another bundle, and kept it by his side. Then he wrapped several bricks and stones in the old clothes and bedding, put them back in the original chest, and pretended to be fast asleep. Sheng, seeing that Guang and his son were both asleep, shouldered Guang's silver chest and carried it off in the dead of night. In bed, Guang listened to Sheng's movements, and when Sheng went out and did not come back, he said, "So he really is a swindler. If it weren't for me, we'd nearly have been cleaned out." The next morning Guang got up and feigned shock that Sheng had stolen his capital; he seized the innkeeper and started beating him, accusing him of colluding to steal the silver. His son, not knowing his father's plan, was even more enraged and pummeled the man without letup. The father secretly told him, "I've already taken care of it, so-and-so." Only then did the son stop. After breakfast, Guang said, "I'm going to the county yamen to file a complaint. If they catch that swindler, you'll testify; otherwise I'll take it out of you." Guang knew that Sheng had fallen into his own trap, and he slipped straight home by a side road. Sheng, congratulating himself on stealing Guang's silver, trudged on in a daze until noon, nearly a hundred li down the road. He opened the chest: inside were bricks, stones, and old clothes. Stamping his feet in fury, he went back to the inn—where the innkeeper seized him and beat him, cursing, "You thief! You stole a man's silver and got me blamed for it!" He tied a rope around Sheng's neck and meant to haul him before the magistrate. Sheng had no choice but to spit out the whole truth and kowtow, begging to be spared. By then he and Guang were already several days' journey apart; there was no catching him, and Sheng could only stew in his own regret.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'loom-woven cloth' for 梭布 is slightly redundant; 'Songjiang broadcloth' or simply 'Songjiang cloth' is more idiomatic in English historical contexts.

### 98. [9/10] 第一類 脫剝騙 Category 1 · Making Off with Goods — 明騙販猪 (cat-01-s02) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 福建建陽人鄧招寶者，常以挑販為生。一日，販小猪四隻，徃崇安、大安去賣。行至馬安嶺上，遇一棍問他買猪。寶意此山徑僻嶺，無人徃來，人家又遠，何此人在路上買猪？疑之，因問其何住。棍曰：「既前馬安坪也。」寶曰：「既要買，我同你家去。」棍曰：「我要徃縣。你拿出與我看，若合吾意，議定價方好囬家秤銀；不然，恐阻程途矣。」此棍言之近理，寶即然之，遂拿一猪與看。棍接過手，拿住猪尾放地上細看，乃故放手，致猪便走。佯作驚恐状曰：「差矣，差矣！」即忙趕捉——不知趕之正驱之也。寶見猪遠走，猛心奔前追捉，豈知已墮其術也。棍見寶趕猪，約離籠二三百步，即旋於籠内拿一猪在手，又踢倒二籠，猪俱迯出，大聲曰：「多謝你！慢慢尋！」寶欲趕棍，三猪出籠迯走，恐因此而失彼；况棍走遠難追，但呪罵一場。幸得三猪成聚，收拾入籠，抱恨而去。
- **Current English**:
  > Deng Zhaobao of Jianyang, Fujian, made his living as a porter-peddler. One day he was carrying four piglets to sell in Chong'an and Da'an. On Ma'an Ridge he met a con man who asked about buying pigs. Bao thought: this is a lonely mountain path, no one comes through, and the houses are far away—why would this man be buying pigs on the road? Suspicious, he asked where the man lived. The con man said, "Just ahead, at Ma'anping." Bao said, "If you mean to buy, I'll go home with you." The con man said, "I'm on my way to the county seat. Take one out for me to look at. If it suits me, we'll settle on a price and then I'll go home to weigh out the silver; otherwise I'm afraid it will delay my trip." The con man sounded reasonable, so Bao agreed and took out a pig to show him. The con man took it, held it by the tail, and set it on the ground to examine closely—then deliberately let go, and the pig bolted. Feigning alarm, he cried, "Oh no, oh no!" and rushed after it—though his chasing was really driving it farther away. Bao saw the pig running off and dashed forward with all his might to catch it, never realizing he had already fallen into the trap. When the con man saw Bao chasing the pig some two or three hundred paces from the cages, he turned back, grabbed a pig from a cage, kicked over the other two cages so the pigs all escaped, and shouted, "Much obliged! Take your time looking!" Bao wanted to chase the con man, but three pigs had bolted from the cages, and he feared losing them while chasing him; besides, the con man had gotten too far to catch. He could only curse him roundly. Luckily the three pigs bunched together; he gathered them back into the cages and went off nursing his grievance.
- **Identified Issues**:

  > Deng Zhaobao of Jianyang, Fujian, made his living as a peddler. One day he carried four piglets to sell in Chong'an and Da'an. On Ma'an Ridge he met a con man who asked to buy pigs. Bao thought: this is a lonely mountain path, no one comes through, and the houses are far away—why would this man be buying pigs on the road? Suspicious, he asked where the man lived. The con man said, "Just ahead, at Ma'anping." Bao said, "If you mean to buy, I'll go home with you." The con man said, "I'm on my way to the county seat. Take one out for me to look at. If it suits me, we'll settle on a price and then I'll go home to weigh out the silver; otherwise I'm afraid it will delay my trip." The con man sounded reasonable, so Bao agreed and took out a pig to show him. The con man took it, held it by the tail, set it on the ground to examine closely—then deliberately let go, and the pig bolted. Feigning alarm, he cried, "Wrong! Wrong!" and rushed after it—though his chasing was really driving it farther away. Bao saw the pig running off and dashed forward with all his might to catch it, never realizing he had already fallen into the trap. When the con man saw Bao chasing the pig some two or three hundred paces from the cages, he turned back, grabbed a pig from a cage, kicked over the other two cages so the pigs all escaped, and shouted, "Much obliged! Take your time looking!" Bao wanted to chase the con man, but three pigs had bolted from the cages, and he feared losing them while chasing him; besides, the con man had gotten too far to catch. He could only curse him roundly. Luckily the three pigs bunched together; he gathered them back into the cages and went off nursing his grievance.

### 99. [9/10] 第一類 脫剝騙 Category 1 · Making Off with Goods — 明騙販猪 (cat-01-s02) `[note]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 吾觀棍之脫猪也，一邂逅相逢之頃，賊念即生，乃以詭言相哄，致寶深信，所謂「君子可欺以其方」者也。乃始也放猪佯逐，以误其遠趕之於前；繼也擒猪踢籠，以制其不趕之於後：使人明墮其術而不自知。倉卒粧套，抑何譎也！商者鑑此！勿謂暗機隱械，宜為慎防；即明圈顯套，尤當加謹。
- **Current English**:
  > Note: Look how the swindler got away with the pig. In the space of a chance meeting, the thievish thought was born; he fooled Bao with lying words until Bao believed him completely—what is meant by 'a gentleman can be deceived by what seems plausible.' First he turned a pig loose and pretended to chase it, to draw Bao far off in pursuit; then he grabbed a pig and kicked over the cages, to keep Bao from coming after him: he made a man fall openly into the trap without ever knowing it. A setup thrown together on the spot—what cunning! Merchants, take warning from this. Do not think that only hidden snares and secret devices deserve careful guarding; even open traps and brazen setups demand still greater care.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The translation of '豬' as 'pig' is technically correct for the literal object, but in the context of this specific swindle story (where a pig is often used as bait or a decoy), the English reader might miss the nuance that the 'pig' represents the victim's property or the lure itself. However, given the text explicitly says 'got away with the pig', this is acceptable.
  - The phrase 'grabbed a pig and kicked over the cages' slightly simplifies '擒猪踢籠'. While accurate to the action, it loses the slight ambiguity of whether the swindler grabbed the pig *while* kicking the cage or if the kicking was the primary distraction. The current translation is clear enough.
  - The translation of '粧套' as 'setup thrown together on the spot' is excellent and captures the improvisational nature well.

### 101. [9/10] 第一類 脫剝騙 Category 1 · Making Off with Goods — 乗鬧明竊店中布 (cat-01-s04) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 吴勝理，徽州府休寧縣人，在蘇州府開鋪，收買各樣色布，揭行生意最大，四方買者極多，每日有幾十兩銀交易。外開鋪面，裡藏各貨。一日，有幾夥客人凑集買布，皆在内堂作帐對銀。一棍乗其叢雜，亦在鋪呌買布。勝理出於施礼，待茶畢，安頓外鋪少坐。勝理復入内，與前客對銀。其棍蓦其鋪無守者，故近門邊，詐拱揖相辭状，遂近鋪邊拿布一捆，拖在肩上，缓步行去。雖對鋪者，亦不覺其盗。後内堂诸商交易畢，勝理送客出外，忽不見鋪上布，問對門店人曰：「我鋪裡一捆布是何人拿去？」對門店人曰：「你�鋪裡一捆布是何人拿去？」對門店人曰：「你遆間後來那客人，與你拱手作辭，方拖布去，眾皆見之，你何佯失布？」勝理曰：「因内忙，故安他在外鋪坐，候前客事畢，然後與他作帐。何曾賣布與他？」鄰人訝曰：「狡哉，此棍！彼佯拱手相辭，令我辈不敢說他是賊，缓步而行，明白脫去矣！将奈何？」勝理隻得懊恨一場而罷。
- **Current English**:
  > Wu Shengli, a man of Xiuning County in Huizhou Prefecture, ran a shop in Suzhou Prefecture buying up colored cloth of every kind. His wholesale business was the biggest around, buyers came from all quarters, and every day tens of taels of silver changed hands. The shop front opened to the street, and the goods were stored inside. One day several parties of customers crowded in to buy cloth, all of them in the inner hall settling accounts and weighing out silver. A swindler, taking advantage of the crush, also called out in the shop to buy cloth. Shengli came out to greet him politely, served him tea, and settled him in the outer shop to sit a while. Then Shengli went back inside to weigh out silver with the earlier customers. The swindler, seeing the shop unwatched, moved toward the doorway and pretended to cup his hands in a gesture of farewell; then he went to the edge of the shop, picked up a bundle of cloth, slung it over his shoulder, and strolled slowly away. Even the shop across the street did not see it as theft. Later, when the merchants in the inner hall had finished their dealings, Shengli saw his customers out—and suddenly found the bundle of cloth gone from the shop. He asked the people in the shop opposite, "Who took a bundle of cloth from my shop?" The people opposite said, "That customer who came later just now cupped his hands to take leave of you, then hauled the cloth off. We all saw it—why pretend you've lost cloth?" Shengli said, "Because I was busy inside, I seated him in the outer shop to wait until the earlier customers were done, and then I would settle accounts with him. When did I ever sell him cloth?" The neighbors marveled, "What a cunning swindler! He pretended to cup his hands in farewell so none of us dared call him a thief, and he strolled off and walked clean away with it in broad daylight. What can be done?" Shengli could only nurse his chagrin and let it go.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - Minor typo in the source text transcription ('你鋪' and '你遆間') which the translator correctly inferred as '你鋪裡' (your shop) and '你這間' (this shop/room), but the translation could explicitly note the correction if this were a critical edition.

### 102. [9/10] 第一類 脫剝騙 Category 1 · Making Off with Goods — 乗鬧明竊店中布 (cat-01-s04) `[note]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 按：棍之竊斯布也，初須乗其叢雜，入其店中，尚未定其騙局之所出也。至勝理待其茶而安之外鋪少坐，左顧右盼而姦謀遂决矣。故拱揖而辭，而明拖其布，如荆州之暗袭，不甚費力，真可謂高手矣。在勝理，店積貨物，宜不離看守，方可保無虞。關防不密，安知無棍徒混入行姦乎？待布既失而後扼腕，何益哉？大凡坐鋪者，當知此而謹慎之可也。
- **Current English**:
  > Note: When the swindler stole this cloth, he first had to take advantage of the crowd to get into the shop; he had not yet settled on how the swindle would play out. Only when Shengli served him tea and sat him down in the outer shop did he glance left and right—and then the crooked plan was fixed. So he cupped his hands in farewell and openly dragged off the cloth: like the sneak attack that took Jingzhou, it cost him hardly any effort. A real master of the game. As for Shengli, with goods piled up in the shop, he should never have left them unwatched; only so could he be sure of their safety. If the guard is not tight, how can you know no swindler will slip in and work his mischief? To wring your hands after the cloth is already lost—what good is that? All who keep shop should take this to heart and be careful.
- **Identified Issues**:

  - The translation of '竊斯布也' as 'stole this cloth' is accurate but could be slightly more idiomatic as 'swindled this cloth' to align with the book's theme of deception, though 'stole' is acceptable in context.
- **Recommended Revision**:

Only when Shengli served him tea and sat him down in the outer shop did he glance left and right—and then the crooked plan was fixed. So he cupped his hands in farewell and brazenly made off with the cloth: like the sneak attack that took Jingzhou, it cost him hardly any effort. A real master of the game. As for Shengli, with goods piled up in the shop, he should never have left them unwatched; only so could he be sure of their safety. If the guard is not tight, how can you know no swindler will slip in and work his mischief? To wring your hands after the cloth is already lost—what good is that? All who keep shop should take this to heart and be careful.

### 103. [9/10] 第一類 脫剝騙 Category 1 · Making Off with Goods — 詐稱偷鹅脫青布 (cat-01-s05) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 有一大鋪，布匹極多，交易叢雜，隻自己一人看店。其店之對門人，養一圈鹅，鸣聲嘈雜。開鋪者惡其聒耳，尝曰：「此惡物何無盗之者？與我耳頭得沉静些。」忽棍聞之。一日，乗其店中閑寂，遂入店拱手，以手按柜頭一捆青布，輕輕言曰：「不敢相瞒，我實是一小偷，愛得對門店下一隻鹅吃，隻大街面難下手。我有一小術，祇要一个人贊成。」店主曰：「如何贊成？」小偷曰：「我在這邊問曰：『可拿去否？』汝在内高聲應曰：『可。』又再問曰：『我真拿去？』汝再應曰：『說定了，任從拿去。』我便去拿，方掩得路人耳目。託你贊成，後日你家不須閉門，亦無賊入矣。但你須在内去，莫得竊視，視則法不灵。你直聽鹅聲息，我事方畢，你可出來。」店主然之。小偷高聲問曰：「我拿去否？」内高聲應曰：「憑你拿去。」又再高聲問曰：「我真拿去？」内又高聲應曰：「說定了，任你拿去。」兩旁店人皆聞其問答之語，小偷遂負其柜上一捆青布而去——人以為借去也。其店主在内，聽得鹅聲，不敢出來。其盗布者匆匆行之乆矣。待之多時，鹅聲不绝。其店主恐店内乆無人守，隻得外出。看鹅尚在，自己柜頭反失一捆青布。顧問兩旁店曰：「遆才誰上我店，拿我一捆布去？」左右店皆答曰：「是那个問你買的，你再三應聲，呌他祇管拿去。今拿去已乆矣。」店主抚心自悔曰：「我明被此人騙了！祇是自己皆死，說不得也。」事乆，眾鄰覺之，始笑此人之痴，而深服此棍賊之高手矣。
- **Current English**:
  > There was a big shop with bolts of cloth in great number and a constant press of business, and only the owner himself to mind the store. The people across the street kept a pen of geese whose honking was a constant racket. The shopkeeper hated the din and once said, "Why has no one stolen those wretched creatures? Then my ears could have some peace." A swindler happened to hear this. One day, finding the shop idle and quiet, he went in, cupped his hands, laid a hand on a bundle of blue cloth on the counter, and said softly, "I won't hide it from you—I'm really a petty thief. I'd love to make a meal of one of the geese at the shop across the way, but it's a busy street and hard to make a move. I have a little trick; I just need one person to play along." The shopkeeper said, "Play along how?" The thief said, "I'll stand here and ask, 'May I take it?' You answer loudly from inside, 'Yes.' I'll ask again, 'I'm really taking it?' You answer again, 'It's settled—take it as you please.' Then I'll go take it, and that will cover the eyes and ears of people in the street. Do me this favor, and afterward your house won't need to shut its door—no thief will ever enter. But you must stay inside; don't peek—if you look, the method won't work. Listen until the goose sounds stop; when my business is done, you can come out." The shopkeeper agreed. The thief asked loudly, "May I take it?" From inside came the loud answer, "Take it as you please." He asked again, louder, "I'm really taking it?" From inside again, "It's settled—take it as you please." The shops on both sides all heard this exchange. The thief then shouldered the bundle of blue cloth from the counter and walked off—people assumed it was a loan. Inside, the shopkeeper heard the geese still honking and dared not come out. The cloth thief had long since hurried away. He waited a good while; the honking never stopped. Fearing the shop had been left unwatched too long, he had to come out. He looked: the geese were still there—and a bundle of blue cloth was gone from his own counter. He turned to the neighboring shops: "Who was it just now came into my shop and took a bundle of my cloth?" The shops on either side all answered, "It was the one who asked to buy from you. You answered him again and again, telling him to go ahead and take it. He's been gone a long time." The shopkeeper beat his breast in remorse: "That man swindled me in broad daylight! And it's all my own doing—I can't even tell anyone." In time the neighbors figured it out; they laughed at the man's folly and deeply admired the swindler-thief's masterly skill.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'people assumed it was a loan' slightly softens the original implication of '借去' (borrowed/taken as if borrowed), which in this context implies a public misunderstanding that the transaction was legitimate rather than a theft. 'Borrowed' alone would be more precise.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > There was a big shop with bolts of cloth in great number and a constant press of business, and only the owner himself to mind the store. The people across the street kept a pen of geese whose honking was a constant racket. The shopkeeper hated the din and once said, "Why has no one stolen those wretched creatures? Then my ears could have some peace." A swindler happened to hear this. One day, finding the shop idle and quiet, he went in, cupped his hands, laid a hand on a bundle of blue cloth on the counter, and said softly, "I won't hide it from you—I'm really a petty thief. I'd love to make a meal of one of the geese at the shop across the way, but it's a busy street and hard to make a move. I have a little trick; I just need one person to play along." The shopkeeper said, "Play along how?" The thief said, "I'll stand here and ask, 'May I take it?' You answer loudly from inside, 'Yes.' I'll ask again, 'I'm really taking it?' You answer again, 'It's settled—take it as you please.' Then I'll go take it, and that will cover the eyes and ears of people in the street. Do me this favor, and afterward your house won't need to shut its door—no thief will ever enter. But you must stay inside; don't peek—if you look, the method won't work. Listen until the goose sounds stop; when my business is done, you can come out." The shopkeeper agreed. The thief asked loudly, "May I take it?" From inside came the loud answer, "Take it as you please." He asked again, louder, "I'm really taking it?" From inside again, "It's settled—take it as you please." The shops on both sides all heard this exchange. The thief then shouldered the bundle of blue cloth from the counter and walked off—people thought he had merely borrowed it. Inside, the shopkeeper heard the geese still honking and dared not come out. The cloth thief had long since hurried away. He waited a good while; the honking never stopped. Fearing the shop had been left unwatched too long, he had to come out. He looked: the geese were still there—and a bundle of blue cloth was gone from his own counter. He turned to the neighboring shops: "Who was it just now came into my shop and took a bundle of my cloth?" The shops on either side all answered, "It was the one who asked to buy from you. You answered him again and again, telling him to go ahead and take it. He's been gone a long time." The shopkeeper beat his breast in remorse: "That man swindled me in broad daylight! And it's all my own doing—I can't even tell anyone." In time the neighbors figured it out; they laughed at the man's folly and deeply admired the swindler-thief's masterly skill.

### 104. [9/10] 第一類 脫剝騙 Category 1 · Making Off with Goods — 借他人屋脫客布 (cat-01-s06) `[note]`

  > 按：布入人家賣，又飲人家之茶，則買主似有着落矣，誰不肯以布與之？詎料此棍借其屋，賺其茶，以為脫布之媒；又還其銀，止爭銀色而許換，誰知防之？今後交易，惟兩相交付。彼雖許換銀，布隻抱去，明日重來，則無受脫之事矣。
- **Current English**:

Who would have thought this swindler borrowed the house and cadged the tea as a means to make off with the cloth—and even paid silver, quibbling only over its purity and promising to exchange it? Who would know to be on guard? From now on, in any deal, let both sides hand over on the spot. Even if he promises to exchange the silver, just carry the cloth back with you and return tomorrow—then you'll never be cheated out of your goods.

Who would have thought this swindler used the house and cadged the tea as a means to make off with the cloth—and even paid silver, quibbling only over its purity and promising to exchange it? Who would know to be on guard? From now on, in any deal, let both sides hand over on the spot. Even if he promises to exchange the silver, just carry the cloth back with you and return tomorrow—then you'll never be cheated out of your goods.

### 106. [9/10] 第一類 脫剝騙 Category 1 · Making Off with Goods — 詐匠修換錢桌厨 (cat-01-s07) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 建寧府凡換錢者，皆以一椅一桌厨列於街上，置錢於桌，以待人換。午則歸家食飯，晚則收起錢，以桌厨寄附近人家。明日復然。有一人桌厨内約積有錢五六千，其桌破坏一角。旁有一棍，看此破桌厨内多錢，心生一計。待此人起身食午，即粧做一木匠，以手巾缚腰，插一利斧於旁，手拿六尺，将此桌厨横量直量一次，高聲自說自應曰：「這樣破东西，當做一新的來換，反呌我修补，怎么修得？真是吝啬的人！」自說了一場。一手拿六尺，将桌厨錢輕輕侧倾作一邊，将桌厨負在無人處，以斧砍開，取錢而迯。時旁人都道是換錢的呌木匠拿去修，那料大眾人群中，有棍敢脫此也。乃午後，換錢者到，問旁人曰：「我桌厨哪裡去？」眾合答曰：「你呌木匠拿去修，匠還說你吝啬，何不再做新的，乃修此破物？彼已負去修矣。」換錢者曰：「我并未呌匠來，此是光棍脫去。」急沿途而访問，見空僻處桌厨剖破，錢無一文，怅恨而歸。
- **Current English**:
  > In Jianning Prefecture, the money-changers all set out a chair and a table-cabinet in the street, placed their coins on the table, and waited for people to come exchange. At noon they went home to eat; in the evening they gathered up the coins and left the table-cabinet with a household nearby. The next day, the same again. One changer had five or six thousand cash stored up in his table-cabinet, and the table had a broken corner. A swindler nearby, seeing how much money sat in that broken table-cabinet, hit on a plan. When the man got up to go eat lunch, the swindler dressed himself up as a carpenter—a towel tied around his waist, a sharp axe stuck at his side, a six-foot rule in his hand. He measured the table-cabinet lengthwise and crosswise, then said loudly, answering himself, "A thing this broken—they ought to have a new one made, and instead they tell me to patch it. How can this be patched? What a stingy man!" On and on he talked to himself. Then, rule in one hand, he gently tipped the coins in the cabinet over to one side, hoisted the table-cabinet onto his back, carried it to a deserted spot, chopped it open with the axe, took the coins, and ran. The bystanders all assumed the money-changer had called a carpenter to take it away for repair; who would have guessed that in the middle of a crowd a swindler would dare walk off with it? That afternoon the money-changer returned and asked the bystanders, "Where has my table-cabinet gone?" They all answered, "You had a carpenter take it for repair. The carpenter even said you were stingy—why not have a new one made instead of fixing that broken thing? He's already carried it off to fix." The money-changer said, "I never called any carpenter. A sharp has walked off with it." He hurried along the road asking after it, and found the table-cabinet split open in a deserted spot, not a single cash left. He went home sick with regret.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - Minor stylistic deviation: 'table-cabinet' is a slightly awkward calque for '厨' (a storage chest/cupboard), though contextually clear. 'Chest' or 'cupboard' would be more natural.
  - The phrase 'sick with regret' at the end is a slight interpretive expansion of '怅恨而歸' (went home in sorrow and resentment); 'filled with regret' or 'sorrowful' might be tighter.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > In Jianning Prefecture, all money-changers set out a chair and a cupboard on the street, placed their coins on the table, and waited for people to exchange them. At noon they went home to eat; in the evening they gathered up the coins and left the cupboard with a nearby household. The next day, the same again. One changer had five or six thousand cash stored in his cupboard, and the cupboard had a broken corner. A swindler nearby, seeing how much money sat in that broken cupboard, hit on a plan. When the man got up to go eat lunch, the swindler dressed himself as a carpenter—a towel tied around his waist, a sharp axe tucked at his side, a six-foot rule in his hand. He measured the cupboard lengthwise and crosswise, then said loudly, answering himself, "A thing this broken—they ought to have a new one made, and instead they tell me to patch it. How can this be patched? What a stingy man!" On and on he talked to himself. Then, rule in one hand, he gently tipped the coins in the cupboard over to one side, hoisted the cupboard onto his back, carried it to a deserted spot, chopped it open with the axe, took the coins, and ran. The bystanders all assumed the money-changer had called a carpenter to take it away for repair; who would have guessed that in the middle of a crowd a swindler would dare walk off with it? That afternoon the money-changer returned and asked the bystanders, "Where has my cupboard gone?" They all answered, "You had a carpenter take it for repair. The carpenter even said you were stingy—why not have a new one made instead of fixing that broken thing? He's already carried it off to fix." The money-changer said, "I never called any carpenter. A sharp has walked off with it." He hurried along the road asking after it, and found the cupboard split open in a deserted spot, not a single cash left. He went home filled with regret.

### 107. [9/10] 第一類 脫剝騙 Category 1 · Making Off with Goods — 詐匠修換錢桌厨 (cat-01-s07) `[note]`

- **Chinese Source**:
  > 按：此棍粧匠而來，大舉大動，大志大言，人哪知他是脫？祇匠人修舊物，須在作場内，何須帶斧帶六尺而來？粧為匠便非匠矣。但他人物件，他人為修，何人替他盤詰？此棍所以得行其詐也。然因此以推其餘，凡來歷不明而粧情甚肖者，倍宜加察也。
- **Current English**:
  > Note: This swindler came dressed as a carpenter, all big gestures and big talk—who would have known he was there to walk off with the goods? But a carpenter mending an old piece does the work in his own shop; why would he show up with an axe and a six-foot rule? Dressing up as a carpenter was itself the proof he was no carpenter. Still, it was another man's property and another man's repair job—who was going to cross-examine on his behalf? That is how the swindler got away with his fraud. And from this we can draw the general lesson: whenever a person's background is unclear and the act is all too convincing, watch him twice as carefully.
- **Identified Issues**:

But a carpenter repairing an old item works within his own workshop; why bring an axe and a six-foot rule along? To pose as a carpenter was itself proof he was no carpenter. Still, it was another man's property and another man's repair job—who would cross-examine on his behalf? That is how the swindler got away with his fraud. And from this we can draw the general lesson: whenever a person's background is unclear and their pretense is all too convincing, one must be twice as vigilant.

### 109. [9/10] 第二類 丟包騙 Category 2 · The Dropped-Parcel Swindle — 丟包於路行脫換 (cat-02-s00) `[note]`

  > 按：賢所賺銀，必早被棍覷見，故先偽設銀包套合。一棍在賢之先於荒僻處，俟賢來，投銀包於地。彼必撿之，乃出而欲與之分，令藏彼箱，則與彼銀共一處矣。其後棍妝情哀取，賢自應開箱還之，何自開箱使棍手親取其原包？則棍得以偽包換賢之銀，賢豈知防其脫換哉？故撿銀之時，即以其撿者與前棍均分，勿入箱中，則彼窮於計矣。然二棍亦必於僻處再搶之矣。故客路不在虛得人之有，而在密藏己之有也，斯無所失矣。
- **Current English**:
  > Note: The silver Xian had earned must have been spotted by the swindlers early on, so they rigged up a fake parcel in advance to match it. One of them went ahead of Xian to a deserted spot, waited for him to come, and tossed the parcel on the ground. Xian was sure to pick it up; the man then stepped out and pressed to share it, and had him hide it in his chest — and just like that, it was sitting in the same place as Xian's own silver. When the second swindler played the grieving loser and begged it back, Xian of course had to open the chest and return it — but why did he let the swindler's own hand reach in and take the original parcel? That is what let the crook trade a fake parcel for Xian's silver; how was Xian to know he should guard against the switch? The right move, the moment he picked up the silver, was to split the find with the first swindler on the spot and never let it into his chest — then they would have been out of tricks. Though even then, the two of them would likely have robbed him outright in some lonely place. So for a man traveling the roads, what matters is not picking up windfalls from other people's losses, but keeping what you have well hidden. Do that, and you will lose nothing.
- **Identified Issues**:

Given the context of '箱' (box/chest), using 'chest' as a container is acceptable but could be clearer as 'his box' or 'the chest' to avoid confusion with the body part.

### 112. [9/10] 第三類 換銀騙 Category 3 · Silver-Exchange Swindles — 道士船中換轉金 (cat-03-s01) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 賁監生在南監，期滿將歸，欲換好金數十兩，歸遺妻妾，以將遠敬。同鄉鄧監生阻之曰：「京城換金者，屢被棍以銅脫去。金非急用，何必在此換為？」賁曰：「京城方有好金。若有棍能脫我者，亦服他好手段。」數日內換金十餘兩，皆照金色交易，都是好金。
- **Current English**:
  > Ben, a student of the Imperial Academy, was at the Southern Academy, and with his term finished he was about to head home. He wanted to exchange for several dozen taels of good gold to bring back to his wife and concubines, a token of regard after his long time away. His fellow townsman Deng, also an Academy student, tried to stop him: "People exchanging gold in the capital are forever being cheated by swindlers passing off copper. You don't need the gold urgently—why exchange it here?" Ben said, "Only the capital has good gold. If any swindler can get the better of me, I'll grant he has real skill." Within a few days he had exchanged for more than ten taels of gold, each deal checked against the gold's fineness, and all of it good gold.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'a token of regard after his long time away' is a slight interpretive expansion of '以將遠敬' (to show respect for the long separation/travel), though it captures the spirit well.
- **Recommended Revision**:

With his term finished and about to head home, he wanted to exchange for several dozen taels of good gold to bring back as a gift to his wife and concubines in honor of his long journey. His fellow townsman Deng, also an Academy student, tried to stop him: 'People exchanging gold in the capital are forever being cheated by swindlers who pass off copper. You don't need the gold urgently—why exchange it here?' Ben said, 'Only the capital has good gold. If any swindler can get the better of me, I'll grant he has real skill.' Within a few days he had exchanged for more than ten taels of gold; each deal was checked against the gold's fineness, and all of it was good gold.

### 113. [9/10] 第三類 換銀騙 Category 3 · Silver-Exchange Swindles — 道士船中換轉金 (cat-03-s01) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 後有一後生，以金錠十二兩來換。賁生取看，幾有足色金。問其換數，後生曰：「某鄉官命換的，要作五換。」賁遞與鄧看，曰：「此金可有六換，若五換，價公道矣。」鄧看曰：「果好。可將此金對明收起，勿過他手，然後對銀六十兩還之。」賁依言，先收入此金，然後還其銀。後生不得展轉，只得領銀歸，見其父，云：「兩監生如此關妨，不能再脫出。」父頓足曰：「一家生意在此，把本子送去了，何以為生？速去訪此監生何時歸？」回報：「已討定船，某日刻期登舟矣。」體探已的。
- **Current English**:
  > Later a young man came with a twelve-tael gold ingot to exchange. Ben took it and looked: it was nearly pure gold. He asked the rate, and the young man said, "A country gentleman ordered it exchanged; it has to go at five to one." Ben passed it to Deng: "This gold could fetch six to one; at five, the price is fair." Deng looked and said, "It really is good. Check this gold and put it away first—don't let it pass through his hands again—then weigh out sixty taels of silver to pay him." Ben followed the advice: he took in the gold first, then paid over the silver. The young man had no chance to work a switch and had to take the silver home. He told his father, "The two Academy students were so on their guard that I couldn't pull it off." The father stamped his foot: "Our whole family's living was in that ingot, and you've handed them our capital! How are we supposed to live? Quick—find out when this Academy student is heading home." The report came back: "They've already booked a boat; they board on a set day at a fixed hour." The information checked out.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'five to one' for '五換' is slightly ambiguous in English; while contextually clear as a ratio, the Ming vernacular idiom often implies a specific exchange rate or discount structure that could be phrased more precisely (e.g., 'at a five-tael-for-one rate' or 'at a five-to-one exchange rate') to avoid confusion with simple multiplication.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Later a young man came with a twelve-tael gold ingot to exchange. Ben took it and looked: it was nearly pure gold. He asked the rate, and the young man said, "A country gentleman ordered it exchanged; it must be done at a five-to-one rate." Ben passed it to Deng: "This gold could fetch six taels per tael; at five, the price is fair." Deng looked and said, "It really is good. Check this gold and put it away first—don't let it pass through his hands again—then weigh out sixty taels of silver to pay him." Ben followed the advice: he took in the gold first, then paid over the silver. The young man had no chance to work a switch and had to take the silver home. He told his father, "The two Academy students were so on their guard that I couldn't pull it off." The father stamped his foot: "Our whole family's living was in that ingot, and you've handed them our capital! How are we supposed to live? Quick—find out when this Academy student is heading home." The report came back: "They've already booked a boat; they board on a set day at a fixed hour." The information checked out.

### 114. [9/10] 第三類 換銀騙 Category 3 · Silver-Exchange Swindles — 道士船中換轉金 (cat-03-s01) `[body]`
