- **Chinese Source**:
  > 及賁監生歸，以金分贈妻妾。數日後，叫匠人來打釧鈿。先以小錠金打，匠皆稱金好。賁誇曰：「更換有一錠十二兩的尤好。」匠曰：「大錠金京中光棍多以銅脫人。」賁曰：「取與你看，有何棍能脫我乎？」匠接過手，笑曰：「正是銅也。」賁怪之，急取回看曰：「果銅也。我與鄧相公看定是上好金，又同船諸人看皆是好金，何都被瞞過？」忽猛省曰：「噯，是也！最後是一道士看，付還時天色近晚，我未及再檢視，即收藏箱中。是此時換去也！此道士何得一銅如此相似，又早已在手，如此換得容易？想京中換金後生，即老棍之子，彼換時未能脫，故來搭船脫歸也。」
- **Current English**:
  > When Ben got home, he divided the gold as gifts among his wife and concubines. A few days later he called in a goldsmith to make bracelets and hair ornaments. First he had the small ingots worked, and the goldsmith praised the gold. Ben boasted, "I also exchanged for a twelve-tael ingot that is even better." The goldsmith said, "With big ingots in the capital, smooth operators often cheat people with copper." Ben said, "I'll fetch it for you to see—what swindler could cheat me?" The goldsmith took it in hand and laughed: "This is copper, all right." Ben was stunned; he snatched it back and looked: "It really is copper. Deng and I examined it and judged it first-rate gold, and everyone on the boat who saw it called it good gold—how were we all deceived?" Then it hit him: "Ah, that's it! The last one to look at it was that Daoist. When he handed it back, the sky was nearly dark; I never checked it again before locking it in the trunk. That is when it was switched! How did that Daoist come to have a piece of copper so alike, already in his hand, to make the switch so easily? The young man who exchanged the gold in the capital must have been the old crook's son. When the son couldn't pull the switch at the exchange, the father came aboard to make the switch on the way home."

  - The translation 'young man who exchanged the gold' adds 'man' which isn't explicitly in '換金後生' (though implied), but 'post-swapper' or 'the young swindler' might be more precise stylistically.
- **Recommended Revision**:

First he had the small ingots worked, and the goldsmith praised the gold. Ben boasted, "I also exchanged for a twelve-tael ingot that is even better." The goldsmith said, "With big ingots in the capital, crooks often cheat people with copper." Ben said, "I'll fetch it for you to see—what swindler could cheat me?" The goldsmith took it in hand and laughed: "This is copper, all right." Ben was stunned; he snatched it back and looked: "It really is copper. Deng and I examined it and judged it first-rate gold, and everyone on the boat who saw it called it good gold—how were we all deceived?" Then it hit him: "Ah, that's it! The last one to look at it was that Daoist. When he handed it back, the sky was nearly dark; I never checked it again before locking it in the trunk. That is when it was switched! How did that Daoist come to have a piece of copper so alike, already in his hand, to make the switch so easily? The young swindler who exchanged the gold in the capital must have been the old crook's son. When the son couldn't pull off the switch at the exchange, the father came aboard to make the switch on the way home."

### 116. [9/10] 第四類 詐哄騙 Category 4 · Fraud-and-Coaxing Swindles — 诈学道书报好梦 (cat-04-s00) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 十二月初间，诸举人都上京矣。省城一棍，与本府一善书秀才谋，各诈为沈道一书，用小印图书，护封完密，分递于新春元家。每到一家，则云：“沈爷有书，专差小人来，口嘱付说，你家相公明年必有大捷。他得异梦，特令先来报知，但须谨密勿泄。更某某相公家，与尊府相近，恐他知有专使来，谓老爷厚此薄彼，故亦附有问安书在；特搭带耳，非专为彼来也。”及到他家，所言亦复如是，谓专为此来，餘者都搭带也。及开书看，则字画精楷，书词玄妙，皆称彼得祥梦，其兆应在某，当得大魁。或借其名，或因其地取义，各做一梦语为由，以报他先兆之意。曾见写与举人熊绍祖之书云：“闽省多才，甲于天下，虽京、浙不多让也。特阅麟经诸卷，无如贤最者。以深沉浑厚之养，发以雄俊爽锐之锋，来春大捷南宫，不卜而决矣。子月念二日夜将半，梦一飞熊，手擎红春花，行红日之中，上有金字‘大魁’二字，看甚分明。醒而忆之，日者，建阳也；熊者，君姓也；春花者，君治《春秋》经也。红亦彩色之象，‘大魁’金字则明有吉兆矣。以君之才，叶我之梦，则际明时魁天下，确有明证。若得大魁出于吾门，喜不能寐，人驰报，幸谨之勿泄。”熊举人之家阅之大喜，赏使银三两。请益，复与二两，曰：“明年有大捷，再赏你十两。”及他所奉之书，大抵都述吉梦，都是此意。人赏之者，皆三五金以上。
- **Current English**:
  > Early in the twelfth month, when the new juren had all gone up to the capital, a con man in the provincial city plotted with a xiucai of this prefecture who wrote a fine hand. For each new graduate they forged a letter in Commissioner Shen's name, stamped it with a small seal, wrapped and sealed it tight, and delivered the letters one by one to the graduates' homes. At each house the messenger would say: "Master Shen has a letter and has sent me specially. He charged me by word of mouth: your young master is certain of a great triumph next year. The master had a wondrous dream and ordered me to bring you word of it first—but you must guard it closely and let nothing leak. There is also young master so-and-so, whose family lives near yours; if he learned that a special messenger had come, he would say the master favors one and slights another, so a letter of greeting for him is enclosed as well. I merely brought it along—I was not sent for him." And when they came to that other house, they said exactly the same: that they had come specially for him, and all the rest were just brought along. When the families opened the letters, the calligraphy was in a fine, exact hand and the wording subtle and marvelous. Each said the commissioner had received an auspicious dream whose omen fell on that particular man, who was destined to be the great champion. Drawing now on a man's name, now on the meaning of his native place, they contrived a dream for each as the excuse, to foretell his coming glory. I once saw the letter they wrote to the juren Xiong Shaozu. It read: "Fujian abounds in talent, the first in the empire; even the capital and Zhejiang can hardly outdo it. And among all the essays on the Spring and Autumn that I read, none stood above yours. A deep and ample cultivation, issuing in a bold and shining edge—your triumph next spring in the Southern Palace, the metropolitan examination, is certain without divination. On the twenty-second night of the eleventh month, near midnight, I dreamed of a flying bear, a red spring flower raised in its paw, walking through the heart of a red sun, with two gold characters above it, 'Great Champion,' all plain to see. Waking, I pondered it: the sun is Jianyang, your native place; the bear is your surname; the spring flower is the Spring and Autumn, the classic you have made your own. Red is the hue of celebration, and the gold words 'Great Champion' are a manifest omen of luck. Your talent answering my dream is sure proof that in this bright reign you will stand first in the empire. If the great champion is to come from my own school, I shall be too joyful to sleep. I send this man at a gallop to tell you; keep it close and let nothing leak." The Xiong household read it with huge delight and rewarded the messenger with three taels of silver. When he begged for more, they gave him two taels more, saying, "If the great triumph comes next year, we will give you another ten." The letters delivered to the other houses all told lucky dreams to the same effect, and everyone who rewarded him gave three to five taels or more.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'new juren' in the first sentence is slightly imprecise; the Chinese '诸举人' refers to the candidates who have already passed the provincial exam (the juren themselves), not 'new' graduates. They are simply 'all the juren'.
  - The translation of '善书秀才' as 'a xiucai... who wrote a fine hand' is accurate but could be slightly more idiomatic as 'a calligraphic xiucai' or 'a xiucai skilled in writing', though the current phrasing is acceptable.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Early in the twelfth month, when all the juren had departed for the capital, a con man in the provincial city plotted with a calligraphic xiucai from this prefecture. Together they forged letters in Commissioner Shen's name for each graduate, stamped them with a small seal, wrapped and sealed them tightly, and delivered them one by one to the graduates' homes. At each house, the messenger would say: "Master Shen has a letter and has sent me specially. He charged me by word of mouth: your young master is certain of a great triumph next year. The master had a wondrous dream and ordered me to bring you word of it first—but you must guard it closely and let nothing leak. There is also young master so-and-so, whose family lives near yours; if he learned that a special messenger had come, he would say the master favors one and slights another, so a letter of greeting for him is enclosed as well. I merely brought it along—I was not sent for him." And when they came to that other house, they said exactly the same: that they had come specially for him, and all the rest were just brought along. When the families opened the letters, the calligraphy was in a fine, exact hand and the wording subtle and marvelous. Each said the commissioner had received an auspicious dream whose omen fell on that particular man, who was destined to be the great champion. Drawing now on a man's name, now on the meaning of his native place, they contrived a dream for each as the excuse, to foretell his coming glory. I once saw the letter they wrote to the juren Xiong Shaozu. It read: "Fujian abounds in talent, the first in the empire; even the capital and Zhejiang can hardly outdo it. And among all the essays on the Spring and Autumn that I read, none stood above yours. A deep and ample cultivation, issuing in a bold and shining edge—your triumph next spring in the Southern Palace, the metropolitan examination, is certain without divination. On the twenty-second night of the eleventh month, near midnight, I dreamed of a flying bear, a red spring flower raised in its paw, walking through the heart of a red sun, with two gold characters above it, 'Great Champion,' all plain to see. Waking, I pondered it: the sun is Jianyang, your native place; the bear is your surname; the spring flower is the Spring and Autumn, the classic you have made your own. Red is the hue of celebration, and the gold words 'Great Champion' are a manifest omen of luck. Your talent answering my dream is sure proof that in this bright reign you will stand first in the empire. If the great champion is to come from my own school, I shall be too joyful to sleep. I send this man at a gallop to tell you; keep it close and let nothing leak." The Xiong household read it with huge delight and rewarded the messenger with three taels of silver. When he begged for more, they gave him two taels more, saying, "If the great triumph comes next year, we will give you another ten." The letters delivered to the other houses all told lucky dreams to the same effect, and everyone who rewarded him gave three to five taels or more.

### 117. [9/10] 第四類 詐哄騙 Category 4 · Fraud-and-Coaxing Swindles — 诈无常烧牒捕人 (cat-04-s01) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 有一游方道士至寺，形容半槁，黄瘦黧黑，敬谒日者曰：“闻先生推命极验，敢求此地老幼，有本年命运该死者、当有疾病者，悉以其姓名八字授我，我愿以游方经验药方几种奉换。”日者曰：“你不知命，要此何干？”道士曰：“我自有别用。”日者悉以推过之命，本年有该病者、该死者，尽录付之。道士后乞食诸家，每逢痴愚样人，辄自称是生无常，奉阴司差，同鬼使捕拿此方某人某人等，限此一季到。痴人代之播传，人多未言。又私将黄纸写一牌文，末写“阴司”二大字，中间计开依日者所授之老幼命该死者，写于上半行。又向本僧寺问本地富家男女，及人家钟爱之子姓名，写于后上层。夜间故在社司前，将黄纸牌从下截无人名处焚化，其上半有人名处打灭存之。次日，人来社司祈，见香炉上有黄纸字半截未焚者，取视之，都是乡人姓名，后有“阴司”字，大怪异之，持以传闻于乡。不一月间，此姓名内果死两人，遂相传谓“前瘦道士是生无常，此阴司黄纸牌彼必知之。”凡牌中有名者皆来问，无名者恐下截已焚处有，亦往问之。道士半吞半吐，认是己同鬼使焚的。由是畏死者问：“阴司牌可计免否？”道士曰：“阴司与阳间衙门则同，有银用者，计较免到；或必要再拿者，亦可挨延二三年。奈何不可用银也。”由是，富家男女多以银贿道士，兼以冥财金银，托其计较免到，亦赚得数十金去。其后牌中有名者多不死，反以为得道士计免之力也，岂不惑哉！
- **Current English**:
  > A wandering Daoist priest came to the temple, his face half-withered, sallow, gaunt, and dark. He paid a respectful call on the day-master and said: "I hear, sir, that your reckonings are infallible. Might I ask you for the names and eight characters of all the old and young of this place who are fated to die this year or to fall ill? In exchange I offer several proven remedies gathered in my wanderings." The day-master said, "You cannot read fates—what use are they to you?" The Daoist said, "I have a use of my own." So the day-master copied out every fate he had reckoned—all who were due for sickness or death that year—and handed the list over. Afterward the Daoist went begging from door to door, and whenever he met a simple-looking soul he would announce that he was a Living Impermanence, one of the underworld's soul-fetchers, commissioned by the court below to join its ghost bailiffs in arresting such-and-such persons of the district, due within the season. The fools spread the word for him, though most people had yet to hear of it. He also secretly wrote out a warrant on yellow paper, ending with the two large characters "Underworld Court." In the middle he itemized, from the day-master's list, the old and young fated to die, written in the upper rows; and he asked at the temple for the names of rich families' sons and daughters and of well-beloved children, and wrote them in the rows just behind. At night he went deliberately to the shrine of the earth god and set the yellow warrant alight from the lower end, where there were no names, then stamped out the flames and kept the upper half with the names. Next day, when people came to pray at the shrine, they saw on the incense burner a half-burned slip of yellow paper with writing on it. Picking it up, they found it full of villagers' names, with "Underworld Court" at the end. Much astonished, they carried the story all through the district. Within the month, two of those named actually died, and word went around: "That gaunt Daoist is a Living Impermanence—he must know all about this yellow warrant from the underworld." Everyone whose name was on the warrant came to ask about it; and those not named, afraid their names had been in the burned part, went to ask too. The Daoist, mumbling and half-hesitant, admitted that he and the ghost bailiffs had burned it. Then those who feared death asked, "Can the underworld warrant be bought off?" The Daoist said, "The underworld is just like a yamen of the mortal world: spend silver and your summons can be arranged away; if they absolutely must arrest you, it can still be put off two or three years. There is nothing silver cannot do." So the sons and daughters of rich houses bribed the Daoist with silver, adding gold and silver spirit money for the dead, and begged him to arrange their exemption; he made off with several dozen taels. Afterward most of those named on the warrant did not die—and people actually credited the Daoist's negotiations. What delusion!
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The translation of '生无常' as 'Living Impermanence' is a creative choice that captures the concept but may be slightly less idiomatic than 'the Living Death God' or 'the mortal incarnation of Impermanence' for general readers, though it is accurate to the Buddhist/Taoist context.

### 118. [9/10] 第四類 詐哄騙 Category 4 · Fraud-and-Coaxing Swindles — 诈无常烧牒捕人 (cat-04-s01) `[note]`

- **Chinese Source**:
  > 按：阴司拘人，何须纸牌？即有牌票，亦何必焚？即焚矣，何为故留残纸餘字以扬于众？此必无之理也。观瘦无常一节，则惑世诬民昭昭矣。人之信鬼幻者，鉴此可以提醒。
- **Current English**:
  > Note: When the underworld arrests a man, what need has it of a paper warrant? If it did issue warrants, why burn one? And if it were burned, why deliberately leave a charred scrap with writing on it for all to see? None of this could possibly be. Look at the episode of the gaunt Impermanence: his duping of the world and swindling of the people is plain to see. Let those who believe in ghostly marvels take this story and wake up.
- **Identified Issues**:

- **Recommended Revision**:

And if it were burned, why deliberately leave a charred scrap with writing on it for all to see? None of this could possibly be. Look at the episode of the gaunt Impermanence: his duping of the world and swindling of the people is plain to see. Let those who believe in ghostly marvels take this story and wake up.

### 119. [9/10] 第四類 詐哄騙 Category 4 · Fraud-and-Coaxing Swindles — 诈以帚柄耍轿夫 (cat-04-s02) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 有一提控，不时往来于路，屡被轿夫刁蹬。一日，复要上县，先把两条纸，题四句嘲诗，以方纸包之，再用敝帚柄两个截齐，以绵纸封之，如两匹缎样。次日，自负上路，轿夫争来抬之。提控曰：“吾为一紧急事回家，身无现银。有能送我直到家者，议轿价二钱，又赏汝今晚明早酒饭。若要现银及转雇，则不能也。”内有二轿夫愿抬。遂以两封缎缚于轿，叮咛曰：“善安顿之，勿损坏！”才升轿，又曰：“我到回窑街，要寄一急信与人。你等到那里，慎勿忘也！”未半午后，已到回窑。提控曰：“你在此暂等，我去寄信便来。”其实抽身从小路归家，一饭久不来。两轿夫曰：“他坐话不觉久。有此两匹绸缎在此，我与你奔回，何须等他！”二人疾行，近晚归家。一曰：“各执一匹去。”一曰：“倘有好歹，须相添贴。”两人扯开绵纸，只是两截敝帚柄，重重封裹。又各有一方包，疑是书信。开之，见有纸题大字云：“轿夫常骗人，今也被我骗。若非两帚柄，险失两匹缎。”二人在家大骂曰：“光棍精光棍！”邻家轿夫闻之，入问何故各骂光棍，二轿夫叙其缘由如此。邻轿夫大笑而出，将两帚柄半封半露挂于排栅边，以两纸诗贴于旁。见者诵其诗，又看其帚柄，无不大笑曰：“此提控甚善骗。只你二轿夫亦不合起歹心，早是敝帚柄，故敢扬言骂人；若果是绸缎，你尚恐人知，那相公能寻汝取乎？此是你不是，何骂相公为？”
- **Current English**:
  > There was a yamen clerk (a tikong) who often traveled this road and had been given the runaround by the chair-bearers time and again. One day he had to go up to the county town again. First he wrote a four-line mocking verse on two slips of paper and wrapped them in square paper; then he took two worn broom handles, cut them to matching lengths, and sealed them up in cotton paper so they looked exactly like two bolts of silk. The next day he set out on the road carrying them himself, and the chair-bearers fought for the job. The clerk said, "I am heading home on urgent business and carry no ready silver. Whoever will take me straight home, we will agree on a fare of two qian, and I will stand you wine and supper tonight and breakfast tomorrow. If you want cash down or to subcontract me, that I cannot do." Two of the bearers agreed to carry him. He tied the two sealed "bolts" to the chair and charged them, "Stow them carefully—mind you don't damage them!" The moment he was seated he added, "At Huiyao Street I must send off an urgent letter to someone. When you get there, whatever you do, don't forget!" Before the afternoon was half spent they reached Huiyao. "Wait here a moment," said the clerk; "I will post the letter and be right back." In fact he slipped away by a side lane and headed home. A meal's time passed and he did not come. The two bearers said, "He is sitting there talking and has lost track of the time. Here are two bolts of silk in our hands—let us just run off home with them. Why wait for him?" The two hurried off and reached home toward evening. One said, "Each of us takes a bolt." The other said, "If one proves better than the other, we even it up between us." They tore open the cotton paper: nothing but two lengths of worn broom handle, wrapped round and round. Each bundle also held a square packet they took for a letter. Opening it, they found a slip inscribed in large characters: "Chair-bearers cheat people every day; today the cheat was mine. But for two broom handles, I would have lost two bolts of silk." At home the two cursed loudly: "The sharp! The very prince of sharps!" Chair-bearers from next door heard the noise and came in to ask why they were cursing sharps, and the two told them the whole story. The neighbors roared with laughter, then went out and hung the two broom handles, still half-wrapped and half-showing, on the palisade fence, pasting the two verse slips beside them. Everyone who passed read the verse aloud, looked at the broom handles, and burst out laughing: "This clerk is a master at the game. But you two bearers had no business nursing thieving thoughts. Luckily they were only broom handles, so you can shout your curses in public; had they really been silk, you would be terrified of anyone knowing, and how could the gentleman have come after you for them? The fault is yours—why curse the gentleman?"
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The translation of '提控' as 'yamen clerk' is slightly anachronistic for the Ming context where 'tikong' (a specific type of local official/clerk) is often better rendered simply as 'clerk' or kept as a transliteration with a brief note, though 'yamen clerk' is acceptable.
  - The phrase 'given the runaround' in the first sentence is a bit too modern/slangy compared to the wry tone of the original text; 'tricked' or 'swindled' might fit the register better.
  - The translation of the poem's last line '险失两匹缎' as 'I would have lost two bolts of silk' misses the nuance of '险' (narrowly escaped/just barely avoided), implying they actually lost it. It should be 'I narrowly escaped losing two bolts of silk' or similar.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > There was a clerk who often traveled this road and had been swindled by chair-bearers time and again. One day he had to go up to the county town again. First he wrote a four-line mocking verse on two slips of paper and wrapped them in square paper; then he took two worn broom handles, cut them to matching lengths, and sealed them up in cotton paper so they looked exactly like two bolts of silk. The next day he set out on the road carrying them himself, and the chair-bearers fought for the job. The clerk said, "I am heading home on urgent business and carry no ready silver. Whoever will take me straight home, we will agree on a fare of two qian, and I will stand you wine and supper tonight and breakfast tomorrow. If you want cash down or to subcontract me, that I cannot do." Two of the bearers agreed to carry him. He tied the two sealed "bolts" to the chair and charged them, "Stow them carefully—mind you don't damage them!" The moment he was seated he added, "At Huiyao Street I must send off an urgent letter to someone. When you get there, whatever you do, don't forget!" Before the afternoon was half spent they reached Huiyao. "Wait here a moment," said the clerk; "I will post the letter and be right back." In fact he slipped away by a side lane and headed home. A meal's time passed and he did not come. The two bearers said, "He is sitting there talking and has lost track of the time. Here are two bolts of silk in our hands—let us just run off home with them. Why wait for him?" The two hurried off and reached home toward evening. One said, "Each of us takes a bolt." The other said, "If one proves better than the other, we even it up between us." They tore open the cotton paper: nothing but two lengths of worn broom handle, wrapped round and round. Each bundle also held a square packet they took for a letter. Opening it, they found a slip inscribed in large characters: "Chair-bearers cheat people every day; today the cheat was mine. But for two broom handles, I narrowly escaped losing two bolts of silk." At home the two cursed loudly: "The sharp! The very prince of sharps!" Chair-bearers from next door heard the noise and came in to ask why they were cursing sharps, and the two told them the whole story. The neighbors roared with laughter, then went out and hung the two broom handles, still half-wrapped and half-showing, on the palisade fence, pasting the two verse slips beside them. Everyone who passed read the verse aloud, looked at the broom handles, and burst out laughing: "This clerk is a master at the game. But you two bearers had no business nursing thieving thoughts. Luckily they were only broom handles, so you can shout your curses in public; had they really been silk, you would be terrified of anyone knowing, and how could the gentleman have come after you for them? The fault is yours—why curse the gentleman?"

### 120. [9/10] 第四類 詐哄騙 Category 4 · Fraud-and-Coaxing Swindles — 诈以帚柄耍轿夫 (cat-04-s02) `[body]`

The clerk laughed heartily and went on his way.

### 121. [9/10] 第四類 詐哄騙 Category 4 · Fraud-and-Coaxing Swindles — 诈以帚柄耍轿夫 (cat-04-s02) `[note]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 按：提控骗轿坐者非棍也，此两轿夫则棍耳。不然，何提控再回询问，而轿夫不敢出也？此谓借棍术，还驭棍徒，亦巧矣。然凡远出，若雇轿夫、挑夫，须从店主同雇。彼知役夫根脚，斯无拐逃失落之虞矣。
- **Current English**:
  > Note: The clerk who swindled a free chair ride was no swindler; the two chair-bearers were the swindlers. If not, why, when the clerk came back asking questions, did the bearers not dare show themselves? This is called borrowing the crooks' own tricks to drive the crooks—neatly done. Still, whenever you travel far and need chair-bearers or porters, hire them through your innkeeper. He knows the laborers' origins, and you need not fear them running off with your goods.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'free chair ride' slightly misrepresents the original nuance of '骗轿坐者' (one who swindled a chair ride), which implies the clerk tricked them into giving him a ride without paying or under false pretenses, rather than it being inherently free.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Note: The clerk who swindled a chair ride was no swindler; the two chair-bearers were the crooks. Otherwise, why did the clerk return to ask questions while the bearers dared not show themselves? This is called borrowing the crooks' own tricks to drive out the crooks—quite clever. Still, whenever you travel far and need chair-bearers or porters, hire them through your innkeeper. He knows the laborers' backgrounds, so there is no fear of them running off with your goods.

### 122. [9/10] 第四類 詐哄騙 Category 4 · Fraud-and-Coaxing Swindles — 巷门口诈买脱布 (cat-04-s03) `[note]`

  > Note: A man selling goods, even when invited in at a gate, must follow the buyer all the way to his house and see that his dwelling is real and settled before he hands anything over. Otherwise, even at a public gateway where households press in thick behind it, you must not be quick to trust. Let every merchant take this to heart.

- **Recommended Revision**:

Otherwise, even at a public gateway where households crowd thickly behind it, one must not be quick to trust. Let every merchant take this to heart.

### 123. [9/10] 第五類 偽交騙 Category 5 · False-Friendship Swindles — 哄饮嫖害其身名 (cat-05-s00) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 不数年，涧故。石孝居忧，无人检束。涓思孝年少不羁，或可诱以酒色。因伪相结纳，孝趋亦趋，孝诺亦诺，终日游戏相征逐，数以曲蘖为欢。或时有美妓，涓邀孝饮其邸；或有好戏妇，涓每搬戏邀孝饮，又令戏妇曲意奉承，务挑其淫荡之心。孝堕其术中而不觉，玩日月，荒废诗书。及服阕补考，竟列劣等。孝因发奋，往寺读书，涓辄拉友挟妓，载酒至寺欢饮。孝见妓，不觉有喜心，故态复萌。涓又劝孝娶美妾二人，朝夕纵淫。内荒于色，外湎于酒，手沾战疯，不能楷书。道考被黜，家业凋零。石涓抚掌大笑曰：“吾生平之恨泄矣，计亦遂矣！”乃呼其子而训之曰：“涧兄在日，家富于我。因生孝不肖，酷好饮酒宿娼，不事诗书，致令丧却前程，身如丧家之狗。尔辈宜以为鉴，慎勿踏其覆辙。”未几，其子亦被人引诱赌嫖，所费不訾。涓因年老，无如之何，唯付之长叹而已。
- **Current English**:
  > A few years later Jian died. Shi Xiao was in mourning, with no one to keep him in line. Juan thought: Xiao is young and unbridled; perhaps he can be baited with wine and women. So he feigned friendship and attached himself to the boy: where Xiao went, Juan went; whatever Xiao approved, Juan approved. They spent whole days chasing amusements together and made merry over wine again and again. Whenever a pretty courtesan appeared, Juan invited Xiao to drink at her house; whenever a good actress came to town, Juan would stage a play and invite Xiao to drink, telling the actress to fawn on him with every art, bent on stirring his lust. Xiao fell into the trap without knowing it. He idled away the months and let his books go to waste. When the mourning ended and he sat the make-up examination, he was placed in the bottom grade. Xiao then roused himself and went to study at a temple, but Juan would drag along friends and courtesans, carting wine to the temple for drinking bouts. At the sight of the courtesans Xiao’s heart lifted despite himself, and his old habits sprouted again. Juan next urged Xiao to take two beautiful concubines, and he wallowed in lust morning and night. Wasted by sex within and soaked in wine without, his hands were seized with a tremor and he could no longer write a steady hand. At the circuit examination he was struck off the rolls, and the family estate withered. Shi Juan clapped and roared with laughter: “The grudge of a lifetime is vented, and my scheme has succeeded!” Then he called his sons and lectured them: “While brother Jian lived, his house was richer than mine. But he sired that worthless Xiao, whose one love was drinking and whoring and who would not touch his books, so he threw away his future and ended up a homeless dog. Take him as a warning, and never follow in his ruts.” Not long after, his own sons were led by others into gambling and whoring, and the money they burned through was beyond counting. Juan was old by then and could do nothing; he could only heave long sighs.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'hands were seized with a tremor' is slightly stiff; the Ming idiom '手沾战疯' (literally: hands touched by shaking madness) refers to severe alcohol-induced tremors or palsy. A more idiomatic rendering like 'his hands shook uncontrollably' or 'he suffered from the shakes' would better capture the physical reality of the condition.
- **Recommended Revision**:

Juan next urged Xiao to take two beautiful concubines, and he wallowed in lust morning and night. ### 127. [9/10] 第五類 偽交騙 Category 5 · False-Friendship Swindles — 激友讼奸以败家 (cat-05-s03) `[body]`

- **Chinese Source**:
  > 马自鸣，浙江绍兴人。狷巧小人，柔媚多奸。族弟马应，轻浮愚昧，家更富于自鸣。其父素与鸣父不睦，两相图而未发。自鸣见应愚呆，性又嗜酒，故时时与之会饮；亦连引诸人，共打平和。唯此两人深相结纳，人多厌之，不与共饮。二人乃对斟对酌，此唱彼和，自号为莫逆交。应有事，多取决于自鸣；鸣亦时献小计，以效忠款。应素与亲兄不睦，数扬其短，欲状告之。自鸣假意劝阻，实于当机处反言以激之，益深其怒。应遂先往告兄，经官断明，拟应欧兄之罪；又投分上解释。此为破家之始。又屡屡唆其与人争讼，家日破败。
- **Current English**:
  > Ma Ziming was a native of Shaoxing in Zhejiang—a small-minded schemer, smooth and fawning and full of guile. His younger cousin Ma Ying was flighty and dull, and his household was even richer than Ziming’s. Ying’s father had long been at odds with Ziming’s father; each side was plotting against the other, but neither had struck. Ziming saw that Ying was a fool and a drunk besides, so he drank with him constantly, sometimes rounding up others to split the bill. But only these two formed a real bond; everyone else grew sick of them and would not drink with them. So the two poured for each other, sang each other’s praises, and called themselves bosom friends. Whenever Ying had business, he mostly let Ziming decide, and Ziming would offer little schemes from time to time to prove his devotion. Ying had long been on bad terms with his own elder brother; he often aired the man’s faults and wanted to file a complaint against him. Ziming made a show of talking him out of it, but at the decisive moment he would say just the opposite to provoke him, and Ying’s anger deepened. Ying went ahead and accused his brother first. The officials saw the case clearly and found Ying guilty of assaulting an elder brother; he got off only by calling in favors. This was the beginning of the family’s ruin. After that Ziming egged him into lawsuit after lawsuit, and the household crumbled by the day.

### 128. [9/10] 第六類 牙行騙 Category 6 · Broker Swindles — 狡牙脱纸以女偿 (cat-06-s00) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 施守训，福建大安人。家资殷富，常造纸卖客。一日，自装千餘篓，价值八百餘两，往苏州卖，寓牙人翁滨二店。滨乃宿牙，叠积前客债甚多，见施雏商，将其纸尽还前客，误施坐候半年。家中又发纸五百餘篓到苏州，滨代现卖。付银讫，托言系取旧帐者，复候半年。知受其笼络，施乃怒骂殴之。滨无言可应，当凭乡亲刘光前议，谕滨立过借批银八百两，劝施暂回。
- **Current English**:
  > Shi Shouxun was a native of Da'an in Fujian. His family was wealthy, and he regularly made paper to sell to traveling merchants. One day he loaded more than a thousand baskets himself, worth over eight hundred taels, and went to Suzhou to sell them, lodging at the shop of the broker Weng Bin'er. Weng was an old hand among brokers and had piled up heavy debts to earlier clients. Seeing that Shi was a novice merchant, he used the entire shipment of paper to pay off those earlier clients and left Shi cooling his heels for half a year. Shi's family then sent another five hundred-odd baskets of paper to Suzhou, and Weng sold them for him for cash. When the silver came in, he claimed it had gone to settle old accounts, and Shi waited another half a year. Realizing he had been strung along, Shi cursed him and beat him. Weng had no answer. With their fellow townsman Liu Guangqian mediating, Weng was made to write out an IOU for eight hundred taels, and Shi was advised to go back home for the time being.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'made paper to sell to traveling merchants' slightly over-specifies the original '卖客' (sell to customers/merchants), though contextually acceptable.

### 129. [9/10] 第六類 牙行騙 Category 6 · Broker Swindles — 狡牙脱纸以女偿 (cat-06-s00) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 次年，复载纸到苏州，滨代现卖。只前帐难还，施又坐待半年。见其女云英有貌，未曾许配，思此银难取，乃浼刘光前为媒，求其女为妾，抵还前帐。滨悦许之。其女年方十五，执不肯从。滨与妻入劝曰：“古有缇萦愿没官为婢，以赎父罪。今父欠客人银八百两，以汝填还；况福建客家多巨富，若后日生子，分其家财，居此致富，享福非小。”女始允诺。时施已六十餘矣。成婚近四载，施后回家身故。未及周期服，滨将女重嫁南京溧水县梁恩赐为妾，重收聘礼一百两。
- **Current English**:
  > The next year Shi again shipped paper to Suzhou, and Weng sold it for cash. But the old debt was still hard to recover, and Shi sat waiting another half a year. He saw that Weng's daughter Yunying was good-looking and not yet betrothed. Thinking the silver would be hard to collect, he asked Liu Guangqian to act as matchmaker and sought the daughter as a concubine, to offset the old debt. Weng happily agreed. The girl had just turned fifteen and flatly refused. Weng and his wife went in to press her: "In ancient times the girl Tiying offered to become a state slave to ransom her father from punishment. Now your father owes a client eight hundred taels of silver, and we are making it good with you. Besides, Fujian merchant families are often immensely rich; if you later bear a son and get a share of the family property, you will be set up in wealth here and enjoy no small fortune." Only then did the girl consent. Shi was already over sixty. They were married nearly four years; later Shi went back home and died. Before the year of mourning was out, Weng married the girl off again as a concubine to Liang Enci of Lishui County, Nanjing, and collected another hundred taels in betrothal gifts.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The translation renders '施又坐待半年' as 'Shi sat waiting another half a year.' While literal, the Ming vernacular '坐待' in this context implies passively enduring or waiting without action due to the debtor's stalling, which is slightly softened by 'sat waiting'. A phrase like 'waited in vain for another half-year' might better capture the frustration implied by the original narrative tone.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > The next year Shi again shipped paper to Suzhou, and Weng sold it for cash. But the old debt remained hard to recover, so Shi waited in vain for another half-year. He noticed that Weng's daughter Yunying was good-looking and not yet betrothed. Thinking the silver would be difficult to collect, he asked Liu Guangqian to act as matchmaker and sought the daughter as a concubine to offset the debt. Weng happily agreed. The girl had just turned fifteen and flatly refused. Weng and his wife went in to press her: "In ancient times the girl Tiying offered to become a state slave to ransom her father from punishment. Now your father owes a client eight hundred taels of silver, and we are making it good with you. Besides, Fujian merchant families are often immensely rich; if you later bear a son and get a share of the family property, you will be set up in wealth here and enjoy no small fortune." Only then did the girl consent. Shi was already over sixty. They were married nearly four years; later Shi returned home and died. Before the mourning year was out, Weng remarried the girl as a concubine to Liang Enci of Lishui County, Nanjing, and collected another hundred taels in betrothal gifts.

### 133. [9/10] 第七類 引賭騙 Category 7 · Lured into Gambling — 危言激人引再赌 (cat-07-s00) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 乡有陈荣一者，乃士升父在日所用做中保供呼唤者。人虽微贱，却有忠义之心，不忍士升之被棍诱引也。乃备一盛筵，单请士升一人，酒筵中慢慢缓谈，将其父在日，始终生财缘由，爱惜钱米实事，一一从头细讲；且赞羡其能，慨叹其苦，后又谈及民情世故，及钱米难得之状，穷民无钱之苦，因劝之曰：“令先尊发此巨富非易，你须念先人勤劳，保守基业，切不可去赌。前者虽赌去数百金，已往勿咎；但从今改过，依旧坐享福泽矣。”士升见荣一词情恳切，一时良心发动，曰：“吾依你言，从今誓不赌也。”次日棍徒引之，果不去赌。众方怪异，后知出于荣一所劝，无可奈何，商议曰：“谁能引其再赌者，众敛十金与之。”有柴昆者曰：“我能引之。”众将银十两封存。
- **Current English**:
  > In the village lived a man named Chen Rongyi, whom Shisheng's late father had employed as a middleman and errand runner. Humble as he was, he had a loyal and upright heart, and he could not bear to watch Shisheng being led on by swindlers. So he prepared a fine feast and invited Shisheng alone. Over the wine he talked slowly and at length: from beginning to end he told how the father had built his fortune, and exactly how he had watched over every coin and every grain of rice. He praised the father's ability and sighed over his hardships; then he spoke of the ways of the world, of how hard cash and rice are to come by, and of the misery of the poor who have none. At last he urged him: “It was not easy for your late father to amass such wealth. You must remember your forebears' toil and hold on to the family foundation. Whatever you do, stop gambling. True, you have already lost several hundred taels, but let bygones be bygones. Mend your ways from today, and you can still live at ease on the family fortune.” Shisheng saw that Rongyi's words were earnest, and for a moment his conscience stirred. “I will do as you say,” he said. “From today I swear off gambling.” The next day the swindlers came for him, and sure enough he would not go. The gang was baffled; when they later learned it was Rongyi's doing, there was nothing they could do. They took counsel: “Whoever can get him gambling again, we will pool ten taels for him.” A man named Chai Kun said, “I can do it.” The gang sealed up ten taels of silver as the prize.

### 134. [9/10] 第七類 引賭騙 Category 7 · Lured into Gambling — 危言激人引再赌 (cat-07-s00) `[body]`

  > 昆见士升在路亭闲坐，挨近其身，先闲谈他事，后问曰：“闻汝今收手不赌乎？”士升曰：“然。”昆曰：“赌非好事，今能自知回头，真是豪杰。盛族富家子弟，果有知识高人，我真羡服！只外人都传，是荣一老劝你而止。果是他劝否？”士升曰：“的是得他劝。”柴昆嗟叹曰：“荣一小辈奔走下贱之流，岂是你父兄，岂是你叔伯，何禁止得人？你名门子弟，聪明男子，何待贱人训诲？使路人传你听下贱人主使，皆暗中非笑，谓你无能为。依我所见，还当暂出小赌，过了半月一月，自己收手，人便说你是自不爱赌，非关听下辈命令也。如此方是大丈夫所为，不羞了故家门风。”士升是无识雏子，闻此佞言，心自猜曰：“果是我今若便止，人道是荣一之功。须再去赌一月，然后自止，岂不挺豪杰哉！”随即入场复赌。柴昆暗领众银而去。
- **Current English**:

Kun said, “Gambling is a bad business. To see that and turn back on your own—that is the mark of a true hero. For the son of a great and wealthy house to show such sense, I honestly admire you. Only—everyone is saying it was old Rongyi who talked you into stopping. Is that really so?” Shisheng said, “It is. It was his advice.” Chai Kun sighed. “Rongyi is a menial, a runner of errands. Is he your father or brother? Is he your uncle? What right has he to forbid you anything? You are the son of a famous family, a clever man—why take instruction from a menial? If word gets around that you take orders from a low fellow, people will laugh at you behind your back and say you have no spine. In my view, you should go back for a little light gambling. After half a month or a month, quit on your own. Then people will say you stopped because you no longer cared for gambling, not because you obeyed a servant's orders. That is how a true man acts, and it brings no shame on your family's name.” Shisheng was a witless greenhorn. Hearing this smooth, false talk, he thought to himself: “True enough—if I stop now, people will call it Rongyi's achievement. Better to gamble another month and then quit on my own. Won't I stand out as a hero then?” He went straight back to the gambling rooms. Chai Kun quietly collected the gang's silver and left.

### 135. [9/10] 第七類 引賭騙 Category 7 · Lured into Gambling — 装公子套妓脱赌 (cat-07-s01) `[body]`

  > 王荻溪，万金之子。好赌无厌，多被赌朋合谋，尽倾其家。后收拾餘资，只得三百两，乃带一仆，复往县中赌。众棍后合本，迭来与赌。时荻溪家已尽破，而赌已学得甚高，虽未能胜众棍，亦不至为棍所胜。相持半月余，无好子弟到，无雏家可网，乃投府去。更无大赌场可快意者，遂往嫖李细卿家。
- **Current English**:
  > Wang Dixi was the son of a family worth ten thousand taels. He loved gambling insatiably, and his gambling friends conspired against him so often that his whole fortune was lost. Later he scraped together what capital remained—barely three hundred taels—took a single servant, and went back to the county town to gamble. The sharps there pooled their funds and took turns at the table with him. By then Dixi's household was ruined, but his skill at gambling had grown very high: he could not beat the pack of sharps, yet they could not beat him either. For over half a month they held each other off, but no new young gentlemen came to town and there were no greenhorns to net. So Dixi went on to the prefectural city; and since no gambling den there was big enough to please him either, he took up with the courtesan Li Xiqing.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'took up with' in the final sentence is slightly ambiguous in modern English; it can imply a romantic relationship or casual association rather than specifically visiting a courtesan for entertainment/gambling. While contextually clear, a more precise term like 'visited' or 'went to see' would better capture the specific action of seeking out a courtesan's house without implying intimacy.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Wang Dixi was the son of a family worth ten thousand taels. He loved gambling insatiably, and his gambling friends conspired against him so often that his whole fortune was lost. Later he scraped together what capital remained—barely three hundred taels—took a single servant, and went back to the county town to gamble. The sharps there pooled their funds and took turns at the table with him. By then Dixi's household was ruined, but his skill at gambling had grown very high: he could not beat the pack of sharps, yet they could not beat him either. For over half a month they held each other off, but no new young gentlemen came to town and there were no greenhorns to net. So Dixi went on to the prefectural city; and since no gambling den there was big enough to please him either, he went to visit the courtesan Li Xiqing.

### 136. [9/10] 第七類 引賭騙 Category 7 · Lured into Gambling — 装公子套妓脱赌 (cat-07-s01) `[body]`

  > 至一更，公子输上百金。细卿亦抽头十餘两矣，即将骰子收起，曰：“今日乘轿劳顿，夜已深矣，须去睡。明日看戏时酒席中再翻，稍抬举我抽头。”公子以输多，发怒要赌。荻溪亦发大言曰：“若再来，须百金一堆，不然且罢！”公子先取定银在，以一百为堆。细卿故执骰不与。公子大怒曰：“只凭一掷，随有无便罢！”细卿付还骰。公子一掷即胜，得百金，曰：“更照前一堆。”又胜；曰：“我生平好大不好细，须二百为堆。”方发性间，忽闻门外火把轿来。慌入报曰：“老爷跟寻至急，可速回去！”公子曰：“我色方来，奈何阻我兴！”其后一掷，又赢二百为堆。家人催如星火，公子曰：“我明日昼间不来，夜定来矣。”荻溪留之，不能得。细卿亦惊作痴呆样，慌忙送别。归怨荻溪曰：“人无全胜。你先赢许多，须当知止。奈何公子欲翻，你更出大堆，是不晓避色也。空作惯家，不及我妇人见矣。”荻溪曰：“吾万金赌尽，何数他三百两！有甚大事，空怨恨为！”在细卿家留宿数日；再留之，坚辞而去。
- **Current English**:
  > By the first watch the young lord had lost more than a hundred taels, and Xiqing had taken over ten taels as her cut. She gathered up the dice and said, “You have had a tiring day in the sedan chair, and the night is deep. You should get some sleep. Tomorrow, over the wine at the play, you can win it back, and perhaps show me a little favor with the cut.” The young lord, having lost heavily, flew into a temper and demanded to play on. Dixi too made a big speech: “If we go on, it is a hundred taels a pile, or we stop where we are!” The young lord pulled out ready silver and stacked it a hundred to the pile. Xiqing deliberately held on to the dice and would not hand them over. The young lord flared up: “One throw, that is all—win or lose, we are done!” Xiqing gave the dice back. The young lord won at a single cast and took the hundred taels. “Another pile, same as before,” he said. He won again. “All my life I have liked things large, never small. Make it two hundred a pile.” Just as he was working himself into a fever, torches and a sedan chair were suddenly heard at the gate. A servant rushed in: “His Lordship is out searching for you, most urgently. You must go back at once!” The young lord said, “My luck has just turned—why spoil my run?” His next throw won another two-hundred pile. The servants pressed him as if the house were on fire. “Tomorrow I cannot come by day,” the young lord said, “but at night I will surely be here.” Dixi tried to keep him, without success. Xiqing put on a stunned, witless look of fright and hurried to see him off. Then she came back and rounded on Dixi: “No one wins forever. You had won a great deal at first; you should have known when to stop. When the young lord wanted to win it back, why did you put out even bigger piles? That is not knowing how to duck a hot hand. A fine old hand you are—your judgment does not match a mere woman's.” Dixi said, “I have gambled away ten thousand taels in my time. What is a mere three hundred to me? What is the great matter, that you should fret and fume over it?” He stayed on at Xiqing's house for several days; when she pressed him to stay longer, he firmly declined and left.

  - The phrase 'duck a hot hand' for '避色' is a creative modern idiom that captures the meaning well but slightly shifts the register from the wry commentary style to contemporary slang. A more period-appropriate phrasing like 'avoid a winning streak' or 'know when to retreat' might better fit the 'Note: I observe' voice.

  > 按：公子是装来的，先以厚礼送妓，令荻溪信为真公子。后来圈套，皆是装成。其药骰已先藏在细卿手，故令其抢起真骰，然后以药骰付还之，使其不疑。三执皆胜，套定催归，其谁防之？然荻溪虽作家，安能测其弊哉！吁！凡赌博者，弊外生弊，鉴此而知机，收手勿赌，真良策也，莫如彼之一旦尽囊而空矣！
- **Current English**:

Three throws, three wins; then the staged summons that hurried him home—who would guard against that? Yet even Dixi, practiced hand that he was, could not have fathomed such a cheat. When Dong saw the assistant magistrate return the visit, he believed the man was a genuine young lord. That night the con man invited Dong to share the gift. Dong accepted gladly, but inwardly he still kept careful guard against theft and dared not drink deep, so the con man still found no chance to strike. - **Chinese Source**:
  > 待栋醒觉，不知棍何处去矣。即在崇安县告店家通同作弊。随即往江西广信府，告其县丞勾引光棍，而以原店家作证。县丞诉曰：“福建巡道实与我同府异县，其人姓氏，我素知之；但公子并未会面，他称其姓氏来拜我，我乃县丞小官，安得不回他拜，不送他赆？今至崇安已经数日，盗你银去，与我何干？”栋曰：“那棍一路同来，我防之甚切。他来谒你，而你回拜，我方信是真公子，故堕其术。今其人系你相识，安得不告你？”本府不能判断。栋又在史大巡处告。史爷判是县丞不合错拜公子，轻易便送下程，致误客商，不无公错。谅断银壹百两，与栋作盘缠之资而归。
- **Current English**:
  > When Dong awoke, the con man was nowhere to be found. He promptly filed a complaint in Chong’an county accusing the innkeeper of colluding in the swindle, then went on to Guangxin Prefecture in Jiangxi and charged the assistant magistrate with being in league with the sharp, naming the innkeeper as his witness. The assistant magistrate protested: “The Fujian circuit intendant really is from my prefecture, though a different county, and I have long known his name. But I had never met his son. He came to call on me under that name, and I am a mere assistant magistrate—how could I not return his call or send him a parting gift? By the time you reached Chong’an, days had passed. He stole your silver—what has that to do with me?” Dong said: “That crook traveled with me the whole way, and I guarded against him closely. Only when he called on you and you returned the call did I believe he was a true young lord, and so I fell into his trap. Since the man is an acquaintance of yours, how can I not bring the charge against you?” The prefecture could not decide the case. Dong then brought it before Inspector Shi. Master Shi ruled that the assistant magistrate had been wrong to pay respects to a false “young lord” and to send provisions so lightly, thereby misleading a traveling merchant—there was official fault in it. He saw fit to award Dong one hundred taels of silver as travel money for the journey home.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'official fault in it' for '不无公错' is slightly stiff; a more natural rendering would be 'there was some official negligence' or 'it was not without official fault'.
  - The translation of '史爷判是县丞不合错拜公子' as 'Master Shi ruled that the assistant magistrate had been wrong to pay respects to a false young lord' is accurate but could be slightly more idiomatic by using 'improperly returned the call' instead of 'pay respects', though the current version is acceptable.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > When Dong awoke, the con man was nowhere to be found. He promptly filed a complaint in Chong'an county accusing the innkeeper of colluding in the swindle, then went on to Guangxin Prefecture in Jiangxi and charged the assistant magistrate with being in league with the sharp, naming the innkeeper as his witness. The assistant magistrate protested: "The Fujian circuit intendant really is from my prefecture, though a different county, and I have long known his name. But I had never met his son. He came to call on me under that name, and I am a mere assistant magistrate—how could I not return his call or send him a parting gift? By the time you reached Chong'an, days had passed. He stole your silver—what has that to do with me?" Dong said: "That crook traveled with me the whole way, and I guarded against him closely. Only when he called on you and you returned the call did I believe he was a true young lord, and so I fell into his trap. Since the man is an acquaintance of yours, how can I not bring the charge against you?" The prefecture could not decide the case. Dong then brought it before Inspector Shi. Master Shi ruled that the assistant magistrate had improperly returned the call to a false "young lord" and sent provisions too readily, thereby misleading a traveling merchant—it was not without official negligence. He saw fit to award Dong one hundred taels of silver as travel money for the journey home.

### 142. [9/10] 第八類 露財騙 Category 8 · Displaying Wealth — 炫耀衣妆启盗心 (cat-08-s01) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 游天生，徽州府人。丰采俊雅，好装饰。尝同一仆徐丁携本银五百馀两，往建宁府买铁。始到崇安县，搭一青流船，艄公名李雅，水手名翁迓。雅先以嫖赌破家，后无赖而撑船。其时船至建阳县，天生起岸，往拜乡亲。将衣箱打开，取出衣服鲜丽，所带用物俱美。雅一见生心。
- **Current English**:
  > You Tiansheng was a man of Huizhou Prefecture, handsome and elegant in bearing, and he loved to dress well. Once, with a single servant, Xu Ding, and more than five hundred taels of capital, he set out for Jianning Prefecture to buy iron. Reaching Chong’an county, he took passage on a Qingliu boat. The boatman was named Li Ya, the deckhand Weng Ya. Ya had ruined his family with whoring and gambling and, left with nothing, took to poling a boat. When the boat reached Jianyang county, Tiansheng went ashore to call on fellow townsmen. He opened his clothes chest and took out bright, handsome garments; everything he carried was fine. The moment Ya saw it, covetousness stirred in his heart.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The translation of '搭一青流船' as 'took passage on a Qingliu boat' is slightly ambiguous; 'Qingliu' likely refers to the boat type or route (Green Current) rather than being a proper name for the vessel itself. A more natural phrasing might be 'boarded a Green Current boat' or 'a boat of the Qingliu type'.
  - The phrase 'covetousness stirred in his heart' is accurate but slightly formal compared to the vernacular tone of the original. A more idiomatic Ming-style rendering could be 'Ya's greed was immediately aroused' or 'Ya saw an opportunity and his mind turned to theft'.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > You Tiansheng was from Huizhou Prefecture—handsome and elegant in bearing, fond of fine dress. Once, accompanied by a single servant, Xu Ding, and carrying over five hundred taels of capital, he set out for Jianning Prefecture to buy iron. Upon reaching Chong'an County, he boarded a Qingliu-type boat. The boatman was named Li Ya, the deckhand Weng Ya. Ya had previously ruined his family through whoring and gambling; left penniless, he took to poling boats. When the vessel arrived at Jianyang County, Tiansheng went ashore to visit fellow townspeople. He opened his clothes chest, revealing bright, handsome garments; everything he carried was of excellent quality. The moment Ya caught sight of it, greed seized him.

### 143. [9/10] 第八類 露財騙 Category 8 · Displaying Wealth — 炫耀衣妆启盗心 (cat-08-s01) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 至晚，天生叫艄公买些酒馔。雅暗将陀陀花入酒中———陀陀花者，乃三年茄花也，人服此则昏迷不能语。是夜，天生主仆中了此毒，醉不能醒。三鼓时候，雅邀水手行谋。水手曰：“钱财有命，不可逆理妄求。倘若事泄，罪将安逃？吾不敢为也。”雅狠心一起，不听水手之阻，将其主仆推入深潭。天生淹死，徐丁幸饮酒少，入水复苏，颇识水性，水上岸。次日，搭后船往建宁府，即抱牌告于王大爷。当差捕兵六名，同徐丁到临江门去缉拿。临江门，乃建宁往来诸船凑集之口岸也。是时李雅谋财在手，正买酒上船，思量作乐。徐丁认得，即引捕兵擒锁，搜其赃物，尚在船中。遂并入赃，俱拿到府。
- **Current English**:
  > That evening Tiansheng told the boatman to buy some wine and food. Ya secretly slipped tuotuo blossoms into the wine—the tuotuo is the flower of a three-year eggplant, and whoever takes it falls senseless and cannot speak. That night Tiansheng and his servant took the poison and sank into a stupor from which they could not wake. At the third drum, Ya invited the deckhand to join the plot. The deckhand said: “Wealth is allotted by fate; you cannot chase it against all right. If this gets out, where will you flee from the crime? I will have no part in it.” But a ruthless resolve rose in Ya; he ignored the deckhand’s protest and pushed master and servant into a deep pool. Tiansheng drowned. Xu Ding, luckily, had drunk little; he came to in the water and, being a fair swimmer, made it up the bank. The next day he caught a later boat to Jianning Prefecture and lodged a formal complaint before Master Wang. Six constables were assigned to go with Xu Ding to the Linjiang Gate wharf to make the arrest—Linjiang Gate being the port where all boats to and from Jianning gathered. Just then Li Ya, the stolen money in hand, was buying wine to take aboard, planning to make merry. Xu Ding recognized him and led the constables to seize and chain him; a search turned up the loot, still on the boat. They took him, loot and all, to the prefecture.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'fair swimmer' slightly over-translates '颇识水性' (familiar with water skills/conditions) into a specific skill level rather than the more general competence implied.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > That evening Tiansheng told the boatman to buy some wine and food. Ya secretly slipped tuotuo blossoms into the wine—the tuotuo is the flower of a three-year eggplant, and whoever takes it falls senseless and cannot speak. That night Tiansheng and his servant took the poison and sank into a stupor from which they could not wake. At the third drum, Ya invited the deckhand to join the plot. The deckhand said: "Wealth is allotted by fate; you cannot chase it against all right. If this gets out, where will you flee from the crime? I will have no part in it." But a ruthless resolve rose in Ya; he ignored the deckhand's protest and pushed master and servant into a deep pool. Tiansheng drowned. Xu Ding, luckily, had drunk little; he came to in the water and, being familiar with the water, made it up the bank. The next day he caught a later boat to Jianning Prefecture and lodged a formal complaint before Master Wang. Six constables were assigned to go with Xu Ding to the Linjiang Gate wharf to make the arrest—Linjiang Gate being the port where all boats to and from Jianning gathered. Just then Li Ya, the stolen money in hand, was buying wine to take aboard, planning to make merry. Xu Ding recognized him and led the constables to seize and chain him; a search turned up the loot, still on the boat. They took him, loot and all, to the prefecture.

### 144. [9/10] 第八類 露財騙 Category 8 · Displaying Wealth — 炫耀衣妆启盗心 (cat-08-s01) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 王爷审问。雅见事露，难以推托，一概供招，攀及水手同谋。徐丁曰：“我当中毒时，酩酊不能言，如梦中，闻得水手劝阻，不与同谋，已先逃去。今若枉及此人，令后人不肯向善也。”王爷即将李雅责四十板收监，依律拟斩。其行李并原银，差防夫二名，同徐丁直解至天生家去。李雅次年冬季处决。后水手翁迓弃船归农，颇致丰足。雅以谋人而促死，迓以阻谏而全家，谚曰：“善有善报，恶有恶报”，信不虚也。
- **Current English**:
  > Master Wang held the hearing. Seeing the affair was out and denial useless, Ya confessed to everything—and dragged in the deckhand as an accomplice. Xu Ding said: “When I was poisoned I was dead drunk and could not speak, as if in a dream; but I heard the deckhand argue against it and refuse to join, and he had already fled. If we wrong this man now, it will make others unwilling to choose the good.” Master Wang had Li Ya beaten forty strokes and thrown in jail, and sentenced him to beheading under the law. The baggage and the original silver were sent under two guards, together with Xu Ding, straight to Tiansheng’s family. Li Ya was executed in the winter of the following year. The deckhand Weng Ya later gave up the boat and went back to farming, and came to live in fair comfort. Li Ya plotted against others and hastened his own death; Weng Ya spoke against it and kept his family whole. As the proverb says, “Good is repaid with good, evil with evil”—truly, it is no empty saying.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The translation uses 'Master Wang' for '王爷'. While understandable, 'Prince Wang' or 'His Highness' is more accurate for the Ming dynasty title 'wangye', which denotes a royal prince rather than a generic master.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Prince Wang held the hearing. Seeing the affair was out and denial useless, Ya confessed to everything—and dragged in the deckhand as an accomplice. Xu Ding said: “When I was poisoned I was dead drunk and could not speak, as if in a dream; but I heard the deckhand argue against it and refuse to join, and he had already fled. If we wrong this man now, it will make others unwilling to choose the good.” Prince Wang had Li Ya beaten forty strokes and thrown in jail, and sentenced him to beheading under the law. The baggage and the original silver were sent under two guards, together with Xu Ding, straight to Tiansheng’s family. Li Ya was executed in the winter of the following year. The deckhand Weng Ya later gave up the boat and went back to farming, and came to live in fair comfort. Li Ya plotted against others and hastened his own death; Weng Ya spoke against it and kept his family whole. As the proverb says, “Good is repaid with good, evil with evil”—truly, it is no empty saying.

### 146. [9/10] 第九類 謀財騙 Category 9 · Scheming for Wealth — 商伙财反丧财 (cat-09-s00) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 张沛，徽州休宁人，大贾也。财本数千两，在瓜州买棉花三百餘担。歙县刘兴，乃孤苦茕民，一向出外肩挑买卖，十餘载未归家，苦积财本七十餘两，亦到此店买棉花。二人同府异县，沛一相见，乡语相同，认为梓里，意气相投，有如兄弟焉。棉花各买毕，同在福建省城陈四店卖，房舍与沛内外。
- **Current English**:
  > Zhang Pei of Xiuning in Huizhou was a great merchant, with several thousand taels of capital. At Guazhou he bought more than three hundred piculs of cotton. Liu Xing of Shexian was a poor, lonely man. He had long gone out peddling with a shoulder pole and for more than ten years had not been home. By hard saving he had gathered a little over seventy taels of capital, and he too came to this shop to buy cotton. The two were of the same prefecture but different counties. The moment Pei met him, their home dialect matched; he took him for a man of his own native place. They found each other congenial and became as close as brothers. When each had finished buying his cotton, they sold it together at Chen Si’s shop in the Fujian provincial capital, lodging in adjoining rooms, one inner and one outer.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'lodging in adjoining rooms, one inner and one outer' slightly over-interprets the original '房舍与沛内外'. While likely correct contextually, a more literal rendering like 'his lodging was arranged with Pei's as inner and outer quarters' might better reflect the specific arrangement implied by '内外' without assuming they were merely 'adjoining'.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Zhang Pei of Xiuning in Huizhou was a great merchant, with several thousand taels of capital. At Guazhou he bought more than three hundred piculs of cotton. Liu Xing of Shexian was a poor, lonely man. He had long gone out peddling with a shoulder pole and for more than ten years had not been home. By hard saving he had gathered a little over seventy taels of capital, and he too came to this shop to buy cotton. The two were of the same prefecture but different counties. The moment Pei met him, their home dialect matched; he took him for a man of his own native place. They found each other congenial and became as close as brothers. When each had finished buying his cotton, they sold it together at Chen Si’s shop in the Fujian provincial capital, where his lodging was arranged with Pei's as inner and outer quarters.

### 147. [9/10] 第九類 謀財騙 Category 9 · Scheming for Wealth — 商伙财反丧财 (cat-09-s00) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 是日，客伙与地方众等，岂止数千人看，兴之廉耻尽丧。后数日，沛归，谓兴曰：“为你这贼，苦我往返海澄一遭。今幸原银仍在，我也不计较你，今后当做好人。若如汝见，定要呈官究治。”兴曰：“须念乡里二字。”曰：“若说乡里，正被乡里误矣！我念前日久与之情，不计较你，你急前去。”兴曰：“我银乞还我。”但兴银却被众等拿去。沛因叫众等“拿还他，我自谢你。”众人曰：“这贼若告官论，命也难保。今不计较，反敢图赖！”众人又欲殴他，沛劝乃止。谓兴曰：“你心不良，反为若此，今反害己，不足恤也。但我自推心，将银五两与你作盘缠。”兴且感且泣，抱头鼠窜而去。
- **Current English**:
  > That day fellow merchants and local people—no fewer than several thousand—came to look; Xing’s shame was complete. A few days later Pei returned and said to Xing: “Because of you, you thief, I had a bitter round trip to Haicheng. Luckily the original silver is still here. I will not settle scores with you. From now on be a decent man. If I treated you as you treated me, I would certainly file a charge and have you punished.” Xing said: “You must remember the two words ‘fellow townsman.’” Pei said: “If we speak of fellow townsmen—I was ruined precisely by a fellow townsman! I remember our old days together, so I will not press it. Get out at once.” Xing said: “Please give me my silver back.” But Xing’s silver had been taken by the crowd. Pei then told them, “Give it back to him; I will thank you myself.” The people said: “If this thief were taken to court, even his life would be hard to keep. You do not even press charges, and he still dares to claim it!” They wanted to beat him again; Pei talked them into stopping. He said to Xing: “Your heart was bad, and it came to this; now you have harmed yourself, and you are not worth pity. But I will open my own heart and give you five taels as travel money.” Xing was grateful and in tears; he covered his head and scurried away.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'If I treated you as you treated me' is a slight interpretive expansion of '若如汝见' (literally: if according to your view/way), though the meaning is contextually sound.

### 148. [9/10] 第九類 謀財騙 Category 9 · Scheming for Wealth — 商伙财反丧财 (cat-09-s00) `[note]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 噫！久旱甘雨，他乡故知。客于外者，一见乡里，朝夕与游，即成绸缪之交，有如兄弟者，人之情也。沛之与兴，以同郡乡人，又同兹贸易，与之共店托处，亦处旅者之势然也。何兴之包藏祸心，同室操戈，利其财而盗之？彼之暗渡荡船，自谓得计；岂知天理昭彰。奸盗不容，卒之擒获丛殴，噬脐无及，数十年苦积七十金，一旦失之。图未得之财，丧已获之利，何其愚也！予深有慨焉，故笔之，以为奸贪丧心者戒，而因告商者之宜慎，勿如乡里之为盗者误也。
- **Current English**:
  > Note: Ah! Sweet rain after a long drought; an old acquaintance in a strange land. A man sojourning away from home, once he meets a fellow townsman, goes about with him morning and evening and soon forms a close bond, like brothers—that is human feeling. Pei and Xing were men of the same prefecture, in the same trade, sharing lodgings at the same shop; that too is how travelers are placed. How could Xing hide a vicious heart, turn the blade on a man under the same roof, and steal for the sake of the money? He slipped onto a river boat in secret and thought himself clever; he did not know Heaven’s justice is plain. Treachery and theft are not tolerated. In the end he was caught and beaten by the crowd, too late for regret. Seventy taels painfully saved over several decades were lost in a single morning. He schemed for wealth he did not yet have, and lost the profit he already held—how foolish! I am deeply moved, so I write this down as a warning to the treacherous and greedy who have lost their conscience, and to tell merchants they should be careful, and not be taken in by a “fellow townsman” who is in fact a thief.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'He slipped onto a river boat in secret' slightly obscures the specific idiom '暗渡荡船', which implies secretly boarding a drifting/empty boat to escape or flee, rather than just 'slipping onto'.
  - The translation 'lost his conscience' for '喪心' is acceptable but could be more idiomatic as 'gone mad with greed' or 'lost their minds' to better capture the moral condemnation of the original.
  - The phrase 'taken in by a fellow townsman' is good, but the original '勿如乡里之为盗者误也' carries a stronger warning against being deceived *by* the very nature of such relationships, not just being taken in.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Note: Ah! Sweet rain after a long drought; an old acquaintance in a strange land. A man sojourning away from home, once he meets a fellow townsman, goes about with him morning and evening and soon forms a close bond, like brothers—that is human feeling. Pei and Xing were men of the same prefecture, in the same trade, sharing lodgings at the same shop; that too is how travelers are placed. How could Xing hide a vicious heart, turn the blade on a man under the same roof, and steal for the sake of the money? He secretly boarded a drifting boat, thinking himself clever; he did not know Heaven’s justice is plain. Treachery and theft are not tolerated. In the end he was caught and beaten by the crowd, too late for regret. Seventy taels painfully saved over several decades were lost in a single morning. He schemed for wealth he did not yet have, and lost the profit he already held—how foolish! I am deeply moved, so I write this down as a warning to the treacherous and greedy who have gone mad with lust for gain, and to tell merchants they should be careful, lest they be deceived by a "fellow townsman" who is in fact a thief.

### 150. [9/10] 第九類 謀財騙 Category 9 · Scheming for Wealth — 傲气致讼伤财命 (cat-09-s01) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 魏邦材，广东客人，富冠一省。为人骄傲非常，辄夸巨富。出外为商，无人可入其目。一日，在湖州买丝一百担，转往本省去卖，在杭州讨大船，共客商二十餘人同船。因风有阻，在富阳县五七日。其仆屡天早争先炊饭，船中往来，略不如意，辄与众斗口。众皆以伙计相聚日知，况材亢傲而相让之，其仆亦倚主势，日与众忤。在邦材，当抑仆而慰同侪可也；反党其仆，屡出言不逊曰：“你这一起下等下流，哪一个来与我和？”动以千金为言。又曰：“一船之货，我一人可买。”如此言者数次，众皆不堪，大恨之。
- **Current English**:
  > Wei Bangcai was a Guangdong merchant, the richest in the province. He was extraordinarily arrogant and always boasted of his great wealth. When he went out to trade, no one was worth a glance. One day he bought a hundred piculs of silk in Huzhou, to take back to his own province to sell. In Hangzhou he hired a large boat; more than twenty merchants shared it. The wind held them up, and they stayed in Fuyang County five to seven days. His servants always rushed to cook first at dawn; as they came and went on the boat, if anything was slightly not to their liking they wrangled with the others. The rest, as fellow merchants thrown together for days and knowing how things stood—and besides because Bangcai was so high and mighty—yielded to them. The servants also leaned on their master’s power and clashed with people daily. Bangcai ought to have checked his servants and soothed his companions; instead he took his servants’ side, and more than once spoke insolently: “You pack of low, inferior types—which of you will come and match me?” He always talked in thousands of taels. He also said: “I could buy this whole boat’s cargo by myself.” He said such things several times. Everyone found it unbearable and hated him deeply.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'You pack of low, inferior types—which of you will come and match me?' slightly distorts the original rhetorical force. The Chinese '哪一个来与我和' implies a challenge to anyone who dares to stand up to him or argue with him, not necessarily to 'match' him in status or wealth. A more precise rendering would be 'Who here dares to stand up to me?' or 'Which one of you has the nerve to take me on?'
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Wei Bangcai was a merchant from Guangdong, the richest man in the province. He was extraordinarily arrogant and always boasted of his vast wealth. When he traveled for business, no one was worth a glance. One day, he bought a hundred piculs of silk in Huzhou to take back to his home province to sell. In Hangzhou, he hired a large boat, and over twenty merchants shared it. Due to wind delays, they stayed in Fuyang County for five or seven days. His servant always rushed to cook first at dawn; whenever he came and went on the boat and found anything slightly unsatisfactory, he would quarrel with the others. The other merchants, having been together as fellow travelers for days and knowing how things stood—and besides, because Bangcai was so high and mighty—yielded to him. The servants also leaned on their master's power and clashed with everyone daily. Bangcai should have restrained his servant and soothed his companions; instead, he sided with his servant and repeatedly spoke insolently: "You pack of low, inferior scum—who among you dares to stand up to me?" He would often speak in terms of thousands of taels. He also said, "I could buy this whole boat's cargo by myself." He said such things several times. Everyone found it unbearable and hated him deeply.

### 151. [9/10] 第九類 謀財騙 Category 9 · Scheming for Wealth — 傲气致讼伤财命 (cat-09-s01) `[body]`

- **Current English**:
  > The two men sued each other for about a year. Bangcai’s remaining silk was all spent. He called an elder brother to come help with the suit; the brother brought more than five hundred taels, which were also mostly used up. Bangcai then fell ill at the inn. The family sent a paternal uncle to come see him. The man was an honest, kindly elder. He asked how it had started, and only then learned that the nephew’s arrogant ways had brought this on. The merchants told the whole affair and wanted a peaceful settlement: each side would put up a hundred taels to settle with the officials, and fifty taels would be taken from that for Bangcai as travel money so he could head home. Bangcai headed home, thought of how much goods and money he had taken out in his trading days, and now was returning empty-handed; he could not bear the anger and gloom. He was also mocked and scolded by the whole household, which only added to his sick rage. Before long a carbuncle broke out; in a few months he died.
- **Identified Issues**:

### 153. [9/10] 第九類 謀財騙 Category 9 · Scheming for Wealth — 高抬重价反失利 (cat-09-s03) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 于定志，云南西河县人。为人心贪性执，冒昧于利。一日，买栀子往四川处卖，得银八十餘两。复买当归、川芎，往江西樟树卖，每担止着本脚银二两六钱。到时，归、芎虽缺，然比前价稍落些。牙人代发当归，十两一担；川芎六两一担。定志怒责牙人曰：“前日十二两价，如何减许多？”牙人辩曰：“若到二三担，则可依前价；今到二十餘担，若从前价，何以服行情？公欲重价，凭公发别店卖之，何必怒焉？”定志与牙角口，旁有一客伙张淳者，劝曰：“公货获利三倍，当要见机。倘价若落，未免有失渡无船之悔矣。”定志坚执不听。
- **Current English**:
  > Yu Dingzhi was a man of Xihe County in Yunnan. He was greedy at heart and stubborn by nature, rash in the pursuit of profit. One day he bought gardenia, took it to Sichuan to sell, and got more than eighty taels. He then bought danggui and chuanxiong, to sell at Zhangshu in Jiangxi; each picul had cost him only two taels six qian, freight included. When he arrived, danggui and chuanxiong were still scarce, but the price had fallen a little from before. The broker sold danggui for him at ten taels a picul, and chuanxiong at six taels a picul. Dingzhi angrily blamed the broker: “The other day the price was twelve taels—why cut so much?” The broker answered: “If two or three piculs had arrived, we could follow the old price. Now more than twenty piculs have come; if we used the old price, how would that match the market? If you want a high price, take it to another shop yourself and sell—why get angry?” Dingzhi wrangled with the broker. A fellow merchant standing by, Zhang Chun, advised: “Your goods already triple the money; you should watch the moment. If the price falls, you will regret missing the ferry when no boat is left.” Dingzhi held firm and would not listen.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'missing the ferry when no boat is left' for 失渡无船 is slightly interpretive; a more literal rendering like 'regret missing the crossing when there are no boats' might better preserve the classical idiom's imagery, though the current version is clear.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Yu Dingzhi was from Xihe County in Yunnan. He was greedy at heart and stubborn by nature, rash in pursuing profit. One day he bought gardenias, took them to Sichuan to sell, and received over eighty taels. He then bought danggui and chuanxiong to sell at Zhangshu in Jiangxi; each picul cost him only two taels six qian, freight included. Upon arrival, although danggui and chuanxiong were still scarce, the price had fallen somewhat compared to before. The broker sold the danggui for him at ten taels a picul and the chuanxiong at six taels a picul. Dingzhi angrily blamed the broker: 'The other day the price was twelve taels—why cut so much?' The broker replied: 'If only two or three piculs had arrived, we could have followed the old price. Now that over twenty piculs have come, if we used the old price, how would that align with market conditions? If you want a higher price, take it to another shop yourself and sell—why get angry?' Dingzhi wrangled with the broker. A fellow merchant standing by, Zhang Chun, advised: 'Your goods have already tripled your money; you should watch the moment. If the price falls further, you will regret missing the crossing when there are no boats left.' Dingzhi held firm and would not listen.

### 154. [9/10] 第九類 謀財騙 Category 9 · Scheming for Wealth — 高抬重价反失利 (cat-09-s03) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 数日后，到有当归三四担，牙人发价十两卖讫。淳又劝之曰：“此客已卖十两价耳，公何不卖也？”彼亦不听。后又二客人，有十五担到，牙人发价七两，亦卖讫。过数日，又有十餘担来，止卖四两。定志暗悔无及，众客又背地代他扼腕。定志又坐一月餘，价落货贱，与牙人不合，遂转发到福建建宁府，止卖三两七钱一担，比樟树价又减，更废船脚又多。定志自恨命薄，不当赚钱。人谓其非命薄也，乃心高也；非挫时也，乃过贪也。故笔之，以为嗜利不饱者鉴。
- **Current English**:
  > A few days later three or four piculs of danggui arrived; the broker sold them at ten taels and they were gone. Chun advised him again: “That merchant already sold at ten taels—why do you not sell?” He still would not listen. Later two more merchants arrived with fifteen piculs; the broker sold them at seven taels, and they too were gone. After a few more days, another ten-odd piculs came, and sold at only four taels. Dingzhi secretly regretted too late; the other merchants also wrung their wrists for him behind his back. Dingzhi sat another month and more; prices fell and the goods grew cheap. He fell out with the broker, so he shipped the lot on to Jianning Prefecture in Fujian, and sold at only three taels seven qian a picul—lower still than the Zhangshu price, and more boat freight wasted besides. Dingzhi cursed his thin luck, that he was not meant to make money. People said it was not thin luck but a proud heart; not bad timing but too much greed. So I write this down as a mirror for those whose thirst for profit is never filled.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'wrung their wrists' for 扼腕 is a literal calque of the idiom. While understandable, it lacks the natural English equivalent (e.g., 'wailed in frustration' or 'sighed deeply') that conveys the Ming vernacular sense of deep regret without sounding like a physical action.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > A few days later, three or four piculs of danggui arrived; the broker sold them at ten taels and they were gone. Chun advised him again: "That merchant already sold at ten taels—why do you not sell?" He still would not listen. Later two more merchants arrived with fifteen piculs; the broker sold them at seven taels, and they too were gone. After a few more days, another ten-odd piculs came, and sold at only four taels. Dingzhi secretly regretted it too late; the other merchants also sighed deeply for him behind his back. Dingzhi sat another month and more; prices fell and the goods grew cheap. He fell out with the broker, so he shipped the lot on to Jianning Prefecture in Fujian, and sold at only three taels seven qian a picul—lower still than the Zhangshu price, and more boat freight wasted besides. Dingzhi cursed his thin luck, that he was not meant to make money. People said it was not thin luck but a proud heart; not bad timing but too much greed. So I write this down as a mirror for those whose thirst for profit is never filled.

### 155. [9/10] 第九類 謀財騙 Category 9 · Scheming for Wealth — 高抬重价反失利 (cat-09-s03) `[note]`

  > 按：商为利而奔驰南北，谁不欲广收多获？特遇时而倍得其利，便可见机发脱。何乃贪黩无厌，至失机会而后扼腕，何益哉？甚矣，贪之为害也！不知凡物贱极征贵，贵极征贱，必无极而不返之理。此阴阳消长之数、造化否泰之机，往往皆然。岂可违时而遂贪心乎？是以从古君子，以不贪为宝。
- **Current English**:
  > Note: Merchants race north and south for profit; who does not want a wide take and a big haul? But when the times let you double what you put in, you should see the moment and sell. Why be endlessly greedy, and only wring your wrists after the chance is gone—what good is that? How harmful greed is! They do not know that when a thing is extremely cheap it trends toward dear, and when extremely dear it trends toward cheap; there is no extreme that does not turn back. This is the waxing and waning of yin and yang, the rise and fall in creation’s working—so it always is. How can you go against the time to feed a greedy heart? That is why gentlemen from of old have taken not being greedy as their treasure.
- **Identified Issues**:

### 156. [9/10] 第十類 盜劫騙 Category 10 · Theft and Robbery Swindles — 公子租屋劫寡妇 (cat-10-s00) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 会城中，每逢科试之年，各府举子到者极多。不论大小房屋，举子俱出重租，暂僦以居。东街王寡妇，其先得丹穴擅利，数世积镪巨万，名闻于人。止生二子，一弱冠，一垂髫。内止一丫头，外用一仆代管家，一小厮供役使，不过五六人家口。其厅堂高敞，房舍深广。其外厢每科租与举子居，常收厚利。
- **Current English**:
  > In the provincial capital, every examination year, candidates from the prefectures arrived in great numbers. They paid heavy rents for houses large and small, just to lodge for a short stay. Widow Wang of East Street came of a family that had grown rich from a cinnabar mine; several generations had piled up a fortune of tens of thousands, and her name was widely known. She had only two sons, one just come of age, one still a small boy. Inside there was only one maid; outside, one servant served as steward and a boy ran errands—no more than five or six mouths in the house. The halls were high and spacious, the rooms deep and wide. Each examination year she rented the outer wings to candidates, and often took in a handsome profit.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'came of a family that had grown rich' slightly dilutes the original's emphasis on Wang herself being the current beneficiary ('其先得丹穴擅利') who inherited and managed the wealth. A more precise rendering would be 'Wang... had long held the exclusive rights to a cinnabar mine,' or similar, to reflect her active role in the fortune.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > In the provincial capital, every examination year, candidates from the prefectures arrived in great numbers. They paid heavy rents for houses large and small, just to lodge for a short stay. Widow Wang of East Street had long held the exclusive rights to a cinnabar mine; over several generations she had piled up a fortune of tens of thousands of taels, and her name was widely known. She had only two sons, one just come of age, one still a small boy. Inside there was only one maid; outside, one servant served as steward and a boy ran errands—no more than five or six mouths in the house. The halls were high and spacious, the rooms deep and wide. Each examination year she rented the outer wings to candidates, and often took in a handsome profit.
