- **Chinese Source**:
  > 辛卯七月初，举子纷至。忽有二家仆，冠服齐楚，来择屋居。王管家引其看左右厅房，皆清幽洁净。二家仆曰：“此屋光明宽大，可中公子意。我全租之，不可再租他人。敢问租金多少？”王管家曰：“往年众人共租，租金常二十两。今你一家租，人少，不乱杂，只十五两亦可。”二家仆还十二两，即以现银付讫。一仆出引公子，乘四轿，带四仆并一小厮来。行李五六担，皆精好物件。到即以土仪送家主，又值银二三两。王寡妇曰：“往年举子送人事皆淡薄，今这公子真方家手面。”次日，命管家排大筵席，敬请公子，二子出陪。公子放怀欢饮，二更方散。
- **Current English**:
  > Early in the seventh month of a xinmao year, candidates poured in. Suddenly two family servants, neatly and smartly dressed, came to choose rooms. Steward Wang showed them the halls and rooms to left and right; all were quiet, secluded, and clean. The two servants said: “These rooms are bright and spacious; they will suit our young master. We will rent the whole place; you must not rent to anyone else. May we ask how much the rent is?” Steward Wang said: “In past years, when many men shared the rent, it was often twenty taels. Now your one household is renting—fewer people, not a mixed crowd—fifteen taels will do.” The two servants bargained it down to twelve taels and paid the silver on the spot. One servant went out and brought the young master, who came in a four-bearer sedan with four servants and a boy. There were five or six loads of luggage, all fine things. On arrival he sent local gifts to the householder, worth another two or three taels. Widow Wang said: “In past years the candidates’ gifts were always meager; this young master truly has a great house’s style.” The next day she ordered the steward to lay on a large feast, invited the young master with due respect, and had her two sons come out to keep him company. The young master drank freely; they did not break up until the second watch.

- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Early in the seventh month of a xinmao year, candidates poured in. Suddenly two family servants, neatly and smartly dressed, came to choose rooms. Steward Wang showed them the halls and rooms to left and right; all were quiet, secluded, and clean. The two servants said: "These rooms are bright and spacious; they will suit our young master. We will rent the whole place; you must not rent to anyone else. May we ask how much the rent is?" Steward Wang said: "In past years, when many men shared the rent, it was often twenty taels. Now your one household is renting—fewer people, not a mixed crowd—fifteen taels will do." The two servants bargained it down to twelve taels and paid the silver on the spot. One servant went out and brought the young master, who came in a four-bearer sedan with four servants and a boy. There were five or six loads of luggage, all fine things. On arrival he sent local gifts to the householder, worth another two or three taels. Widow Wang said: "In past years the candidates' gifts were always meager; this young master truly has the bearing of a real master." The next day she ordered the steward to lay on a large feast, invited the young master with due respect, and had her two sons come out to keep him company. The young master drank freely; they did not break up until the second watch.

- **Chinese Source**:
  > 董荣，山东人也。往南京廊下邓铺中买丝绸三匹，价银四两四钱。以天平对定，只差银色，讲议未成。忽一人骑白马，戴笼巾，穿青绢双摆，亦来铺买绸。邓店以绸与看。其人将董荣的绸来看：曰：“吾为你二家折衷。”叫董荣再添银二钱，荣意亦肯添。其人接银过手看，一跳上马，加鞭而行，马走如飞。荣忙赶上，过一巷，转一弯，其人与马俱不见踪。无奈，再至邓铺，谓其与棍相套，互争扭打。
- **Current English**:
  > Dong Rong was a Shandong man. He went to Deng’s shop in the Langxia corridors of Nanjing to buy three bolts of silk, at a price of four taels four qian. They had already settled the weighing on the balance; they only differed on the purity of the silver, and the deal was not yet made. Suddenly a man riding a white horse, wearing a cage-hat and a dark silk robe, also came to the shop to buy silk. Deng’s shop showed him silk. The man took Dong Rong’s silk to look at and said: “I will split the difference for you two.” He told Dong Rong to add two qian more; Rong was willing to add it. The man took the silver in hand to look, then sprang onto the horse, laid on the whip, and the horse flew. Rong hurried after him, went down an alley, turned a corner—man and horse were both gone. He had no choice but to go back to Deng’s shop, saying the shop was in cahoots with the crook; they wrangled and came to blows.

- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Dong Rong was a man from Shandong. He went to Deng's shop in the Langxia corridor of Nanjing to buy three bolts of silk at a price of four taels and four qian. They had already settled the weighing on the balance but differed on the purity of the silver, so the deal was not yet made. Suddenly, a man riding a white horse, wearing a cage-hat and a dark silk robe, also came to the shop to buy silk. Deng showed him some silk. The man took Dong Rong's silk to look at and said, "I will split the difference for you two." He told Dong Rong to add two qian more; Rong was willing to do so. The man took the silver in hand to inspect it, then sprang onto his horse, laid on the whip, and the horse flew off. Rong hurried after him, went down an alley, turned a corner—but both the man and the horse were gone. With no choice, he returned to Deng's shop, claiming the shopkeeper was in cahoots with the crook; they wrangled and came to blows.

### 161. [9/10] 第十類 盜劫騙 Category 10 · Theft and Robbery Swindles — 京城店中响马贼 (cat-10-s02) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 忽巡街刘御史到，二人皆拦街口告。御史带回衙，拘其左邻右舍来审。邻舍曰：“先是荣入铺买绸，只争银色未成。一棍忽骑马至，亦称买绸，自言为彼二家折衷，叫荣添银。棍把其银入手，一跳上马而去，荣忙赶未见。以故二人争打，告在天台。谅此棍正是响马贼，必非通同店家作弊者。”刘爷曰：“邻佑所证是实，此非店家通同者。但在伊店而遭失脱，合令邓店补还银二两二钱，董荣亦自认二两二钱。”发出依处，彼此无罪。
- **Current English**:
  > Suddenly Censor Liu, on street patrol, arrived; both men blocked his way in the street to complain. The censor took them back to his yamen and summoned the neighbors on either side to hear the case. The neighbors said: “First Rong came into the shop to buy silk; they only differed on the silver’s purity and had not closed. A crook suddenly rode up, also claiming to buy silk, said he would split the difference for the two of them, and told Rong to add silver. The crook got the silver in his hand, sprang onto the horse and left; Rong hurried after and saw nothing. That is why the two fought and complained before Your Honor. This crook was surely a mounted highwayman; he cannot have been in league with the shop.” Master Liu said: “The neighbors’ testimony is true; this was not a shop collusion. But the loss happened in his shop, so Deng’s shop should make good two taels two qian, and Dong Rong should also bear two taels two qian himself.” They were sent out under this settlement; neither was found guilty.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'split the difference' is a modern idiom that slightly softens the con man's manipulative tone; 'mediate' or 'act as an intermediary' might be more neutral and period-appropriate.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Suddenly Censor Liu arrived on street patrol; both men blocked his way in the street to complain. The censor took them back to his yamen and summoned the neighbors on either side to hear the case. The neighbors said: “First Rong came into the shop to buy silk; they only differed on the silver’s purity and had not closed. A crook suddenly rode up, also claiming to buy silk, said he would act as an intermediary for the two of them, and told Rong to add silver. The crook got the silver in his hand, sprang onto the horse and left; Rong hurried after but saw nothing. That is why the two fought and complained before Your Honor. This crook was surely a mounted highwayman; he cannot have been in league with the shop.” Master Liu said: “The neighbors’ testimony is true; this was not a shop collusion. But the loss happened in his shop, so Deng’s shop should make good two taels two qian, and Dong Rong should also bear two taels two qian himself.” They were sent out under this settlement; neither was found guilty.

### 162. [9/10] 第十一類 搶劫騙 Category 11 · Robbery Swindles — 私打印记占铺陈 (cat-11-s00) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 乡有尤刁民者，侮法健讼，渔猎下民。人闻其刁风，莫不畏而远之。一日，往府搭船，已先入船坐。后搭船者群至，萍水相逢，彼此各不相识，船中对坐漫谈。忽讲及按院拿刁民事。内有姓丘后生，不知尤刁民之在船也，与众曰：“闻此时本县唯尤五最刁，凡与人暂处，无不被其骗害者。若得按院除了此人，民亦安生。”尤五心中冷笑，谓“吾与尔何干，既扬我刁，又愿按院除我？此人若不白骗他一场，枉得此刁名也。”见丘生所带铺陈甚好，即取一木印，挨近其毡条白处，私打一印号于中。
- **Current English**:
  > In the country there was a man known as You the Shyster, who flouted the law, was a relentless litigant, and preyed on ordinary folk. Word of his shifty ways spread, and everyone feared him and kept their distance. One day he went to the prefectural city by boat and was the first aboard, already seated. The later passengers came crowding in — strangers thrown together by chance, none knowing any of the others — and they sat facing one another in the boat, chatting idly. The talk happened to turn to the touring censor's arrests of litigious troublemakers. Among them was a young man surnamed Qiu, who did not know You the Shyster was on the boat. He said to the group: “I hear that in our county these days You Wu is the worst shyster of all. No one who spends even a short time around him escapes being cheated and hurt. If the touring censor would only get rid of the man, the people could live in peace.” You Wu laughed coldly to himself: “What is it to you, that you trumpet my shiftiness and even wish the censor would be rid of me? If I don't swindle this fellow out of something for nothing, my name as a shyster is wasted on me.” Noticing that young Qiu's bedding roll was a fine one, he took out a wooden seal, edged up to a light patch on the felt wrapping, and secretly stamped a mark on it.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The translation uses 'shyster' for both 刁民 and 棍，which is acceptable but slightly repetitive; the guide suggests varying terms like 'swindler' or 'trickster' for 棍 to avoid robotic overuse.
  - The phrase 'edged up to a light patch on the felt wrapping' is slightly awkward; 'slipped close to a light-colored spot on the felt cover' might flow better while maintaining accuracy.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > In the countryside there was a man known as You the Shyster, who flouted the law, was a relentless litigant, and preyed on ordinary folk. Word of his shifty ways spread, and everyone feared him and kept their distance. One day he went to the prefectural city by boat and was the first aboard, already seated. The later passengers came crowding in — strangers thrown together by chance, none knowing any of the others — and they sat facing one another in the boat, chatting idly. The talk happened to turn to the touring censor's arrests of litigious troublemakers. Among them was a young man surnamed Qiu, who did not know You the Shyster was on the boat. He said to the group: "I hear that in our county these days You Wu is the worst swindler of all. No one who spends even a short time around him escapes being cheated and hurt. If the touring censor would only get rid of the man, the people could live in peace." You Wu laughed coldly to himself: "What is it to you, that you trumpet my shiftiness and even wish the censor would be rid of me? If I don't swindle this fellow out of something for nothing, my name as a trickster is wasted on me." Noticing that young Qiu's bedding roll was a fine one, he took out a wooden seal, slipped close to a light-colored spot on the felt cover, and secretly stamped a mark on it.

### 164. [9/10] 第十一類 搶劫騙 Category 11 · Robbery Swindles — 膏药贴眼抢元宝 (cat-11-s01) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 县城有一银匠，家颇殷实，解户领秋粮银，常托其倾煎。一日倾煎元宝，心内尚有系未透处，夜间又煮洗之。其铺门有一大缝，外可窥见其内。一棍买一大膏药，夜间潜往窥之。见其把两元宝洗讫，放于炉边，棍在外作叫痛声，呼曰：“开门！”银匠问曰：“是谁？”棍外答曰：“被赃坯打得重，求你炉边灼一膏药贴之。”银匠开门与入。棍作瘸行状，且手战呼痛，蓬头俯视，以一大膏药在炉边灼开，把两手望银匠当面一贴，即抢一元宝以逃。银匠不胜热痛，急扯下膏药，元宝已被其窃一去矣。急叫：“有贼！”且出门追赶，不知从哪路去。彷徨追过数十步，只得怅恨而归。
- **Current English**:
  > In the county town there was a silversmith whose family was fairly well off. The tax forwarders who collected the autumn grain-tax silver often entrusted him with melting it down. One day he was melting silver ingots, and, feeling there were still spots inside that had not fused through, he boiled and washed them again that night. His shop door had a wide crack in it, and from outside one could see in. A crook bought a large medicinal plaster and crept up in the night to peek. He watched the man finish washing two ingots and set them by the furnace. Then the crook, outside, began to groan in pain and cried: “Open the door!” The silversmith asked: “Who is it?” The crook answered from outside: “I've been badly beaten by a thieving brute. I beg you, let me warm a plaster at your furnace so I can put it on.” The silversmith opened the door and let him in. The crook limped along, hands trembling, groaning in pain, his hair a tangled mess, head bowed. He warmed the large plaster at the furnace until it softened, then slapped it with both hands straight onto the silversmith's face, snatched one ingot, and ran. The burning pain was more than the silversmith could stand; he tore the plaster off in a frenzy, but the ingot was already gone with the thief. He shouted “Thief!” and rushed out in pursuit, but had no idea which way the man had gone. After a few dozen paces of chasing at a loss, he could only go back, bitter and heartsick.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'tax forwarders' for 解户 is slightly awkward; 'tax collectors' or 'officials in charge of grain tax transport' would be more precise.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > In the county town there was a silversmith whose family was fairly well off. The officials responsible for transporting autumn grain-tax silver often entrusted him with melting it down. One day he was melting silver ingots, and feeling there were still spots inside that had not fused through, he boiled and washed them again that night. His shop door had a wide crack in it, and from outside one could see in. A crook bought a large medicinal plaster and crept up at night to peek. He watched the man finish washing two ingots and set them by the furnace. Then the crook, outside, began to groan in pain and cried: "Open the door!" The silversmith asked: "Who is it?" The crook answered from outside: "I've been badly beaten by a thieving brute. I beg you, let me warm a plaster at your furnace so I can put it on." The silversmith opened the door and let him in. The crook limped along, hands trembling, groaning in pain, his hair a tangled mess, head bowed. He warmed the large plaster at the furnace until it softened, then slapped it with both hands straight onto the silversmith's face, snatched one ingot, and ran. The burning pain was more than the silversmith could stand; he tore the plaster off in a frenzy, but the ingot was already gone with the thief. He shouted "Thief!" and rushed out in pursuit, but had no idea which way the man had gone. After chasing a few dozen paces in vain, he could only go back, bitter and heartsick.

### 165. [9/10] 第十一類 搶劫騙 Category 11 · Robbery Swindles — 膏药贴眼抢元宝 (cat-11-s01) `[note]`

- **Current English**:
  > Note: This crook's act — groaning in pain, begging to be let in, warming a plaster at the furnace — was truly hard to see through. But an ingot is a weighty thing; had he put it away first and only then opened the door, there would have been no loss. Let those who come after take note: anyone carrying silver must never lightly let a suspicious stranger get close.

- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Note: This crook's act — groaning in pain, begging to be let in, scorching his skin with a hot plaster by the furnace — was truly hard to see through. But an ingot is a weighty thing; had he put it away first and only then opened the door, there would have been no loss. Let those who come after take note: anyone carrying silver must never lightly let a suspicious stranger get close.

### 167. [9/10] 第十一類 搶劫騙 Category 11 · Robbery Swindles — 石灰撒眼以抢银 (cat-11-s02) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 孙滔，河南人也。常买绵布，在福建建宁府卖。一夜，在银匠王六店煎银，倾煎已讫，时对二包在桌。二人复在对银，有一盗径入其铺，将石灰撒其目。二人救目不暇，盗即将桌上所包之银拿走。滔拼命赶去，将及，盗乃丢一包于地。滔拾包归，到银铺开视之，则皆铁矣。后竟无迹可捕也。
- **Current English**:
  > Sun Tao was a man of Henan. He regularly bought cotton cloth and sold it in Jianning Prefecture in Fujian. One night he was melting silver at the shop of Wang Liu the silversmith. The melting was done, and two packets of silver sat on the table. The two men were still checking the silver when a thief walked straight into the shop and flung lime into their eyes. The two had their hands full saving their eyes, and the thief simply took the packets of silver from the table and walked off. Tao chased after him for dear life. He had nearly caught him when the thief dropped one packet on the ground. Tao picked up the packet and went back; when he opened it at the silversmith's shop, it was all iron. And in the end there was no trail to catch him by.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'two packets of silver sat on the table' slightly misses the nuance of '对' (facing each other/arranged for comparison) implied in the context of checking silver, though the meaning is clear.
  - The translation 'checking the silver' for '对银' is accurate but could be slightly more specific to the Ming context of verifying purity or weight, though 'checking' is acceptable.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Sun Tao was a man from Henan. He regularly bought cotton cloth and sold it in Jianning Prefecture, Fujian. One night, he was melting silver at the shop of Wang Liu, a silversmith. After the melting was finished, two packets of silver were placed on the table facing one another. While the two men were still comparing the silver, a thief walked straight into the shop and flung lime into their eyes. With their hands full trying to save their sight, they couldn't stop the thief from taking the packets of silver off the table and making off with them. Tao chased after him for dear life. Just as he was about to catch up, the thief dropped one packet on the ground. Tao picked it up and returned; when he opened it at the silversmith's shop, it turned out to be all iron. In the end, there was no trail left to catch him.

### 168. [9/10] 第十一類 搶劫騙 Category 11 · Robbery Swindles — 石灰撒眼以抢银 (cat-11-s02) `[note]`

- **Chinese Source**:
  > 按：语云：“贼是小人，智过君子。”诚哉是言也！其始入铺，撒灰腌人之目，致人无暇顾其财；追将近身、丢包于地，乃杜赶以脱其身也。此岂贼窥伺之机熟，而慢藏诲盗？然滔之不谨于其素，有以致之矣。鉴此惩噎，是为得之。
- **Current English**:
  > Note: As the saying goes, “A thief is a petty man, but his wits outrun a gentleman's.” How true that is. When he first came into the shop, he flung lime to burn their eyes, leaving them no time to look to their money; when the chase had nearly caught up with him, he dropped a packet on the ground — that stopped the pursuit and got him clear. Was this just a case of the thief biding his time to perfection, and of careless keeping inviting theft? Still, Tao's own habitual carelessness had brought it about. Be warned by this, as a man once choked grows careful with his food — that is the lesson to take away.
- **Identified Issues**:

- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Note: As the saying goes, “A thief is a petty man, but his wits outrun a gentleman's.” How true that is! When he first entered the shop, he flung lime to burn their eyes, leaving them no time to look after their money; when the chase was nearly upon him, he dropped a packet on the ground to block the pursuit and make off with it. Was this merely the thief perfecting his biding of time, and careless keeping inviting theft? Yet Tao’s own habitual carelessness had brought it about. Be warned by this, for just as a man who has been choked becomes careful with his food—that is the lesson to take away.

### 169. [9/10] 第十一類 搶劫騙 Category 11 · Robbery Swindles — 大解被棍白日抢 (cat-11-s03) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 王亨，南京扬州府人，是本府典吏。二考已满，该上京办事。家贫无措，措借亲朋银十馀两，独往北京，为办事使用。始到京中，在教军场边草坪中大解。方脱下裤，陡被二棍拿住，且骂且剥曰：“你这贼偷我衣物来。”即把其衣服并银一时抢去逃走。待他起来，缚裤赶之，二棍逃已远矣。亨行路日久，力已疲倦，拼死赶他不上，懊恨冲天，只得在会同馆乞借盘缠回家，另作区处。
- **Current English**:
  > Wang Heng was a man of Yangzhou Prefecture in the Nanjing region, a clerk in the prefectural office. His two terms of service were complete, and he was due to go up to the capital to be processed for a post. His family was poor and he had no funds, so he scraped together a loan of ten-odd taels of silver from relatives and friends and set out alone for Beijing, to cover the costs of the appointment. He had just arrived in the capital and was relieving himself on the grass by the drill ground. No sooner had he pulled his trousers down than two crooks seized him, cursing him as they pulled at his clothes: “You thief — you stole our clothes!” Then and there they grabbed his clothes together with his silver and ran off. By the time he got up, tied his trousers, and gave chase, the two crooks were already far away. Heng had been on the road for many days and his strength was spent; though he chased for dear life, he could not catch them. Choked with regret and rage, he had no choice but to beg a loan of travel money at the Huitong government hostel and head home, to make other arrangements later.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'to be processed for a post' is slightly stiff and bureaucratic; 'to attend to official business' or 'to handle duties in the capital' would better capture the Ming context of '办事'.
  - 'Tied his trousers' is a literal translation of '缚裤'; while accurate, it sounds slightly awkward in English. 'Fastened his trousers' or 'pulled up his trousers' flows more naturally.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Wang Heng was from Yangzhou Prefecture in the Nanjing region, serving as a clerk in the prefectural office. Having completed his two terms, he was due to go up to the capital to attend to official business. Poor and with no funds of his own, he borrowed over ten taels of silver from relatives and friends and set out alone for Beijing to cover the costs of his appointment. Upon arriving in the capital, he relieved himself on the grass near the drill ground. No sooner had he pulled down his trousers than two crooks seized him, cursing him as they stripped his clothes: "You thief—you stole our clothes!" They immediately snatched his clothes along with his silver and fled. By the time he got up, fastened his trousers, and gave chase, the two crooks were already far away. Heng had been traveling for many days and was exhausted; though he chased them for dear life, he could not catch them. Choked with regret and rage, he had no choice but to beg for travel money at the Huitong government hostel and head home to make other arrangements later.

### 170. [9/10] 第十二類 在船騙 Category 12 · On-the-Boat Swindles — 船载家人行李逃 (cat-12-s00) `[body]`

- **Chinese Source**:
  > 倪典史以吏员出身，家实巨富。初受官，将赴新任，在京置买器用什物、珍玩缎匹，色色美丽，装作行李六担。打点俱备，先遣三个家人押往江边搭船，以一家人在船中守护，其二人复归。次日，同倪典史大伙人俱到江边寻船，并不见前船；其守船家人不知载在何去，知被贼艄所拐矣。

Newly appointed and about to leave for his post, he bought household goods, curios, and bolts of silk in the capital—every piece fine and beautiful—and packed them as six loads of luggage. When all was ready, he first sent three servants to the riverbank to hire a boat. One stayed on board to watch the luggage; the other two came back. The next day Assistant Magistrate Ni and the rest of the party went to the river to find the boat, and the earlier boat was nowhere to be seen. No one knew where the watching servant had been carried off to; they knew a rogue boatman had made off with them.

- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Assistant Magistrate Ni had risen from the clerical ranks; his family was in fact extremely wealthy. Newly appointed and about to leave for his post, he bought household goods, curios, and bolts of silk in the capital—every piece fine and beautiful—and packed them as six loads of luggage. When all was ready, he first sent three servants to the riverbank to hire a boat. One stayed on board to watch the luggage; the other two came back. The next day Assistant Magistrate Ni and the rest of the party went to the river to find the boat, but the earlier boat was nowhere to be seen. No one knew where the watching servant had been taken; they knew a rogue boatman had swindled them away with the goods.

  > 按：娶妾于妻岳之家，既在店成亲，又送别登舟，可谓极稳矣。谁知在船后，夜复能逃？故在外娶妾，不唯审择外家，兼亦宜审媒人居止，及靠店家一同核实，方可无失。然大抵不及娶本地人女，为更稳也。
- **Current English**:

Who knew that later, on the boat, she could still slip away at night? So when you take a concubine away from home, you should not only look into the girl’s family; you should also look into where the matchmaker lives, and have the innkeeper join in checking the facts—only then will there be no loss. But on the whole it is still safer to take a local girl.

Who knew that later, on the boat, she could still slip away at night? Thus, when taking a concubine from outside, one must not only scrutinize the girl’s family but also investigate where the matchmaker lives, and have the innkeeper join in verifying the facts to avoid loss. Still, generally speaking, it is safer to take a local girl.

### 173. [9/10] 第十二類 在船騙 Category 12 · On-the-Boat Swindles — 买铜物被艄谋死 (cat-12-s02) `[note]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 按：溪河本险危之地，舵公多蠢暴之徒。若带实银在身，须深藏严防。或带铜器铅锡等物，镇重类银，须明与说之，开与见之，以免其垂涎，方保安全。不然，逐金丸以弹雀，指薏苡为明珠，其不来奸人之睥睨者几希。若维仇之能报，犹幸子达之得其根脚也。使非因写船者以穷其舵公，何以歼罪人而殄厥慝乎？然诛逆何如保躬？死偿何如生还？故出行而带重物者，宜借鉴于斯而慎之密之，其永无失矣。
- **Current English**:
  > Note: Streams and rivers are dangerous places by nature, and helmsmen are often coarse, violent men. If you carry ready silver on you, you must hide it deep and watch it strictly. If you carry copper, lead, tin, and the like—heavy things that resemble silver—you must tell them plainly, open the loads and show them, so they will not drool, and only then will you be safe. Otherwise it is like shooting sparrows with gold pellets, or letting coix seeds pass for pearls: few will escape a villain’s greedy eye. As for Siwei’s feud being avenged, it was still luck that Zida got the man’s origin. If they had not used the man who booked the boat to track down the helmsman, how could they have wiped out the guilty and cut off the evil? But punishing the wicked is no match for keeping yourself safe; a death repaid is no match for coming home alive. So those who travel and carry heavy goods should take a lesson from this, be careful and keep things close, and they will never come to grief.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The translation of 'Siwei' and 'Zida' as proper names is likely a hallucination or misinterpretation of the text's context. The original Chinese '维仇之能报，犹幸子达之得其根脚也' uses '维' (wei) as a particle or part of a phrase meaning 'only if', and '子达' (zi da) likely refers to 'the son who succeeded/arrived' or is a generic reference to the protagonist's success in finding the root cause, not specific names like 'Siwei' and 'Zida'. Without prior context identifying these characters, treating them as proper nouns distorts the general moral lesson.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Note: Streams and rivers are inherently dangerous places, and helmsmen are often coarse, violent men. If you carry ready silver on you, you must hide it deep and watch it strictly. If you carry copper, lead, tin, and the like—heavy things that resemble silver—you must tell them plainly, open the loads and show them, so they will not drool, and only then will you be safe. Otherwise it is like shooting sparrows with gold pellets, or letting coix seeds pass for pearls: few will escape a villain’s greedy eye. As for avenging the feud, it was still fortunate that the one who succeeded found the root of the matter. Had they not used the man who booked the boat to track down the helmsman, how could they have wiped out the guilty and cut off the evil? But punishing the wicked is no match for keeping yourself safe; a death repaid is no match for coming home alive. So those who travel and carry heavy goods should take a lesson from this, be careful and keep things close, and they will never come to grief.

### 174. [9/10] 第十二類 在船騙 Category 12 · On-the-Boat Swindles — 带镜船中引谋害 (cat-12-s03) `[note]`

  > 按：镐本膏粱之子，以纵性为快，以夸口为高，哪知世路之险？若非满起心明，轻以二命付鱼腹耳。凡远行者，主若疏满，得一谨密家人，亦大有益。故旅以丧童仆为厉，以得童仆为吉。圣人系旅之义大矣哉。

If Man Qi had not had a clear mind, two lives would have been lightly given to the fishes’ bellies. Whenever you travel far, if the master is careless and slack, getting one close, careful servant is also a great benefit. So in the Travel hexagram, losing a servant is an omen of trouble, and gaining a servant is auspicious. How great is the Sage’s teaching on Travel!

A more active phrasing like 'would have been cast into the fish-bellies' might better capture the suddenness of the fate.

Had Man Qi not had a clear mind, two lives would have been cast into the fish-bellies. Whenever one travels far, if the master is careless and slack, securing even one close, careful servant brings great benefit. Thus, in the Travel hexagram, losing a servant is an omen of trouble, while gaining one is auspicious. How great is the Sage’s teaching on Travel!

### 175. [9/10] 第十二類 在船騙 Category 12 · On-the-Boat Swindles — 脚夫挑走起船货 (cat-12-s05) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 建城溪边，凡客船到岸，众脚夫丛集，求雇担代挑入城。有老成客，必喝退众夫，待船货齐收上岸，都数纪定，然后分作几担，叫几名脚夫，自相识认，乃发入城，急令人跟行其后，方保无失。若雏家到，众脚夫不管物件检齐否，即为收拾上担，及急跟夫去，多致遗物在船中未尽收。
- **Current English**:
  > By the creek at Jianning city, whenever a merchant boat reaches shore, porters swarm and beg to be hired to carry loads into the city. An experienced traveler will always shout the crowd of porters back, wait until the boat’s goods are all brought ashore and counted and recorded, then divide them into several loads, call several porters he can recognize, send them into the city, and at once have someone follow behind; only then is there no loss. If a greenhorn arrives, the porters, without waiting to see whether the things have been fully checked, pack them onto the poles at once; and if you hurry after the porters, you often leave things still on the boat not yet brought in.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'pack them onto the poles' is slightly literal for '上担'; 'load them onto the carriers' or 'put them on the shoulder-poles' would be more natural in English.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > By the creek at Jianning city, whenever a merchant boat reaches shore, porters swarm and beg to be hired to carry loads into the city. An experienced traveler will always shout the crowd of porters back, wait until the boat's goods are all brought ashore and counted and recorded, then divide them into several loads, call several porters he can recognize, send them into the city, and at once have someone follow behind; only then is there no loss. If a greenhorn arrives, the porters, without waiting to see whether the things have been fully checked, load them onto the carriers at once; and if you hurry after the porters, you often leave things still on the boat not yet brought in.

### 176. [9/10] 第十二類 在船騙 Category 12 · On-the-Boat Swindles — 脚夫挑走起船货 (cat-12-s05) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 有侯官县一田秀才，出外作馆。年冬归，得束金四十餘两，衣被物件亦十餘两，共作两大笼。经过建城，欲入拜乡亲，命一脚夫挑笼先行。田乃儒家，从后缓步随之。脚夫见其来迟，一步紧一步，趱入城门；人闹攘处，更是疾行，遂挑入曲巷逃走。田从后虽叫“止步”，哪能止得！入城曲巷多歧，何处可寻？次日，往府吕巡捕呈之。
- **Current English**:
  > There was a licentiate named Tian of Houguan County who had gone out to tutor. At year’s end he was heading home; he had more than forty taels of teaching silver, and clothes and bedding worth another ten-odd taels, packed as two large baskets. Passing Jianning, he wanted to go in and call on a fellow townsman, and ordered a porter to carry the baskets ahead. Tian was a Confucian scholar, and followed slowly behind. The porter, seeing him come late, walked faster and faster, hurried in at the city gate; in the crowded press he went still quicker, then carried the load into a winding alley and fled. Though Tian called from behind, “Stop!” how could he stop him? Inside the city the winding alleys forked many ways—where could one search? The next day he presented the matter to Prefectural Constable Lü.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The translation renders '建城' as 'Jianning'. While likely the intended place name (Jiancheng County), the source text uses the generic term 'Jian City' or a specific local name that might be better transliterated as 'Jiancheng' rather than anglicized to 'Jianning' without context, though this is a minor geographical ambiguity.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > There was a licentiate named Tian from Houguan County who went out to tutor. At year's end he returned home with more than forty taels of teaching silver and another ten-odd taels worth of clothes and bedding, packed into two large baskets. Passing through Jiancheng, he wished to enter and visit a fellow townsman, so he ordered a porter to carry the baskets ahead. As a Confucian scholar, Tian followed slowly behind. The porter, seeing him lagging, walked faster and faster, hurried into the city gate; in the crowded throng, he moved even quicker, carrying the load into a winding alley and fled. Though Tian called from behind, "Stop!" how could he stop him? Inside the city, the winding alleys forked many ways—where could one search? The next day, he presented the matter to Prefectural Constable Lü.

### 177. [9/10] 第十二類 在船騙 Category 12 · On-the-Boat Swindles — 脚夫挑走起船货 (cat-12-s05) `[body]`

  > 吕捕衙是精明官，以脚扶拐物，须用脚夫查之。即叫二差人来：“你认定这田相公，今午后穿白长衫在船中。行李到，必有脚夫挑走，你二人从后密跟，到他家拿来。”再对秀才曰：“你今再讨假行李一担，在十里外搭船来府，照前日到岸时，叫脚夫来挑。你穿白长衫去，此两差人易认。若已在旁，你故意缓行，任此脚夫挑去，必能拿得前脚夫来。”秀才未会其意。
- **Current English**:
  > Constable Lü was a sharp official. To catch a porter who had made off with goods, you must use porters to find him. He at once called two runners: “You mark this Licentiate Tian. This afternoon he will wear a white long gown on a boat. When luggage arrives, a porter will surely run off with it; you two follow secretly behind and seize him at his house.” He then said to the licentiate: “Today get a dummy load of luggage, board a boat ten li out, and come to the prefecture. When you reach shore as you did the other day, call a porter to carry it. Wear a white long gown so these two runners can know you. If they are already beside you, walk slowly on purpose and let this porter carry it off; they will surely catch the earlier porter.” The licentiate did not yet see his meaning.

- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Constable Lü was a sharp official. To catch a porter who had cheated out of goods, you must use porters to find him. He at once called two runners: "You mark this Licentiate Tian. This afternoon he will wear a white long gown on a boat. When luggage arrives, a porter will surely make off with it; you two follow secretly behind and seize him at his house." He then said to the licentiate: "Today get a dummy load of luggage, board a boat ten li out, and come to the prefecture. When you reach shore as you did the other day, call a porter to carry it. Wear a white long gown so these two runners can recognize you. If they are already beside you, walk slowly on purpose and let this porter carry it off; they will surely catch the earlier porter." The licentiate did not yet see his meaning.

### 178. [9/10] 第十二類 在船騙 Category 12 · On-the-Boat Swindles — 脚夫挑走起船货 (cat-12-s05) `[note]`

- **Current English**:
  > Note: Porters running off with goods happen everywhere, so when you travel you should most carefully guard against it. As for Constable Lü’s exposing the fraud and catching the thief with no effort spared, the whole thing lay in that one move of using a porter to find a porter. This is what is meant by “using barbarians to fight barbarians.” The proverb also says, “Set a thief to catch a thief; use a needle to pick out a splinter”—the same idea. Those who hire porters may take it as a warning; those who catch thieves may take it as a method.

As for Constable Lü, who uncovered the deceit and captured the thief without sparing any effort, the whole matter hinged on one move: using a porter to catch a porter. This is what is meant by 'using barbarians to fight barbarians.' The proverb also says, 'A thief catches a thief; use a needle to pick out a splinter'—the same idea. Those who hire porters should take this as a warning; those who hunt thieves may take it as a model.

### 179. [9/10] 第十三類 詩詞騙 Category 13 · Swindles by Verse — 伪装道士骗盐使 (cat-13-s00) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 唐寅，字伯虎，又字子畏，南京吴趋里人也。中弘治戊午南京解元，因事被黜之。后遂放浪不羁，流留花酒。善诗文，画极工。与文征明、文征仲、祝希哲等为友，皆极一时之名流也。日游平康妓家，滑稽为乐，随口成文。有一皂隶，携纸一张求画。伯虎援笔，画螺蛳十餘个，题诗于上云：“不是蝤蛑不是蛏，海味之中少此名。千呼万呼呼不出，只待人来打窟臀。”众皆大笑。偶一日出，见县前枷一和尚。众人请曰：“可将此和尚作诗一首。”伯虎询知和尚被枷缘由，援笔题于枷上曰：“皂隶官差去采茶，不要纹银只要赊。县里捉来三十板，方盘托出大西瓜。”知县送客出来见之，问是何人所作，或以伯虎对，即将和尚释之。其捷于口才，大约类此。
- **Current English**:
  > Tang Yin, courtesy name Bohu, also Ziwei, was a man of Wuqu Lane in Nanjing. He placed first in the Nanjing provincial examination in the wuwu year of the Hongzhi reign (1498), then lost his degree over a scandal. After that he lived unbridled, lingering among flowers and wine. He was skilled at poetry and prose, and his painting was especially fine. He was friends with Wen Zhengming, Wen Zhengzhong, Zhu Xizhe, and others—all famous men of that generation. He spent his days in the pleasure houses, amusing himself with jokes and composing off the cuff. A yamen runner once brought a sheet of paper and asked for a painting. Bohu took up the brush, painted a dozen or so snails, and wrote on it: “Not a swimming crab, not a razor clam; among seafood this one barely has a name. Call a thousand times, ten thousand times, it will not come out; it only waits for someone to smack its rump.” Everyone laughed hard. One day he went out and saw a monk in a cangue in front of the county yamen. People asked him to make a poem on the monk. Bohu asked why the monk had been cangued, then wrote on the cangue: “A runner, sent on official business, went to pick tea; he wanted no fine silver, only credit. The county seized him for thirty strokes; on a square tray they served up a big watermelon.” The magistrate, seeing guests out, noticed it, asked who had written it, was told it was Bohu, and promptly released the monk. His ready tongue was generally of this kind.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'lingering among flowers and wine' is slightly passive; the original '放浪不羁，流留花酒' implies a more active, dissolute lifestyle of wandering and indulging in pleasure.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Tang Yin, courtesy name Bohu, also Ziwei, was from Wuqu Lane in Nanjing. He topped the Nanjing provincial examination in the wuwu year of the Hongzhi reign (1498) but lost his degree due to a scandal. Thereafter he lived unbridled, drifting through life in revelry and wine. Skilled in poetry and prose, his painting was especially fine. He befriended Wen Zhengming, Wen Zhengzhong, Zhu Xizhe, and others—all the most famous figures of their day. He spent his days visiting pleasure houses, amusing himself with jokes and composing off the cuff. A yamen runner once brought a sheet of paper asking for a painting. Bohu took up the brush, painted a dozen or so snails, and wrote on it: "Not a swimming crab, not a razor clam; among seafood this one barely has a name. Call a thousand times, ten thousand times, it will not come out; it only waits for someone to smack its rump." Everyone laughed hard. One day he went out and saw a monk in a cangue in front of the county yamen. People asked him to compose a poem about the monk. Bohu learned why the monk had been cangued, then wrote on the cangue: "A runner, sent on official business, went to pick tea; he wanted no fine silver, only credit. The county seized him for thirty strokes; on a square tray they served up a big watermelon." The magistrate, seeing guests out, noticed it, asked who had written it, was told it was Bohu, and promptly released the monk. His ready tongue was generally of this kind.

### 180. [9/10] 第十三類 詩詞騙 Category 13 · Swindles by Verse — 伪装道士骗盐使 (cat-13-s00) `[body]`

  > 一日，与祝希哲等十数辈，携装游维扬，日与妓者饮酒，声色为乐。将及一月，资用殆尽。希哲曰：“黄金用尽，作何计策乎？”伯虎曰：“无妨！当今盐使者，资财巨万。我和你二人，可假扮女贞观道士以化之。”二人即扮道士。值盐使者升堂，二人俯伏阶下，云：“女贞观道士参见。”盐使者大怒曰：“岂不闻御史台风霜凛凛耶？是何道者，敢此无状！”将挞之。二人徐对曰：“明公以小道为游方觅食者耶？小道遍游天下，所交者皆极海内名流。即如吴邑唐伯虎、文征明、祝希哲辈，无不与小道折节为友。凡诗词歌赋，应口辄成。明公如不信，愿奏薄技，惟明公所命。”盐使者乃指堂下石牛为题，命二人联诗一首。伯虎应声即吟云：“嵯峨怪石倚云边，”哲云：“抛掷于今定几年。”虎云：“台藓作毛因雨长，”哲云：“藤萝穿鼻任风牵。”虎云：“从来不食溪边草，”哲云：“自古难耕陇上田。”虎云：“怪杀牧童鞭不起，”哲云：“笛声斜挂夕阳烟。”
- **Current English**:
  > One day, with Zhu Xizhe and a dozen others, he packed up and traveled to Yangzhou. Day after day they drank with courtesans and took their pleasure in music and beauty. After nearly a month their money was almost gone. Xizhe said, “The gold is spent—what’s the plan?” Bohu said, “No matter. The salt inspector these days is worth tens of thousands. When the salt inspector held court they prostrated themselves at the foot of the steps and said, “Daoist priests of Nüzhen Abbey present themselves.” The salt inspector was furious: “Have you not heard that a censor’s court is severe as frost? What kind of Daoists dare this insolence!” He was about to have them beaten. The two answered calmly: “Does Your Honor take us for wandering priests out begging meals? We have traveled the whole empire; those we keep company with are all the most famous men in the land. Even Tang Bohu, Wen Zhengming, and Zhu Xizhe of Wu county set aside their rank to befriend us. Poetry, lyrics, songs, rhapsodies—we finish them the moment we open our mouths. If Your Honor does not believe us, we offer a modest sample; command us as you please.” The salt inspector pointed to a stone ox below the hall as the topic and ordered them to compose a linked verse. Bohu answered at once: “A towering strange rock leans against the clouds.” Zhe: “Thrown down—who can tell how many years by now.” Hu: “Moss on the terrace makes its hair, grown by the rain.” Zhe: “Vines thread its nose, left for the wind to tug.” Hu: “It has never eaten grass beside the stream.” Zhe: “From of old it could never plow the ridge fields.” Hu: “No wonder the herdboy’s whip cannot raise it.” Zhe: “The sound of his flute hangs aslant in the sunset haze.”

The salt inspector was furious: "Have you not heard that a censor's court is severe as frost? What kind of Daoists dare such insolence!" He was about to have them beaten. The two answered calmly: "Does Your Honor take us for wandering beggar-priests seeking alms? We have traveled the whole empire; those we keep company with are all the most famous men in the land. Even Tang Bohu, Wen Zhengming, and Zhu Xizhe of Wu county set aside their rank to befriend us. Poetry, lyrics, songs, rhapsodies—we finish them the moment we open our mouths. If Your Honor does not believe us, we offer a modest sample; command us as you please." The salt inspector pointed to a stone ox below the hall as the topic and ordered them to compose a linked verse. Bohu answered at once: "A towering strange rock leans against the clouds." Zhe: "Thrown down—who can tell how many years by now." Hu: "Moss on the terrace makes its hair, grown by the rain." Zhe: "Vines thread its nose, left for the wind to tug." Hu: "It has never eaten grass beside the stream." Zhe: "From of old it could never plow the ridge fields." Hu: "No wonder the herdboy's whip cannot raise it." Zhe: "The sound of his flute hangs aslant in the sunset haze."

### 181. [9/10] 第十三類 詩詞騙 Category 13 · Swindles by Verse — 伪装道士骗盐使 (cat-13-s00) `[body]`

- **Chinese Source**:
  > 盐使者览毕，霁色问曰：“诗则佳矣。将欲何为？”二人曰：“顷者女贞观圮坏。闻明公宽仁好施，愿捐俸金修葺，以成胜事，亦且不朽。”盐使者大悦，即檄吴兴二县，可给库银五百与之。二人见盐使者应允，速夜赴吴兴，假为道士说关节行状，对吴兴二县云：“今有盐使者修葺女贞观，此系盛举，可依数与之，不可宽缓。”吴兴二县果如数与之。二人得银大悦，曰：“不将万丈深潭计，安得骊龙项下珠？”复往维扬，聚交游十数辈，于妓者家欢呼剧饮，纵其所乐。不十数日，五百之金费用殆尽。后盐使者按临吴兴，束衣冠往女贞观，则见其倾圯如故。召吴兴二县责之，二县对曰：“日前唐伯虎与祝希哲从维扬来，极称明公兴此盛举，小知县即依数与之矣。”盐使者怅然，知为二人所骗，但惜其才，故亦不究。
- **Current English**:
  > The salt inspector finished reading, his expression cleared, and he asked, “The poem is fine. What do you want?” The two said, “Nüzhen Abbey has lately fallen into ruin. We hear Your Honor is generous and fond of giving. If you would give from your salary to repair it, it would complete a worthy deed, and the merit would not fade.” The salt inspector was delighted and issued a dispatch to the two counties of Wuxing: they were to issue five hundred taels from the treasury to them. Seeing that he had agreed, the two hurried that very night to Wuxing, and, still posing as Daoists, they cited their official backing and told the two counties: “The salt inspector is repairing Nüzhen Abbey. This is a grand undertaking. Give the sum as ordered; do not delay.” The two counties paid it in full. The two men, delighted with the silver, said, “If we had not risked the ten-thousand-fathom pool, how would we have got the pearl under the black dragon’s chin?” They went back to Yangzhou, gathered their dozen companions, shouted and drank in the courtesans’ houses, and did as they pleased. In fewer than ten days the five hundred taels were gone. Later the salt inspector came through Wuxing on tour, put on his robes and cap, and went to Nüzhen Abbey—only to find it still in ruins as before. He summoned the two magistrates and rebuked them. They answered, “The other day Tang Bohu and Zhu Xizhe came from Yangzhou and praised Your Honor for raising this grand work; we magistrates paid the sum as ordered.” The salt inspector was dismayed. He knew he had been taken in by the two men, but he valued their talent, so he did not pursue it.

### 182. [9/10] 第十三類 詩詞騙 Category 13 · Swindles by Verse — 伪装道士骗盐使 (cat-13-s00) `[note]`

  > 按：唐伯虎、祝希哲，皆海内一时名家也。但以不得志于时，遂纵于声色，青楼酒肆中，无不闻其名。然非口若悬河，才高倚马，何以能倾动使院？此之骗，可谓骗之善矣。独计当今冠进贤而坐虎皮者，咸思削民脂以润私橐，敛众怨以肥身家，其所以骗民者何如！乃一旦反为唐、祝所骗，亦可为贪墨者一儆。但其知而不究，亦可谓有怜才之心者矣。
- **Current English**:
  > Note: Tang Bohu and Zhu Xizhe were both famous masters of their generation throughout the land. Blocked in their careers, they gave themselves over to music and beauty; there was no brothel or tavern that had not heard their names. Yet without tongues as fluent as a river in flood, and talent ready to compose at a moment’s notice, how could they have shaken the inspector’s yamen? This swindle may be called a swindle well done. Think of the men who wear official caps and sit on tiger skins today: they all think of scraping the people’s fat to grease their private purses, gathering the crowd’s resentment to fatten their own households—and how they cheat the people! Then one day they themselves are taken in by Tang and Zhu, which may serve as a warning to the greedy. That the inspector knew and still did not pursue it may also be called a heart that pities talent.

- **Recommended Revision**:

Blocked in their careers, they gave themselves over to music and beauty; there was no brothel or tavern that had not heard their names. ### 183. [9/10] 第十三類 詩詞騙 Category 13 · Swindles by Verse — 陈全遗计嫖名妓 (cat-13-s01) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 金陵陈全者，百万巨富也。其为人风流潇洒，尤善滑稽。凡见一物，能速成口号。尝与本地院妓往来，唯一妓最得意。夏间瓜初出，院妓将瓜皮二片，放于门限内，诈令一人慌忙叫全云：“某姐姐偶得危病，要你一相见方瞑目。”全即乘马速至，慌忙进门，脚踹瓜皮跌倒。众妓鼓掌大笑云：“陈官人快做一口号，不得迟！”全即答曰：“陈全走得忙，院子安排定。只因两块皮，几丧我的命。”众妓欣然，遂会饮而罢。
- **Current English**:
  > Chen Quan of Jinling was a man of enormous wealth. He was dashing and easy in his ways, and especially good at jokes. Whatever he saw, he could turn into an improvised verse at once. He used to keep company with the local courtesans; one of them was his particular favorite. In summer, when the first melons came in, the house put two pieces of melon rind just inside the threshold and had someone run to Quan in a panic: “Sister So-and-so has suddenly taken a mortal illness; she wants to see you once before she can close her eyes.” Quan mounted at once and rode hard. He rushed in, stepped on the rind, and fell. The courtesans clapped and laughed: “Master Chen, make a verse at once—no delay!” Quan answered: “Chen Quan came in a rush; the house had it all arranged. Just because of two pieces of rind, I nearly lost my life.” The women were delighted; they drank together and left it at that.
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The phrase 'house' in 'the house had it all arranged' is slightly ambiguous; 'the brothel' or 'the establishment' would be more precise for the Ming context of a courtesan house (院).
  - 'Sister So-and-so' is a functional translation for '某姐姐', but adding 'a certain sister' or keeping 'a certain sister' might flow slightly better as a narrative device.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > Chen Quan of Jinling was a man of enormous wealth. He was dashing and easy in his ways, and especially good at jokes. Whatever he saw, he could turn into an improvised verse at once. He used to keep company with the local courtesans; one of them was his particular favorite. In summer, when the first melons came in, the brothel placed two pieces of melon rind just inside the threshold and had someone run to Quan in a panic: "A certain sister has suddenly taken a mortal illness; she wants to see you once before she can close her eyes." Quan mounted at once and rode hard. He rushed in, stepped on the rind, and fell. The courtesans clapped and laughed: "Master Chen, make a verse at once—no delay!" Quan answered: "Chen Quan came in a rush; the brothel had it all arranged. Just because of two pieces of rind, I nearly lost my life." The women were delighted; they drank together and left it at that.

### 184. [9/10] 第十三類 詩詞騙 Category 13 · Swindles by Verse — 陈全遗计嫖名妓 (cat-13-s01) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
- **Chinese Source**:
  > 彼时，浙江杭州有一名妓，号花不如。姿态甚佳，且琴棋诗画，无不通晓。但身价颇高，不与庸俗往来，唯与豪俊交接。每宿一夜，费银六七两方得。全闻之，欲嫖此妓，因而骗之。故令十餘家丁，陆续运船到杭，彼与二三家丁先往。到花不如家，即令家人扛抬皮箱一个———下面俱系纸包砖石，上面一重，俱是纸包真银，每十两为一封———入花不如卧房内，当面开箱取银，赏赐妓家诸役甚厚，奉不如白金十两。不如与众役俱大欢喜，以为此大财主也，所得必不资矣。不如问曰：“客官贵处？”全曰：“金陵。”又问曰：“高姓？”答曰：“姓浪。”又问曰：“尊号？”答曰：“子遂。”不如整盛席相款。子遂不去，只在彼家。过两日，又一家人来报云：“某号船已到。”子遂云：“餘货只放船内，但扛抬皮箱进姐姐家来。”如是者三四次，皮箱有五六个在不如卧房内矣。子遂见不如带珠，云：“你这珠俱不好。我有大珠数百颗，个个俱圆，候此号船到，我去取与你。”
- **Current English**:
  > At that time Hangzhou in Zhejiang had a famous courtesan called Hua Buru. She was a great beauty, and she was versed in the qin, chess, poetry, and painting. But her price was high. She did not mix with ordinary men, only with the rich and distinguished. A night with her cost six or seven taels. Quan heard of her and wanted to sleep with her, so he set out to cheat her. He had a dozen or more household men send boats on to Hangzhou one after another, while he and two or three servants went first. At Hua Buru’s house he had a leather chest carried into her bedroom—the bottom all paper packets of brick and stone, only the top layer paper packets of real silver, ten taels to a packet. In front of everyone he opened the chest, took out silver, tipped the house servants very generously, and presented Buru with ten taels of silver. Buru and the servants were all delighted, thinking this was a great rich man and that what they would get would be beyond counting. Buru asked, “Where is the gentleman from?” Quan said, “Jinling.” “Your surname?” “Lang.” “Your style name?” “Zisui.” Buru laid out a rich feast. Zisui did not leave; he stayed in her house. After two days another servant came to report: “Boat number such-and-such has arrived.” Zisui said, “Leave the rest of the goods on the boat; just carry the leather chests into Sister’s house.” This happened three or four times, until five or six chests were in Buru’s bedroom. Zisui saw Buru wearing pearls and said, “These pearls of yours are all poor stuff. I have several hundred large pearls, every one of them round. When this next boat arrives I will fetch them for you.”
- **Identified Issues**:
  - The translation of '浪' as 'Lang' is technically accurate for the surname but misses the wry pun intended in the original (a play on 'wave' or 'unstable'), which could be noted in a footnote rather than translated directly.
- **Recommended Revision**:
  > At that time, Hangzhou in Zhejiang had a famous courtesan named Hua Buru. She was a great beauty and versed in the qin, chess, poetry, and painting. Her price was high; she did not associate with ordinary men, only with the wealthy and distinguished. A night with her cost six or seven taels. Quan heard of her and wanted to sleep with her, so he set out to cheat her. He arranged for more than a dozen household servants to send boats to Hangzhou one after another, while he and two or three servants went first. At Hua Buru's house, he ordered a leather chest carried into her bedroom—the bottom layers were paper packets filled with bricks and stones, while the top layer contained paper packets of real silver, ten taels per packet. In front of everyone, he opened the chest, took out silver, tipped the household servants very generously, and presented Buru with ten taels of silver. Buru and the servants were all delighted, thinking this was a great rich man and that what they would get would be beyond counting. Buru asked, "Where is the gentleman from?" Quan said, "Jinling." "Your surname?" "Lang." "Your style name?" "Zisui." Buru laid out a rich feast. Zisui did not leave; he stayed at her house. After two days, another servant came to report: "Boat number such-and-such has arrived." Zisui said, "Leave the rest of the goods on the boat; just carry the leather chests into Sister's house." This happened three or four times until five or six chests were in Buru's bedroom. Zisui saw Buru wearing pearls and said, "These pearls of yours are all poor stuff. I have several hundred large pearls, every one of them round. When this next boat arrives, I will fetch them for you."

The several leather chests are in your bedroom; you must watch them. I won't be back until afternoon. Just send one of your men and a mount with me." Buru sent a man and a donkey to accompany Zisui.

My mother’s name, and her looks?” The con man answered without a mistake. The con man said to his “brother-in-law,” “I need some loose silver for spending. Let me take fifteen or sixteen taels from your house for my sundry expenses." The farmer then took out more than ten taels of his own genuine silver and gave it to the con man.

### 188. [9/10] 第十四類 假銀騙 Category 14 · Counterfeit-Silver Swindles — 设假元宝骗乡农 (cat-14-s00) `[body]`

  > 按：此乡农心苦力勤，啬用薄奉以致富，幸矣！何乃为贪心所使，落贼牢笼，以致失财被辱，反不如江源之妇之智哉！然末世滋伪，奸宄百出，近有丢包贼骗人甚多。更江淮间，又有扯遂法，尤难防检。贼止问你一句，你若答应一句，即被他迷。此妖术也，害人尤多。世道人心，一变至此极乎！偶因前事，遂备述之，以为出途者警。
- **Current English**:
  > Note: This farmer worked himself hard, scrimped on food and clothing, and so grew rich—a fortunate man. Why did he let greed drive him into the crooks’ cage, to lose his money and be shamed, ending up less wise than the woman of Jiangyuan? But in these latter days deceit flourishes and villains appear in a hundred shapes. Lately “dropped-bundle” thieves have cheated a great many people, and in the country between the Yangzi and the Huai there is another trick, called “the pull,” that is even harder to guard against: the thief only has to ask you one question—answer him once and you are spellbound. It is sorcery, and it has harmed even more people. Have the ways of the world and the hearts of men really sunk to this? Coming across these stories, I have set them down in full as a warning to all who travel.
- **Identified Issues**:

Why did he let greed drive him into the crooks’ cage, to lose his money and be shamed, ending up less wise than the woman of Jiangyuan? But in these latter days deceit flourishes and villains appear in a hundred shapes. Lately “dropped-bundle” thieves have cheated a great many people, and in the country between the Yangzi and the Huai there is another trick, called “the tug-and-drop,” that is even harder to guard against: the thief only has to ask you one question—answer him once and you are spellbound. It is sorcery, and it has harmed even more people. Have the ways of the world and the hearts of men really sunk to this? Coming across these stories, I have set them down in full as a warning to all who travel.

### 190. [9/10] 第十四類 假銀騙 Category 14 · Counterfeit-Silver Swindles — 冒州接着漂白皛 (cat-14-s01) `[body]`
- **Score**: **9/10** (Accuracy: **10/10**, Fluency: **9/10**)
